The Case Against Smooth Null Infinity V: Early-Time Asymptotics of Linearised Gravity Around Schwarzschild for Fixed Spherical Harmonic Modes
Leonhard Kehrberger, Hamed Masaood

Abstract
In this work, starting from the predictions of the Post-Newtonian theory for a system of N N infalling masses from the infinite past i − i^{-} , we formulate and solve a scattering problem for the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild in a double null gauge as introduced in [ DHR19a ] . The scattering data are posed on a null hypersurface 𝒞 ¯ \underline{\mathcal{C}} emanating from a section of past null infinity ℐ − \mathcal{I}^{-} , and on the part of ℐ − \mathcal{I}^{-} that lies to the future of this section: Along 𝒞 ¯ \underline{\mathcal{C}} , we implement the Post-Newtonian theory-inspired hypothesis that the gauge-invariant components of the Weyl tensor α (1) \accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}} and α ¯ (1) \accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}} (a.k.a. Ψ 0 \Psi_{0} and Ψ 4 \Psi_{4} ) decay like r − 3 r^{-3} , r − 4 r^{-4} , respectively, and we exclude incoming radiation from ℐ − \mathcal{I}^{-} by demanding the News function to vanish along ℐ − \mathcal{I}^{-} . We also show that compactly supported gravitational perturbations along ℐ − \mathcal{I}^{-} induce very similar data, with α (1) \accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}} , α ¯ (1) \accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}} decaying like r − 3 r^{-3} , r − 5 r^{-5} . After constructing the unique solution to this scattering problem, we then provide a complete analysis of the asymptotic behaviour of projections onto fixed spherical harmonic number ℓ \ell near ℐ − \mathcal{I}^{-} , spacelike infinity i 0 i^{0} and future null infinity ℐ + \mathcal{I}^{+} , crucially exploiting a set of approximate conservation laws enjoyed by α (1) \accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}} and α ¯ (1) \accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}} . Having obtained a clear understanding of the asymptotics of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, we also give constructive corrections to popular historical notions of asymptotic flatness such as Bondi coordinates or asymptotic simplicity. In particular, confirming earlier heuristics due to Damour and Christodoulou, we find that the peeling property is violated both near ℐ − \mathcal{I}^{-} and near ℐ + \mathcal{I}^{+} , with e.g. α (1) \accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}} near ℐ + \mathcal{I}^{+} only decaying like r − 4 r^{-4} instead of r − 5 r^{-5} . We also find that the resulting solution decays slower towards i 0 i^{0} than often assumed, with α (1) , α ¯ (1) \accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}} both decaying like r − 3 r^{-3} towards i 0 i^{0} . The issue of summing up the estimates obtained for fixed angular modes in ℓ \ell in order to obtain asymptotics for the full solution is dealt with in forthcoming work.



1
Introduction

This paper is the fifth in a series of papers (initiated with
[
Keh21
,
Keh22a
,
Keh22b
]
) dedicated to the rigorous analysis of the asymptotic properties of gravitational radiation in astrophysical contexts.
More specifically, we study the precise
early-time asymptotics
of
scattering solutions

[
DRS18
,
Mas22a
,
Mas22b
]
to the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild (as introduced in
[
DHR19a
]
), with an emphasis on solutions with particular physical relevance in the context of
N
-body scattering constructions
[
WW79
,
Dam86
,
Chr02
]
.

Here, the expression
early-time asymptotics
is used to denote the asymptotics up until some finite retarded time. Thus, our results complement and directly affect the impressive body of literature on asymptotics of solutions to wave equations on black hole geometries at
late times
, see e.g.
[
DR05
,
LO19
,
AAG21
,
AAG23
,
Hin22
,
MZ22a
]
and references therein, as well as
[
GK22
]
for the relation between early- and late-time asymptotics.

Describing the asymptotic properties of gravitational radiation, our results also affect popular notions of asymptotic flatness such as that of a smooth null infinity
[
Pen65
]
or Bondi coordinates
[
Sac62b
]
. More details will be given below.

Structure of the introduction and relation to the overview paper
[
Keh24
]

This paper is also the longer companion paper to the much shorter overview paper
[
Keh24
]
.
The latter provides a thorough introduction to and discussion of the history of the problem studied in the series
The Case Against Smooth Null Infinity
, as well as a sketch of the derivation of some of the main results of the present paper, and we recommend reading it before reading the present paper—indeed, it is a good starting point not only as an introduction to the present paper, but also to the series.
Nevertheless, we will still give a completely self-contained introduction here, using, however, the existence of the overview paper to allow ourselves to be brief at some places while going into more detail at other places. In general, the present paper is written in an entirely self-contained manner.

The reader who has read the overview paper may feel free to skip directly to §
1.3
, as the contents of §
1.1
and §
1.2
, which contain motivation and historical background for the problem studied as well as a basic summary of the setup and the notation employed in the paper, are discussed in more detail in
[
Keh24
]
.

Sections §
1.3
and §
1.4
then give a thorough overview of the main results of the paper, namely a complete asymptotic analysis near spacelike infinity of physically well-motivated solutions to the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild.
In §
1.5
, we offer some reflection on how our results affect the various historical
notions of asymptotic flatness discussed in §
1.1
.
In §
1.6
, we state a conjecture on the
global
asymptotics (including the late-time asymptotics near future timelike infinity) for solutions to the nonlinear Einstein vacuum equations.
Finally, in §
1.7
, we explain the structure of the remainder of the paper (which is closely aligned to that of
[
Keh24
]
).

1.1
Motivation and context–Asymptotic flatness, isolated systems and the
N
-body problem

Like we said before, this paper’s object of study is the
precise asymptotic analysis of solutions to the semi-global scattering problem near spacelike infinity
i^{0}
for linearised gravity around Schwarzschild
, with an emphasis on those solutions that arise from scattering data that have particular physical relevance in the context of the
N
-body problem.
More precisely, we consider the linearisation of the Einstein vacuum equations,

\mathrm{Ric}[\bm{g}]=0,

(1.1)

around the Schwarzschild solution

g_{M}=-\Omega^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\Omega^{-2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}),\quad\quad\Omega^{2}:=D:=1-\frac{2M}{r}

(1.2)

recast in a double null gauge (see already (
1.5
) or §
3
for details).
We formulate for the arising system a scattering problem with
seed scattering data
posed on an ingoing null cone
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
(truncated away from the event horizon) and on the part of past null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{-}
that lies to the future of this cone. We prove existence and uniqueness of solutions arising from the seed scattering data (cf. Thm.
1.1
).
We then analyse the semi-global
1
1

1

We use the word semi-global to denote the entire domain of dependence of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}
. Since the cone
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
is truncated away from the event horizon, this region excludes future timelike infinity
i^{+}
. See, however, §
1.6
for conjectures on the
global
asymptotic behaviour (including
i^{+}
).
asymptotic properties of these solutions (cf. Thms.
1.2
–
1.4
).
In doing so, we single out three particular classes of scattering data to which we prescribe specific physical relevance, namely that they each capture a system of
N
infalling objects from the infinite past (cf. Def.
1.4
).

Before diving into the details, let us motivate why we study this problem.
From a mathematical point of view, there is the desire to develop a deeper understanding of scattering properties and asymptotics near spacelike infinity of solutions to (systems of) wave equations such as (
1.1
).
This should be seen as complementing the ongoing research program concerning the
late-time asymptotics
of such systems in the context of black hole geometries
[
DR05
,
LO19
,
AAG18b
,
AAG18a
,
AAG21
,
AAG23
,
Gaj22
,
Hin22
,
MZ22b
,
MZ22a
]
; indeed, the two are intimately connected, see
[
GK22
]
and §
1.6
.

On the other hand, there is the desire to slowly approach the difficult question of developing even a basic rigorous understanding of the
N
-body problem in general relativity, cf. Open Problems 1 and 2 from the introduction of
[
Keh23
]
.

Now, it turns out that both of these directions of motivation can be naturally included within the more conceptual motivation related to the much broader debate on how to model isolated systems in general relativity, or how to choose or justify different notions of asymptotic flatness. Since much has been said on this in
[
Keh24
]
already, we shall here be quite brief:
The central difficulty of modelling isolated systems in relativity is, to a large extent, rooted in the problem that one inevitably has to model the gravitational radiation emitted by the corresponding system in the asymptotic regime.
Historically, there have been various approaches and proposals how to tackle this difficulty; here, we give a schematic list of some of them:

1)

Bondi coordinates/Bondi–Sachs formalism:
One notion for a system to be asymptotically flat and to potentially capture the physics of isolated systems is that such a system should admit
Bondi coordinates
near future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
; these are coordinates that ensure, on the one hand, that
\mathcal{I}^{+}
itself behaves like the Minkowskian
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, and, on the other hand, that
\mathcal{I}^{+}
is approached in inverse powers of a radial coordinate
r_{\mathrm{BS}}
along outgoing null geodesics.
The connection of this latter requirement, loosely referred to as
the peeling property
or
the outgoing radiation condition
, to physics consisted of it holding true for the linearised theory around Minkowski provided that there is
no incoming radiation from past null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.
A definition of the peeling property in our context is given in Def.
1.1
.
The groundwork for this notion was set in
[
Bon60
,
Sac61
,
BVdBM62
,
Sac62b
,
Sac62a
]
.

2)

Asymptotic simplicity/Smooth null infinity:
In a similar spirit, but from a geometrical point of view much more appealing, is Penrose’s notion of asymptotic simplicity
[
Pen63
,
Pen65
]
, defining a class of spacetimes essentially via the requirement that they should admit a smooth conformal compactification (with smooth boundaries
\mathcal{I}^{-}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
). This requirement, just like Bondi coordinates did, implies that gravitational radiation decays in inverse powers of a certain radial coordinate along outgoing null geodesics towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(and, in addition, along ingoing null geodesics towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
). Cf. Def.
1.1
.

3)

Asymptotic flatness at the Riemannian level/Initial data decay assumptions:
The two previous notions were global in spirit (although one can certainly make dynamical sense of them in the context of a characteristic initial value problem, see
[
Fri86
]
). Viewing GR as an evolutionary theory, with solutions arising from initial Cauchy data
(\Sigma,\bar{g},\bar{k})
, one can also introduce Riemannian notions of asymptotic flatness by demanding that
\bar{g}
,
\bar{k}
, respectively, measured with respect to a suitable coordinate chart, approach the Euclidean metric
\delta
, and 0, respectively, at a suitable rate near their flat ends.
This naturally induces a notion of asymptotic flatness on the arising solution.
More generally, in any initial value problem, (i.e. also characteristic IVPs), the corresponding assumptions on the asymptotic behaviour of the data can be viewed as a notion for asymptotic flatness.
See, for instance,
[
CK93
]
and Remark 1.1 of
[
Keh21
]
, or, in the case of the characteristic IVP, Holzegel’s notion of asymptotic extendability
[
Hol16
]
.

4)

Past-Stationarity:
A fourth notion how to model isolated systems is to simply say that there was no radiation in the infinite past because the system was stationary up until some finite time.

Each of these notions have in common that they introduce some
ad hoc
assumptions on the asymptotic behaviour of gravitational radiation. Historically, in the case of the first two, these assumptions were partially, but incorrectly, motivated by the
no incoming radiation condition
(we will see in this paper that, already for linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, there is no logical connection between no incoming radiation and
1/r
-expansions towards null infinity).

In the case of the third notion, the connection to physics is less clear. One could certainly entertain sufficiently weak assumptions on the decay rate of the data towards spatial infinity so as to
not exclude
any physics. In the context of proving the stability of the Minkowski spacetime, research in this direction has been undertaken in
[
BZ09
]
and, more recently, in
[
She23
]
. In fact, we will see later on that, with the exception of
[
IP22
]
, all other Minkowski stability proofs starting from Cauchy data (see
[
CK93
,
LR10
,
Hin23
]
and references therein) consider initial data decay that is too fast for the spacetimes constructed in the present work to be admitted.
At the same time, working with notions of asymptotic flatness as general as in
[
BZ09
]
will likely wash away a lot of the physically interesting information.
Indeed, we will see that a more appropriate approach would be to impose a certain decay rate
together with extra structure
that captures the no incoming radiation condition. See already §
1.5.3
.

The fourth notion, finally, is by far the simplest, and the prohibition of radiation near spacelike infinity has the clear physical interpretation of an approximation. But when saying that radiation only started at some finite retarded time
u_{-\infty}
, one should really be keeping track of the parameter
u_{-\infty}
in order to understand the error made by assuming the past to be stationary; this, however, is tantamount to allowing for radiation at arbitrarily early times. In other words, justifying past-stationarity requires some quantitative understanding of radiation in the infinite past, and, indeed, as was shown in
[
GK22
]
, the effects of radiation in the infinite past will dominate everything else at sufficiently late times, cf. §1(b) of
[
Keh24
]
.

Now, clearly, it would be desirable to not have to make
ad hoc
assumptions on the spacetime or the initial data that one is studying.
The right framework to meet this desire is given by the
scattering framework
, where, here, we mean by scattering simply that the (scattering) data are posed in the infinite past, with a scattering solution being defined as a solution that attains these (scattering) data in the limit.
For linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, a global scattering theory has been developed by the second author in
[
Mas22a
,
Mas22b
]
, following earlier works on scattering for the Einstein vacuum equations
[
Chr09
,
DHR13
]
and for the linear wave equation
[
AAG20
,
DRS18
,
Nic16
,
Bac97
,
DK87
,
Fri80
,
Vis70
]
etc.

The approach that the present paper is based on, and which is motivated by the works
[
WW79
,
Dam86
,
Chr02
]
and described in detail in
[
Keh24
]
, is the following:
Wanting to ultimately understand the asymptotic properties (near
\mathcal{I}^{-}
,
i^{0}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
) of a system of
N
infalling masses from past timelike infinity
i^{-}
, we resort to Post-Newtonian methods to understand the radiation emitted by such a system in the infinite past up until some finite advanced time
v_{1}
, i.e. up until some null cone
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}=\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{1}}
, cf. Fig.
1
. The idea is that beyond this advanced time, the spacetime is purely vacuum.
On the null cone
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, we then implement the information from the previous Post-Newtonian analysis as initial data for the linearised Einstein vacuum equations around Schwarzschild, and complete these data with scattering data on
\mathcal{I}^{-}
that capture the no incoming radiation condition. Cf. Fig.
2
.

Figure 1
:
Depiction of infalling masses from the infinite past. We use heuristic arguments to understand the gravitational radiation emitted by these masses up until some null cone
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

While our scattering data choice is thus motivated from
heuristic
analyses of infalling
masses
, we can also provide a
rigorous
basis for this choice by, instead of considering infalling masses, considering compactly supported gravitational perturbations along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, see already Def.
1.4
and Fig.
3
. As we will see, the behaviour of the corresponding solutions near
i^{0}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
will be almost indistinguishable from the case of
N
infalling
masses
, see already §
1.4.2
.

In this way, we have set up a mathematical scattering problem that we can solve and asymptotically analyse. In particular, we can now gain a complete understanding of the asymptotic behaviour of gravitational radiation in a neighbourhood of spacelike infinity (including the null infinities) in the context of physically justified data.
The results of the corresponding analysis will, in particular, show that the notions of asymptotic flatness in the list above are inadequate, and also give constructive corrections to all these notions, see §
1.5
.

Our setup is motivated by the heuristic works of Walker–Will
[
WW79
]
, Damour
[
Dam86
]
and Christodoulou
[
Chr02
]
. In the first, the Post-Newtonian analysis referred to above is carried out (for the lowest angular modes, the generalisation to higher modes is given in section 2 of
[
Keh24
]
) to obtain decay rates near
\mathcal{I}^{-}
inconsistent with peeling, while the latter two also put forth heuristic arguments against the failure of peeling towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, their rates towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
being in agreement with Thm.
1.2
of the present work. See, however, §
1.5.4
, where we discuss some accounts of Christodoulou’s argument in more detail.

1.2
Preliminary description of the setup and the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild

Notation:

We now collect a small subset of the notation employed in this paper so that we’ll be able to state the main results more clearly.
We will mostly be working with standard Eddington–Finkelstein double null coordinates
(u,v)
(cf. (
2.3
)) on a submanifold of the Schwarzschild manifold
\mathcal{M}_{M}
close to spacelike infinity (i.e. away from the event horizons):

\mathcal{D}:=\{(u,v,\theta,\varphi)|\,u\leq u_{0}<0,v\geq v_{1}>0\}=D^{+}({\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}).

(1.3)

This region is the domain of dependence of the union of the ingoing null cone
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}=\{u\leq u_{0},\,v=v_{1}\}
and the part of
\mathcal{I}^{-}
with
v\geq v_{1}
, see Figure
2
.
The set
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
is where we will pose our scattering data. We denote by
{\mathcal{S}_{1}},\,{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
the future and past end sphere of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, respectively, and we further define

r_{0}(u):=r|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}(u)=|u|-2M\log|u|+\mathcal{O}(1).

(1.4)

Figure 2
:
Depiction of the Penrose diagram of
\mathcal{D}
. In §
1.3
, we will pose data on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
whose choice is informed by the heuristic analysis of the setup of Fig.
1
.

Limits

?
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
?
, or

?
as
v\to\infty
?
will always be taken along constant
u
, and vice versa.
We will denote by
\partial_{u}
and
\partial_{v}
the partial derivatives with respect to
(u,v)
-coordinates, and by
\not{\nabla}_{u}
,
\not{\nabla}_{v}
the corresponding projected covariant derivatives. Furthermore, any operator adorned with a
\mathring{}
(e.g.
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1},\mathring{\not{\nabla}},\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}
) will denote a differential operator on the unit sphere.
We will mostly be working in a double null orthornormal frame so that, for any covariant
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent tensor
\Xi
,

(\not{\nabla}_{u}\Xi)(e_{A},\dots,e_{B})=\partial_{u}(\Xi(e_{A},\dots,e_{B})),

e_{A},\dots,e_{B}
denoting the components of the frame on the sphere. Whenever we write the integral of a tensor field (e.g.
\int_{u_{1}}^{u_{2}}\Xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u
), this, too, is understood componentwise.

We make use of the standard
\mathcal{O}
notation, see §
2.4
for definitions.

Finally, for any covariant
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent tensor
\Xi
, we will use the notation
\Xi_{\ell}
to denote the projection onto spherical harmonics with angular frequency
\ell
, we write
\Xi^{\mathrm{E}}
and
\Xi^{\mathrm{H}}
to denote electric and magnetic part, and we write
\overline{\Xi}=\overline{\Xi^{\mathrm{E}}}+\overline{\Xi^{\mathrm{H}}}=\Xi^{\mathrm{E}}-\Xi^{\mathrm{H}}
to denote the magnetic conjugate (cf. Def.
2.4
).
All these notions and notations are explained in detail in §
2
.

The system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild,

whose stability was shown in
[
DHR19a
]
(see also
[
Joh19
]
) and whose scattering theory was constructed in
[
Mas22a
,
Mas22b
]
, is introduced in detail in §
3
, see Def.
3.1
, and arises from linearising solutions to (
1.1
) in the double null form

\displaystyle\bm{g}(\epsilon)=-4\bm{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{u}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{v}+\bm{\not{g}}_{ab}(\epsilon)(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\theta}^{a}-\bm{b}^{a}(\epsilon)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{v})(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\theta}^{b}-\bm{b}^{b}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{v}).

(1.5)

around Schwarzschild, writing
\bm{\Omega}=\Omega+\epsilon\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
etc.
(We here used lowercase Latin indices
a,\,b
to distinguish from the orthonormal frame
e_{A},\,e_{B}
.)

The arising linearised equations can be regarded as an independent system of equations on Schwarzschild that consists of transport and elliptic equations for the linearised metric components

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}},\,{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b},

which we will schematically refer to as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{g}
,
transport and elliptic equations for the connection coefficients

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}},

which we will schematically refer to as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Gamma}
, and of hyperbolic equations for the Weyl curvature coefficients

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}.

We will schematically refer to these as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{R}
, and we will also include the Gaussian curvature
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}
in
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{R}
.

Some examples for equations from the system of linearised gravity are given by

For the definition of these quantities as well as the full set of equations, we again refer to §
3
. Let us already emphasize that the curvature coefficients above correspond exactly to the celebrated Newman–Penrose scalars
\Psi_{0},\dots,\Psi_{4}
, i.e., we have

\displaystyle\{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}},(\rho,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}),\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}\}\longleftrightarrow\{\Psi_{0},\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4}\}.

(1.9)

Furthermore,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
, the out- and ingoing null shear, respectively, correspond to
\sigma
and
\lambda
in the notation of the Newman–Penrose formalism.
For a dictionary between the formalism employed here (which goes back to
[
CK93
]
) and that of Newman and Penrose
[
NP62
]
, we refer the reader to Appendix
A.3
.
In particular, we can now define the peeling property:

Definition 1.1
.

A solution to the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild satisfies peeling/peels towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
if
r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
attains a limit towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
. This implies that
r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}},\,r^{3}(\rho,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}),\,r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}
and
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
also attain limits towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

Similarly, a solution satisfies peeling towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
if
r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
attains a limit towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, which implies that
r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}},\,r^{3}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}),\,r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}
and
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
attain limits towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

Remark 1.1
.

One could additionally require that the quantities
r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
,
r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
, respectively, admit
1/r
expansions towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
,
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, respectively, and refer to this as
strong peeling
.

Let us recall the following insights from
[
DHR19a
]
: The usual complexity one encounters when studying (
1.1
), namely that it constitutes a set of
geometric
PDE, is still inherited in the linearised system in form of the existence of
linearised gauge solutions
that do not carry physical information. See already §
4
.
It is therefore helpful to identify certain
gauge invariant
quantities, i.e. quantities that remain unchanged under addition of such a gauge solution. The quantity
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
turns out to be such a gauge invariant quantity; moreover, as can already be seen from the equations above (multiply (
1.8a
) with
\Omega^{-4}r^{4}
and then act with
\not{\nabla}_{u}
, using (
1.7a
) and (
1.8b
)), it satisfies a decoupled wave equation, called the Teukolsky equation (of spin
-2
), see (
Teuk
). Similarly,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
is gauge invariant and satisfies the Teukolsky equation of spin
+2
, see (
Teuk
).
What’s more is that both of these equations can, by commutation with suitable vector fields, be transformed into the Regge–Wheeler equation, which is the standard tensorial linear wave equation on Schwarzschild with a potential:

\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)\Psi=\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{3}}\Psi.

(1.10)

We conclude this discussion by remarking that the
dynamics
of the system of linearised gravity are entirely described by the projection to
\ell\geq 2
-angular modes; see §
4.3
for details.
In particular, the Teukolsky quantities are supported on
\ell\geq 2
.

1.3
The semi-global scattering theory and the choice of seed scattering data

1.3.1
The general semi-global scattering theory

We now provide a summary of the main results obtained in Part
I
of the paper:
First, we identify a notion of seed scattering data. The precise definitions being given in Definitions
5.1
–
5.2
, the specification of seed scattering data along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}
consists of specifying

•

the limits of
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
,
r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
,

•

the restrictions of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
to
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
,

•

the restrictions of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}
,
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
to
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
, the final sphere of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

The main result that is proved over the course of sections
5
–
7
is the following (see Thm.
5.1
for the precise statement):

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose that a smooth scattering data set on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}
as above is given, and suppose there exist positive numbers
\epsilon,\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as well as a smooth symmetric and tracefree two-tensor field
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
such that, as
u\to-\infty
, these seed data satisfy along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=\mathcal{O}_{2}\left(r^{-\frac{1}{2}-\delta}\right),

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}\left(r^{-1-\epsilon}\right).

(1.11)

Then there exists a unique scattering solution realising this seed scattering data set.

An overview of the proof of Theorem
1.1
can be found in §
5.2
.

Remark 1.2
.

The precise requirement on the initial data captured by (
1.11
) is that they induce finite Regge–Wheeler energy. In view of the recent work of Shen
[
She23
]
, one might expect to be able to prove this result under weaker decay assumptions (
\delta>-1/2
) by working directly with Bianchi pairs instead, but the rates (
1.11
) are sufficiently general for the present work.

Remark 1.3
.

There are, of course, many different but equivalent choices one can make to specify seed data. For instance, in view of (
1.6b
) we could have specified
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
instead of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}
.
Notice, however, that it is, in general, not possible to replace the specifications along
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
with specifications along
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
, unless the initial data decay very fast.
One way of seeing this is that the decay rate in (
1.11
) corresponds, by (
1.6a
) and (
1.7a
), to a
r^{-5/2-\epsilon}
-decay rate for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
, which means that (
1.8b
) cannot be integrated from
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
: In order to obtain
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, we are thus forced to specify its values along
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
.

The seed scattering data carry a large number of unphysical degrees of freedom. Although not necessary for the proof of the theorem, it is helpful to understand this freedom in more detail.
One way of entirely removing this freedom is as follows:

Definition 1.2
.

It is possible to subtract pure gauge solutions such that
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
vanish on
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
vanishes along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, the limit
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
vanishes along
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
, and the limits
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
as well as
r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
vanish along
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
, cf. Prop.
5.2
and Corollary
5.4
.
A solution for which all these quantities vanish is said to be in
the initial data gauge.

The initial data gauge indeed entirely fixes the unphysical degrees of freedom of the data in the sense that any nontrivial addition of a
linearised gauge solution
will make at least one of the quantities in Def.
1.2
nonvanishing.

The initial data gauge reveals that the physical degrees of freedom of the data are then entirely carried by
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
along
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
, by
\not{\nabla}_{u}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}=2\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, and by
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}
(which, by (
1.8a
) is equivalent to
\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
) along
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
.

Later on, we will fix the gauge by a double Bondi normalisation condition, which is defined as follows:

Definition 1.3
.

A solution is said to be
Bondi-normalised towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
if the limits of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
and
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
vanish.

A solution is said to be
Bondi-normalised towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
if the limits of

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
and
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
vanish.

A solution is
doubly Bondi-normalised
if it is Bondi-normalised towards both
\mathcal{I}^{-}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

The effect of Bondi normalisation is that the limit of the linearised metric perturbation vanishes identically along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
,
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, respectively.

Remark 1.4
.

Our initial data gauge in particular implies the corresponding solution to be Bondi-normalised towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

At this point, we believe it is appropriate to briefly contrast the present work to the second author’s
[
Mas22b
]
: In the present work, we write down specific scattering data that model a certain class of physics; so the central object are the
scattering data and their asymptotics
.
On the other hand,
[
Mas22b
]
studies the
global scattering map
and its properties in the entire domain of outer communications of Schwarzschild. But in order to study properties of the scattering map, it suffices to only analyse the scattering data in some convenient dense subset (namely that of smooth, compactly supported data), and to then extend the results by density arguments.
Since the semi-global scattering problem is simpler, but somewhat different, from the global scattering problem, we use the opportunity to give an entirely self-contained, alternative presentation of the former, which, in particular, does not use a gauge fixing procedure and clearly elucidates how to implement physically motivated assumptions on the scattering data.

1.3.2
The seed data describing the exterior of the
N
-body problem.

Having discussed a fairly general class of seed scattering data so far, we now want to establish the connection of these seed data to some actual physics, namely to the physics of
N
infalling bodies from the infinite past.
For such systems, we always want to exclude radiation coming in from the infinite past, at least for sufficiently late times.
The gauge-invariant mathematical version of this condition is given by the following requirement:

\lim_{u\to-\infty}(r\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}-r^{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K})=0\quad\text{for all}\,v\geq v_{1},

(1.12)

see already Def.
8.1
in §
8
for details and further commentary.

While the results of this paper provide the asymptotics for general seed scattering data, we here single out three specific cases, each of them capturing a different version of
N
infalling bodies from the infinite past:
A loose definition of each of these three different cases is as follows (see Definitions
8.2
–
8.4
for precise versions).

Definition 1.4
.

Suppose that a given seed scattering data set has no incoming radiation along
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
. Then we say that it describes the exterior of a system of

N
infalling masses following approximately hyperbolic orbits near the infinite past
if
1.13
–
(III)
hold:

(I)

There exists
\delta>0
and some nonvanishing
\underline{\mathscr{B}}
with
\not{\nabla}_{u}\underline{\mathscr{B}}=0
(and supported on all angular modes) such that

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-6\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-4}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-4-\delta}).

(1.13)

(II)

The limit
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
exists and is non-vanishing (and is supported on all angular modes).

(III)

The limit
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{A}
exists and is non-vanishing (and is supported on all angular modes).

If, on the other hand, we replace in the above (
1.13
) by

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{C}r^{-5}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-6})

(1.14)

for some
\mathscr{C}
supported on all angular modes with
\not{\nabla}_{u}\mathscr{C}=0
, moreover assuming that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
admits an asymptotic expansion in powers of
1/r
, and otherwise leave items
(II)
and
(III)
unchanged, then we say that the seed scattering data describe the exterior of a
compactly supported gravitational perturbation
along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. (The reader may think of this perturbation as being localised around
N
points along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.)

If, finally, we instead replace in the above (
1.13
) by

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}r^{-11/3}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-4}),

(1.15)

with
\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}
supported only on
\ell=2
(and
\not{\nabla}_{u}\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}=0
), and demand the limits in items
(II)
and
(III)
to vanish, then we say that the seed scattering data describe the exterior of a system of
N
infalling masses following approximately parabolic orbits
.

We note that these definitions do not entirely fix the seed scattering data, but they fix the relevant leading order asymptotic behaviour of the data and the resulting solutions!

The definitions for hyperbolic orbits and parabolic orbits are both merely justified by the Post-Newtonian theory (cf. Fig.
1
); where, for instance, the projection
\mathscr{A}_{\ell}
attains the physical interpretation of corresponding to the
\ell
-th Newtonian matter multipole moment of the infalling masses. This has been discussed in detail in §2 of
[
Keh24
]
, see also §
8
for a brief recap.

In contrast, the definition for a compactly supported gravitational perturbation is, at the same time, a mathematical proposition that generic compactly supported gravitational perturbations (this means that the limit on the LHS of (
1.12
) is compactly supported along
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v<v_{1}}
) induce such seed scattering data, cf. Fig.
3
.
To further the comparison to the case of infalling masses following hyperbolic orbits, we note that
\mathscr{A}_{\ell}
is then given by (
8.13
), which can be viewed as the radiative analogue to the Newtonian matter multipole moment. Cf. Prop.
8.1
as well as Remark
8.5
.

Figure 3
:
Instead of using heuristic methods to understand the gravitational radiation emitted by infalling masses as in Fig.
1
up until some null cone
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, we can
rigorously
understand the radiation emitted by compactly supported gravitational perturbations along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

1.4
Semi-global asymptotics for seed data describing the exterior of the
N
-body problem

Having discussed the general scattering theory for linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, as well as motivated our choices for the seed scattering data, we now move on to discuss the asymptotics of solutions to the semi-global scattering problem.
The way we find these asymptotics is by exploiting a class of approximate conservation laws (related to the Newman–Penrose charges, see already §
1.5.5
) that are valid for fixed angular mode solutions to the Teukolsky equations of general spin
s
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
corresponding to
s=+2
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
corresponding to
s=-2
.
Very schematically, in the case
s=2
, these look like

\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-2\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell}(r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}))\approx r^{-2\ell-3}\cdot M(r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell}(r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}).

(1.16)

See §
9
for a general discussion and (
9.15
) for the precise version. See also
[
AAG21
,
Keh22b
,
MZ22b
,
GK22
]
, where such conservation laws have been used in similar contexts.
Essentially, as a consequence of the extra power in decay on the RHS of (
1.16
), this equation can be integrated along characteristics an appropriate amount of times to obtain an asymptotic expression for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}
, see also §5 of
[
Keh24
]
for a sketch.
Roughly speaking, the general correspondence coming out of this is then as follows:

Let
p\in\mathbb{R}_{>-1/2}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider solutions to the Teukolsky equation (
Teuk
) for which
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
decays like
r^{-3-p}\log^{q}r
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and for which
\lim_{u\to-\infty}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})=0
. If
p<1
, then
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}\sim r^{-4-p}\log^{q}r
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, whereas, if
p\geq 1
, then the first term in the asymptotic expansion of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
that is not regular in
1/r
is given by
Mr^{-4-p}(1+\delta_{p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}}\log r)\cdot\log^{q}r
.

See Theorem
10.1
for the precise version for general spin.

Below, we will content ourselves with stating the asymptotic results associated to the specific data choices of Def.
1.4
.

Remark 1.5
.

In the boxed correspondence above, the assumption
\lim_{u\to-\infty}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})=0
, on its own, is, in fact, consistent with nontrivial incoming radiation. However, together with the assumption that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
decays faster than
r^{-5/2}
, incoming radiation is excluded. Cf. Rem.
8.2
.

1.4.1
The case of hyperbolic orbits

We employ the notation
\mathcal{O}^{u}
with a
u
-superscript to mean that the precise error is a function of only the variable
u
, see §
2.4
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Given a smooth seed scattering data set describing the exterior of
N
infalling masses following approximately
hyperbolic orbits
near the infinite past, then the corresponding solution satisfies the following estimates throughout
\mathcal{D}
for any
\ell\geq 2
:

(I)

The projections
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}
exhibit the following asymptotics, where
\mathscr{B}_{\ell}:=\tfrac{(\ell+2)!}{(\ell-2)!}\overline{\underline{\mathscr{B}}}_{\ell}
:

\frac{r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}}{\Omega^{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\ell-2}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\{\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0}^{2}S_{\ell,0,\ell-2,n,2}+\mathscr{B}_{\ell}r_{0}\log r_{0}S_{\ell,1,\ell-2,n,2}+\mathcal{O}^{u}(r_{0})\}\\
+\frac{(-1)^{\ell+1}2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r}{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}+\frac{(-1)^{\ell+1}{3}M\mathscr{B}_{\ell}\log^{2}r}{\ell(\ell+1)}+M\mathcal{O}(r_{0}\log r),

(1.17)

where the nonvanishing constants
S_{\ell,0,\ell-2,n,2}
are defined in (
10.64
).
In particular, the following limits are conserved

\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell+1}2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}\frac{1}{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}=\frac{(\ell-2)!}{(\ell+2)!}\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{1}{r}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{4}(\Omega^{2}r\overline{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}}_{\ell})

(1.18)

As
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
are both gauge invariant, these statements are gauge-independent.
We now fix the gauge by imposing the
initial data gauge
defined in Def.
1.2
.

(II)

All curvature coefficients decay like
r^{-3}
along
t=0
, i.e. the limits of
r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{R}_{\ell}
along
t=0
towards
i^{0}
are all finite and generically nonvanishing.
Similarly, all connection coefficients
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Gamma}_{\ell}
decay like
r^{-2}
towards
i^{0}
, and all metric coefficients
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{g}_{\ell}
decay like
r^{-1}
towards
i^{0}
.

(III)

The curvature coefficients
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}
,
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}_{\ell}
,
r^{3}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}_{\ell},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}_{\ell})
all attain finite limits towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, and we have:

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}:=\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell}\left(\frac{12\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\ell}}{\ell(\ell+1)}-\frac{4M(\ell-2)!\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{\ell}}{(\ell+2)!}\right)r_{0}^{-3}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-3-\delta}+Mr_{0}^{-4}),

(1.19)

as well as the relations

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}:

\displaystyle=\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}_{\ell}=-\int_{-\infty}^{u}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime},

(1.20)

\displaystyle(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}):

\displaystyle=\lim_{v\to\infty}(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}_{\ell},r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}_{\ell})=(-1)^{\ell}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})_{\ell}-\int_{-\infty}^{u}(\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}},\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

(1.21)

Here,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
are the unique
\ell\geq 2
scalar functions such that
\mathscr{A}=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})
.

Moreover, we have

\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{r^{4}}{\log r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}_{\ell}=\frac{(-1)^{\ell}2M}{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathscr{A}_{\ell}.

(1.22)

(IV)

The connection coefficients
\frac{r^{3}}{\log r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}_{\ell},\,r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}_{\ell},\,r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}_{\ell}
,
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}_{\ell},\,r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}_{\ell}
,
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell},\,r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell}
, and, in addition, the components
r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}_{\ell},\,r{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}_{\ell},r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}_{\ell}
as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}_{\ell},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}_{\ell}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}_{\ell}
all attain finite limits towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, with the latter limit decaying like
r_{0}^{-2}\log r_{0}
as
u\to-\infty
.

We can use the limit of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
to define and subtract a linearised gauge solution such that the solution becomes
doubly Bondi-normalised.

(V)

The doubly Bondi-normalised form of the solution exhibits the following semi-global asymptotics for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell}
:

\displaystyle\frac{{r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}}_{\ell}}{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}-\frac{(-1)^{\ell}2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}}{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}\frac{1}{r}-\frac{(-1)^{\ell}3M\mathscr{B}_{\ell}}{2\ell(\ell+1)}\frac{\log^{2}r}{r^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(M\frac{r_{0}\log r}{r^{2}}+\frac{r_{0}^{2}}{r^{2}}\right),

(1.23)

\displaystyle r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}-\frac{1}{r}\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell})+(-1)^{\ell}\cdot M\overline{\mathscr{A}_{\ell}}\frac{r_{0}\log r}{r^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}/r^{2}),

(1.24)

with the limits of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}^{-}-\int_{-\infty}^{u}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}},

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}=-\int_{-\infty}^{u}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}},

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}},

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}.

(1.26)

The precise expression for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}^{-}=\lim_{u\to-\infty}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}
is given in (
16.3
). In particular, we have, for any
\ell\geq 2
:

\lim_{u\to-\infty}\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{H}}=(-1)^{\ell}\lim_{v\to\infty}\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell}^{\mathrm{H}}.

(1.27)

(VI)

The quantities
\frac{r^{3}}{\log r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}_{\ell}
,
\frac{r^{2}}{\log r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}_{\ell}
and
\frac{r^{2}}{\log r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}_{\ell}
attain a finite, conserved limit towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
iff
M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}\neq 0
.
Finally, the quantities
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}_{\ell},\,r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}_{\ell}
,
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}_{\ell},\,r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}_{\ell}
,
r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}_{\ell}
,
r{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}_{\ell}
,
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}_{\ell}
and
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}_{\ell}
all attain a finite limit at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
that schematically behaves like

?
\mathrm{constant}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-1}\log r_{0})
?
as
u\to-\infty
, with the limits of
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}_{\ell}
,
r{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}_{\ell}
and
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}_{\ell}
being exactly conserved.

Let us provide some commentary:
The two most important results of the theorem are (
1.17
) and (
1.19
) of points
(I)
and
(III)
—they form the basis for all remaining statements of the theorem.
Indeed, at the heart of the proof of the theorem lies the asymptotic analysis of the Teukolsky variables
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
, which is presented in §
11
and §
13
(and which is a generalisation of the analysis presented in
[
Keh22b
]
).
Notice, in particular, that equation (
1.17
) shows that the peeling property does not hold (cf. Def.
1.1
).

At this point, we already want to make the important remark that the methods employed in the present work are tailored specifically to fixed angular mode analysis, and the control on the
\ell
-dependent error terms hidden in the
\mathcal{O}
-terms of e.g. (
1.17
)
does not yet allow for summation of any of these estimates.

This issue is resolved in upcoming work
[
KK24
]
, see also §
15
for further commentary.

The main point of part
(IV)
of the theorem is to show that, if the solution is Bondi-normalised towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, then it is
almost
Bondi-normalised towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, with only
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
not vanishing towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
. We can then eliminate
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
by subtracting a gauge solution that leaves the Bondi-normalisation of
\mathcal{I}^{-}
in tact.
As in
[
Mas22b
]
, one can also show that this gauge solution can be controlled in
L^{2}
in terms of an initial data energy. (See Theorem III of Section 8.2.4 therein for an example of such an estimate for the
global
scattering problem. See also Section 10.4 and Remark 11.2 of
[
Mas22b
]
for the detailed derivation of the estimate of Theorem III of
[
Mas22b
]
.)

Part
(V)
then provides the expansions of the shears towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, along with the
laws of gravitational radiation
(
1.25
)–(
1.26
) (in integral form) and what can be interpreted as the
antipodal matching condition

[
Str14
]
, namely (
1.27
) (see also
[
PS22
]
,
[
CNP23
]
).

Part
(VI)
provides a complete description of the asymptotic behaviour of the doubly Bondi-normalised solution towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
. These rates are relevant for e.g. studying the black hole stability problem starting from characteristic initial data extending towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(as in
[
DHR19a
,
KS20
,
DHRT21
]
), cf. Holzegel’s definition of asymptotic extendability
[
Hol16
]
and the discussion in §
1.5.3
.

1.4.2
The massless case

The theorem above describes the semi-global asymptotic properties of solutions describing the exterior of
N
infalling masses following approximately hyperbolic orbits near the infinite past. We can make a similar statement in the massless case (cf. Def.
1.4
):

Theorem 1.3
.

All results of Theorem
1.2
also apply to the case of seed scattering data describing the exterior of a compactly supported gravitational perturbation along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
when setting
\underline{\mathscr{B}}
to vanish.

In view of the linearity of the system, one can of course also consider a situation that describes the exterior of both infalling masses and compactly supported gravitational perturbations. (For instance, one could add a fine-tuned choice of compactly supported data for the gravitational field to the data of Thm.
1.2
such that the limit (
1.18
) vanishes.)
It is somewhat remarkable that the asymptotic behaviour near
\mathcal{I}^{+}
of a massive and a massless scattering process is so similar. This is in stark contrast to the behaviour on Minkowski: To illustrate this, we record the following curious

Observation 1
.

Consider the case of seed scattering data describing the exterior of a compactly supported gravitational perturbation along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.
If
M\neq 0
, then, for any
\ell\geq 2
,

\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell+1}\frac{(\ell-2)!}{(\ell+2)!}4M\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{\ell}r_{0}^{-3}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-4}).

(1.28)

On the other hand, if
M=0
, then there exist generically nonvanishing
\mathscr{E}_{\ell}
such that, for any
\ell\geq 2
:
2
2

2

Recall that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
is only supported on
\ell\geq 2
.

\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}=\mathscr{E}_{\ell}r_{0}^{-3-\ell}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-4-\ell}).

(1.29)

See also Fig.
6a
.
While (
1.28
) follows directly from (
1.19
), (
1.29
) is the cause of many special cancellations present on Minkowski and is discussed at the end of §
13
, cf. Thm.
13.3
.
The observation thus shows that the structure afforded by these cancellations
3
3

3

These cancellations are generalisations of the following: Solutions to the wave equation
\Box\phi=0
on Minkowski with
r\phi|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=r^{-p}
and with
\partial_{v}(r\phi)|_{\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}}=0
satisfy
\lim_{v\to\infty}r\phi_{\ell}=0
if
\ell\geq p
and
p\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, cf. Remark 1.4 of
[
Keh22b
]
.
is destroyed by the presence of nonzero mass, changing the decay rate by
\ell
powers, and making the asymptotic behaviour towards and along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
look almost identical to that of the case of masses following hyperbolic orbits.

1.4.3
The case of parabolic orbits

In the case of parabolic orbits, on the other hand, the difference to hyperbolic orbits is more notable. The following theorem summarises the main differences. Recall that we assumed the quantity
\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}
to be supported only on
\ell=2
, nevertheless, we state the theorem for
\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}
supported on all
\ell
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Given a smooth seed scattering data set describing the exterior of a system of
N
infalling masses following approximately parabolic orbits near the infinite past, then we have the following estimates throughout
\mathcal{D}
for any
\ell\geq 2
:

\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{14/3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell+1}\sqrt{3}\pi(\ell-\tfrac{2}{3})(\ell+\tfrac{5}{3})\overline{\underline{\mathscr{B}}}_{\mathrm{par},\ell}.

(1.30)

Moreover, we have

\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}=C_{\ell}\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par},\ell}r_{0}^{-\frac{8}{3}}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-\frac{8}{3}-\delta}+Mr_{0}^{-\frac{11}{3}}),

(1.31)

with
C_{\ell}=-1/2
for
\ell=2
.

Note that the decay of
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}
along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
is slower in the parabolic case than in the hyperbolic case (cf. (
1.19
)) even though, at any finite
v
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
decays faster towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
in the parabolic case! This is because the hyperbolic case features a cancellation that only occurs for integer decay rates, cf. Footnote
3
.
There is also a simple physical explanation for this fact: The quadrupole formula predicts, roughly speaking, that the limit of
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell=2}
behaves like the fourth time derivative of the quadrupole moment of the matter distribution under consideration. In the case of hyperbolic orbits, where the matter distribution grows like
|t|+\log|t|
, the leading order term is annihilated by four derivatives, and only the
\log
-term survives. This is not the case for parabolic orbits, for which the matter distribution grows like
|t|^{2/3}
.

1.5
Revisiting previous notions of asymptotic flatness and further remarks

Recall from §
1.1
that a central motivation of the present work is rooted in the desire to understand how to model isolated systems/asymptotic flatness in general relativity.
Now, we certainly do not labour under any illusions to the account of having satisfied this desire entirely, not least because our work completely sidesteps the question of the cosmological constant.

Nevertheless, we still believe that the present work marks definitive progress towards the question of modelling isolated systems.
This is because the constructions presented in this work should not just be viewed as counter-examples to, say, a smooth null infinity—instead, the work constructively provides an alternative approach (as well as corrections) to the notions listed in §
1.1
.

Furthermore, even though our constructions are merely examples, they may potentially serve to cover a quite general range of physics: For, on the one hand, there is a certain sense in which one might view e.g. hyperbolic orbits in the infinite past as
worst-case scenarios
in a class of

?
tame
?
physics, as they correspond to the fastest possible growth in size of a system near the infinite past. This would suggest that the decay rate
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}\sim r^{-4}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
is as bad as it gets.
Here, tame physics loosely means physics that are well-described by the Post-Newtonian approximation, and would exclude, for instance, hypothetical systems that remain bounded and exhibit oscillations near the infinite past and are somehow stabilised by incoming gravitational radiation.
4
4

4

The construction of an interactive system where the masses remain confined in a bounded region and are stabilised by radiation would be an interesting problem in its own right!
On the other hand, within this class of tame physics, the cases described in Def.
1.4
are the only ones we can reasonably expect, so long as we exclude incoming radiation at late advanced times or non-gravitational interactions.

We therefore believe that the asymptotics found in §
1.4
have a high degree of physical relevance.

In view of this, we will list below the modifications to the notions mentioned in §
1.1
that are induced by the results of the present work.
We hope that this will provide those who commonly work with these notions (Bondi coordinates etc.) with clearly motivated alternative assumptions.

1.5.1
Modifications to Bondi coordinates

The metric in Bondi coordinates as originally written down in
[
Sac62b
]
is given by

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\frac{V\mathrm{e}^{2\beta}}{r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{2}-2\mathrm{e}^{2\beta}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r+r^{2}h_{AB}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{A}-U^{A}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u)(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{B}-U^{B}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u),

(1.32)

with (frequently, this metric is instead written down with
(\gamma,\delta)\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(\gamma+\delta,\gamma-\delta)
, see
[
MW16
]
)

2h_{AB}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{A}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{B}=(\mathrm{e}^{2\gamma}+\mathrm{e}^{2\delta})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+4\sin\theta\sinh(\gamma-\delta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi+\sin^{2}\theta(\mathrm{e}^{-2\gamma}+\mathrm{e}^{-2\delta})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}.

(1.33)

Without going into much detail on the construction in
[
Sac62b
]
, what was then called the
outgoing radiation condition
(and what is essentially the imposition of analyticity in
1/r
) is most prominently implemented by the assumption that both
\gamma
and
\delta
have an expansion towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
of the form

(\gamma,\delta)=\frac{c(u,\theta,\phi)}{r}+\frac{b(u,\theta,\phi)}{r^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-3}),

(1.34)

with
b

vanishing
! (For, it was argued, if
b
does not vanish, then logarithmic terms would appear in the expansion of
V
, for instance, violating the analyticity assumption.)

Now, as was shown in
[
Kro99
]
, the asymptotics derived in Theorem
1.2
, namely that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
(which corresponds to
\Psi_{0}
in the N–P formalism) decays like
r^{-4}+r^{-5}\log^{2}r+r^{-5}\log r+\mathcal{O}(r^{-5})
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, mean that both
\gamma
and
\delta
have a schematic expansion of the form

(\gamma,\delta)=\frac{c}{r}+\frac{b}{r^{2}}+\frac{b^{\prime}\log^{2}r}{r^{3}}+\dots,

(1.35)

with neither
b
nor
b^{\prime}
vanishing (note that this roughly corresponds to the expansion of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
in (
1.23
)
5
5

5

Furthermore,
c(u,\theta,\phi)
then roughly corresponds to the limit of
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
in our notation. In particular, in the setting of Theorem
1.2
, we would get that
c(u,\cdot)=c_{0}+c_{1}|u|^{-1}+\dots
as
u\to-\infty
, with the coefficient
c_{0}
determined by antipodal matching as in (
1.27
).
).
(Similarly, in the case of parabolic orbits (Thm.
1.4
), one would instead find an expansion of the form
c/r+b_{\mathrm{par}}/r^{8/3}
.)

If one now follows through the integrations presented in
[
Sac62b
]
(see also
[
MW16
,
CMS95
]
), one will find that the quantity
U
has a logarithmic term appearing at order
r^{-3}\log r
, the quantity
V
has a logarithmic term appearing at order
r^{-1}\log r
, and so on. We leave the details of the derivation to the reader.

In other words, the notion of Bondi coordinates is capable of capturing the constructions of the present work if one simply weakens the assumption on the expansions on
\gamma
and
\delta
from (
1.34
) to (
1.35
). That some weakening of this kind should be done was already suggested in
[
Sac62b
]
, Page 110.
See also
[
CMS95
,
Cap21
,
GZ22
]
for references where such generalisations are studied in the physics literature. (Note, for instance, that in
[
GZ22
]
, the quantity
b
in (
1.34
) is not assumed to vanish, but
b^{\prime}
in (
1.35
) is).

1.5.2
Modifications to asymptotic simplicity

In the case of asymptotic simplicity, the modification is less straight-forward.
Of course, one can sufficiently weaken the requirement on the regularity with which a conformal boundary can be attached. The results of
[
HV20
]
, for instance, suggest that one can attach a boundary with
C^{1,\alpha}
-regularity for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
It is entirely unclear to us, however, what the
optimal
regularity is with which a conformal boundary can be attached given the decay rates of Theorem
1.2
.

1.5.3
Asymptotic flatness in the context of the IVP: The no incoming radiation condition

As we have already briefly mentioned in §
1.1
, it is not so straight-forward to come up with a modification of asymptotic flatness at the Riemannian level that captures the physics described in this paper.
Indeed, as point
(II)
of Thm.
1.2
shows, the decay along
t=0
is such that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}\sim r^{-3}
(which loosely corresponds to
\bar{g}-\delta\sim r^{-1}
). This decay is
slower
than what is assumed in most stability works starting from Cauchy data
[
CK93
,
KN03b
,
LR10
,
HV20
]
.
At the same time, point
(I)
of Thm.
1.2
shows that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}\sim r^{-4}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, which is
faster
than what is shown in most stability works starting from Cauchy data.
For instance, in the monumental
[
CK93
]
,
6
6

6

We will occasionally use bold font to clearly distinguish quantities that concern solutions to the full, nonlinear equations, from the corresponding linearised quantities.

\bm{\alpha}
decays like
\bm{r}^{-7/2}
near
i^{0}
, and this decay is propagated towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, i.e.
\bm{\alpha}
decays like
\bm{r}^{-7/2}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, cf. Fig.
4
.
Furthermore, this decay can be shown to be sharp (using, for instance, the methods of this paper). See also
[
KN03a
]
, where it is shown that if
\bm{\alpha}
decays faster on
t=0
, then it will decay faster towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

The improvement in decay towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
compared to
i^{0}
as showcased in Thm.
1.2
is thus something special. In fact, it is precisely related to the no incoming radiation condition. Thus, the appropriate correction to the usual notions of asymptotic flatness at the level of Cauchy data would, on the one hand, involve slower decay than usually assumed, and, on the other hand, involve some extra structure capturing the no incoming radiation condition, leading to faster decay towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

We will investigate this further in future work, but we want to stress the slight
shift in paradigm, namely that the scattering problem, as opposed to the Cauchy problem, is the more natural problem for understanding problems concerning spacelike infinity
.

(a)

No incoming radiation, improved decay towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

(b)

Generic data, no improvement towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

Figure 4
:
In Fig.
4a
, we depicted the results of Thm.
1.2
, which state that the decay of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\alpha}}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
is one power better than the decay towards
i^{0}
. This is in contrast to the generic situation, where you can only propagate the decay from
i^{0}
as depicted in Fig.
4b
.

Let us finally mention that points
(III)
and
(VI)
of Theorem
1.2
provide clear asymptotics for
characteristic
initial value problems set up near future null infinity, cf. Holzegel’s definition of asymptotically extendable data
[
Hol16
]
.

1.5.4
A word on Christodoulou’s argument

The starting point for this series was an attempt (cf. §1.2 of
[
Keh21
]
) to describe a very short sketch of an argument against smooth conformal compactification due to Christodoulou
[
Chr02
]
in the
nonlinear theory
, which suggests that a contradiction to a smooth null infinity can already be reached by just making an (a priori) assumption on the limit of
\bm{r}\hat{\underline{\bm{\chi}}}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(denoted
\bm{\Xi}
), namely that it decays like
|\bm{u}|^{-2}
as
\bm{u}\to-\infty
(as predicted by the Post-Newtonian theory for a system of
N
infalling masses following approximately hyperbolic orbits in the infinite past), and by assuming that
\bm{r}\hat{\bm{\chi}}
vanishes towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, i.e. by prohibiting incoming radiation from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

Now, the scattering theory approach taken in the present work is of course perfectly tailored to
dynamically
capturing the radiation emitted by
N
infalling masses from
i^{-}
as well as the assumption of no incoming radiation from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.
Moreover, at least within the linear theory around Schwarzschild, it provides a complete understanding of the asymptotics around spacelike infinity, which, in particular,
definitively confirms
the prediction from
[
Chr02
]
that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}
decays like
r^{-4}\log r
(cf. (
1.22
)) and that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
decays like
r^{-4}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(cf. (
1.17
)), and which recovers the prediction that the limit of
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
decays like
|u|^{-2}
(cf. (
1.19
) and (
1.25
)).
In addition, as we said in the previous section, the decay rate towards
i^{0}
is actually slower than what is assumed in
[
CK93
]
.

In order to clear up some confusion, we nevertheless want to point out that the accounts given in
[
Daf13
]
(Thm. 1.4 therein) or in
[
Keh21
]
(Section 1.2 therein), which attempt to capture Christodoulou’s argument, are incorrect without additional assumptions, at least in the linearised theory around Schwarzschild as considered in the present work.
This can be seen, for instance, by considering (
1.22
) and (
1.25
) combined with (
1.19
) of Thm.
1.2
.
Together, these three equations imply that the limits
\lim_{u\to-\infty}|u|^{2}\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
and
\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{r^{4}}{\log r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}
are independent, contradicting eq. (19) of
[
Daf13
]
, which states that these limits are related by a differential operator on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
7
7

7

We recall that (19) in Thm. 1.4
[
Daf13
]
simply combines (4), (8), and the three equations below (11) in
[
Chr02
]
, and is identical to (1.9) in
[
Keh21
]
.

Indeed, whether or not
\mathscr{A}
in Thm.
1.2
vanishes, the decay rate of
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
is
|u|^{-2}
if
\underline{\mathscr{B}}\neq 0
.
But if
\mathscr{A}
vanishes, then
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-4})
according to (
1.22
).
At the same time, the no incoming radiation condition is, of course, completely independent of
\mathscr{A}
.

Another way of putting this is as follows: There exist solutions to linearised gravity around Schwarzschild that have

•

No incoming radiation from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

•

The limit
\lim_{u\to-\infty}u^{2}\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
is non-vanishing.

•

The decay of the solution restricted to
t=0
is consistent with the decay assumed in
[
CK93
]
.

•

r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}
takes a finite limit towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

But in both
[
Daf13
]
and
[
Keh21
]
, it is stated that if the first three bullet points are satisfied, then
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-4}\log r)
.
Thus, Theorem
1.2
with
\mathscr{A}=0\neq\underline{\mathscr{B}}
would form a counter-example to both Theorem 1.4 of
[
Daf13
]
and to the argument presented in §1.2 of
[
Keh21
]
. (The same is true for the account given in §7.1 of
[
CKWY21
]
.)
Again, there is the caveat that Thm.
1.2
concerns linearised gravity, whereas the references above all concern the nonlinear theory!
We expect, however, that nonlinear effects will not alter our conclusion, e.g. because the relation (19) in
[
Daf13
]
is linear.

Notice that the counter-example above still has a non-smooth future null infinity, because, in view of (
1.17
),
\frac{r^{5}}{\log^{2}r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
attains a finite limit towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
In particular, this counter-example is
not
a counter-example to the idea that the two assumptions on
\mathcal{I}^{-}
(no incoming radiation) and on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(namely that
\bm{\Xi}
decays as in the Post-Newtonian prediction) are sufficient to preclude a smooth
\mathcal{I}
.
We here do not give any speculation on the mathematical realisation of this idea; see, however, Thm. 2.2 of
[
Keh21
]
.

1.5.5
The modified Newman–Penrose charges

A particular discovery that was made within the Newman–Penrose framework was a set of conserved quantities along null infinity nowadays called the Newman–Penrose charges
[
NP65
,
NP68
]
, the physical interpretation of which having occupied researchers’ minds ever since.

Now, the definition of the original N–P charges requires asymptotic simplicity; moreover, it is believed that, in the full, nonlinear theory, only a finite amount of them are conserved (see also
[
Lon22
]
).
It turns out, however, that modified versions of the Newman–Penrose charges can still be defined in a much more general context, see also
[
Kro00
,
GK22
]
, and, in the context of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, §
9.4
of the present paper.
Indeed, the reason why these are conserved (namely the existence of a set of approximate conservation laws that asymptotically become exact conservation laws) plays a crucial role in the proof of Thm.
1.2
.
The modified Newman–Penrose charges corresponding to the solution of Thm.
1.2
are given in Theorems
11.1
and
13.1
, respectively, and it turns out that they are equivalent to the conserved limits in (
1.18
).

We here want to highlight three features of these modified Newman–Penrose charges:
Firstly, concerning their physical interpretation: In our context, even though they are conserved along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, the modified N–P charges are still dynamically obtained quantities to which we can ascribe a clear interpretation in terms of the initial seed data, with the modified N–P charge of angular mode
\ell
loosely being a product of monopole (in this case the Schwarzschild mass
M
) and
\ell
-th multipole of the data.

Secondly, in the linear theory, the modified N–P charges entirely fix the leading-order asymptotics at late times, as was described in detail in
[
GK22
]
.

Thirdly, we strongly expect all (infinitely many) modified N–P charges to still be conserved even in the full, non-linear theory. Loosely speaking, this is because of the high degree of conformal irregularity that they measure, which we expect to be robustly propagated also in the nonlinear theory.
The robustness of the
modified
N–P charges in the case of low conformal regularity can be showcased already at the linear level: Under the assumption of a smooth null infinity, the associated
classical
Newman–Penrose charges for linearised gravity around Schwarzschild need to be substantially altered compared to the Minkowski case in order to still be conserved.
