On the other hand, if null infinity is suitably irregular, then the modified Newman–Penrose charges wash away the difference between the Minkowski and the Schwarzschild case.

1.6
A conjecture on global asymptotics

Based on Theorem
1.3
, the discussion in §
1.5.5
and the general insights of
[
GK22
]
, we formulate the following conjecture:

(a)

The dispersive case.

(b)

The collapsing case.

Figure 5
:
Penrose diagrams corresponding to Conjecture
1

Conjecture 1
.

Consider the scattering problem for the
nonlinear Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
)
with a Minkowskian
i^{-}
and with sufficiently small, compactly supported data along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.
Then the resulting spacetime will exhibit asymptotics as depicted in Fig.

, namely:
The component of the Weyl curvature
\alpha
decays like

towards

are conserved along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
for all
\ell
.
Furthermore, each angular mode of the limit of
r\underline{\alpha}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
decays like
u^{-3}
both towards

and towards

.

We further conjecture that the same rates are valid in the case of black hole formation such as in

.

In order to give context for the rates conjectured above, we want to contrast them to the asymptotic behaviour in a few other situations:

1.)

Asymptotics near

, and where

.

2.)

Compactly supported data along
t=0
in Schwarzschild.
If, on the other hand, we consider compactly supported data along a Cauchy surface for

, then the late-time decay behaviour will be, by the results of
[

]

only deals with the Teukolsky equations on Schwarzschild, not the full system of linearised gravity.
,
\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}\sim M|u|^{-4-\ell}
as
u\to\infty
. (If
M=0
, then the late-time asymptotics are trivial by the strong Huygens’ principle.) Cf. Fig.

.

3.)

Compactly supported Cauchy data in the nonlinear theory.
There is, as of now, no rigorous work on the late-time asymptotics for solutions to the nonlinear Einstein vacuum equations (

) arising from compactly supported perturbations of, say, Schwarzschild initial data. However, heuristics

,

for compactly supported data along

.

.

).

1.7
Overview of the paper

We finally give an overview of the paper’s structure.
We begin with some preliminaries to make the paper self-contained.
First, in §
2
, we introduce the Schwarzschild family of spacetimes and give the geometric foundations for the remainder of the paper. This section is largely informed by
[
Chr09
,
DHR19a
,
Czi17
]
.

In §
3
(corresponding to §3(a) of
[
Keh24
]
), we then introduce the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, following
[
DHR19a
,
Mas22b
]
. As this system is written down using the Christodoulou–Klainerman formalism, and many are more familiar with the Newman–Penrose formalism, we provide a detailed dictionary between the two formalisms in Appendix
A.3
.

In §
4
(corresponding to §3(b) of
[
Keh24
]
), we recall the class of pure gauge and linearised Kerr solutions identified in
[
DHR19a
]
.

Beyond these preliminaries, the paper is divided into three parts, the first one corresponding to the results introduced in §
1.3
, and the last two corresponding to the results introduced in §
1.4
.

In Part
I
,
comprised of §
5
–§
8
, we set up and solve the scattering problem for the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild.

In §
5
, we define a notion of seed scattering data; we write down the main theorem (corresponding to Thm.
1.1
), give an overview of its proof, and we provide an
a priori
analysis of the corresponding solution along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}
.
We also give an account of how to remove the unphysical degrees of freedom from a seed scattering data set (cf. Def.
1.2
).

In §
6
, we then develop a
semi-global
scattering theory for the Regge–Wheeler equation (
1.10
). The main result of §
6
is a statement adapted from
[
Mas22a
,
Mas22b
]
, but we give an alternative proof and prove a few extra results concerning uniqueness and decay that will prove useful for our schemes.

In §
7
, we then infer from this scattering theory for the Regge–Wheeler equation the existence of a unique scattering solution to the entire system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild.

Finally, in §
8
, we give the explicit definition of the no incoming radiation condition (which is gauge-independent and somewhat surprising), and we make various definitions for what it means for a seed scattering data set to describe the exterior of
N
incoming bodies from the infinite past, cf. Def.
1.4
. We then provide a preliminary analysis of solutions arising from such data.

To facilitate the comparison with
[
Keh24
]
, we state that §
5
corresponds to §3(c)–§3(d) of
[
Keh24
]
, §
6
corresponds to §4(b), §
7
to §4(c), and §
8
to §3(e) and §5(a) of
[
Keh24
]
.

In Part
II
,
comprised of §
9
–§
15
, we find the asymptotics for solutions to the Teukolsky and Regge–Wheeler equations of general spin. This part of the paper can be read independently of the previous part, and it corresponds to §5(b)–§5(e) of
[
Keh24
]
.

In §
9
, we derive a class of approximate conservation laws enjoyed by fixed angular frequency solutions to the Teukolsky (or Regge–Wheeler) equations, and we define the associated modifed Newman–Penrose constants.

In §
10
, we then perform the asymptotic analysis for general solutions to the Teukolsky equations of spin
s
(
s=2
corresponds to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
,
s=-2
to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
) with no incoming radiation, making, however, a simplifying additional assumption in the case
s<0
.

In §
11
, we then apply these results to the physically motivated data of §
8
for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
, and then, in §
12
, for the Regge–Wheeler quantities.

Similarly, in §
13
, we apply the results of §
10
to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
, also removing the simplifying assumption made in §
10
.

Section §
14
contains various explicit computations whose results have been used in the previous sections.

Finally, in §
15
, we comment on (but don’t resolve) the issue of summing the asymptotics obtained for fixed angular frequencies.

In Part
III
,
corresponding to §6 of
[
Keh24
]
, we finally take the results from the previous part on the asymptotic behaviour of solutions to Teukolsky and Regge–Wheeler equations in order to compute the semi-global asymptotics for the entire system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, restricting to solutions arising from scattering data describing the exterior of hyperbolic orbits or compactly supported gravitational perturbations (cf. Def.
1.4
or Defs.
8.2
,
8.4
).

Comparisons of the formalism used in the present work to the Newman–Penrose formalism, as well relations to spin-weighted functions, operators and spherical harmonics are found in Appendix
A
. Various computations of integrals used throughout the work are found in Appendix
B
.

2
The Schwarzschild family of spacetimes

2.1
The geometry of the Schwarzschild solution

We define, for
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the Schwarzschild family of spacetimes as the family of smooth Lorentzian manifolds
(\mathcal{M}_{M},g_{M})
, where
\mathcal{M}_{M}=\{(t,r,\theta,\varphi)\in\mathbb{R}\times(2M,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}
, and where the metric
g_{M}
in coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
is given by

\displaystyle g_{M}=-\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\not{g},

(2.1)

where
\not{g}
is simply the rescaled metric on the unit sphere:

\not{g}=r^{2}\mathring{\not{g}}=r^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})

(2.2)

Next, we introduce the Eddington–Finkelstein double null coordinates

\displaystyle u=\frac{1}{2}(t-r_{*}),\qquad v=\frac{1}{2}(t+r_{*}),

(2.3)

where
r_{*}
is the familiar radial function defined by

\displaystyle r_{*}=r+2M\log\left(\frac{r}{2M}-1\right).

(2.4)

In this double null coordinate system
(u,v,\theta,\varphi)
, the metric takes the form

\displaystyle g_{M}:=-4\Omega^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v+r^{2}\mathring{\not{g}},

\displaystyle\Omega=\sqrt{1-\frac{2M}{r}}.

(2.5)

We denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}
the outgoing null hypersurface
\{u=\lambda\}
, while
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{\nu}
will refer to the ingoing null hypersurface
\{v=\nu\}
. The sphere formed by the intersection of
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}\cap\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{\nu}=\{(\lambda,\nu)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda,\nu}
.

The Schwarzschild manifold can be extended to a manifold with boundary in various directions.
Using the coordinate system
(v,r,\theta,\varphi)
, we can attach the boundary
\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\{(v,r,\theta,\varphi):r=2M\}
, called the future event horizon, thus extending the manifold to
\mathbb{R}\times[2M,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
We can similarly attach
\mathcal{H}^{-}
, the past event horizon, by working in coordinates
(u,r,\theta,\varphi)
.

In a similar fashion, we can attach boundaries at infinity (without viewing them as conformal boundaries).
Working in the coordinate system
(x,u,\theta,\varphi)
, where
x(u,v):=\frac{1}{r(u,v)}
,
we attach the hypersurface
\mathcal{I}^{+}:=\{x_{v},u,\theta,\varphi:x_{v}=0\}
, called future null infinity.
We can similarly attach
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, called past null infinity, by working in coordinates
(x_{u},v,\theta,\varphi)
.
See
[
Mas22a
,
DRS18
]
for an extended discussion, in particular Def. 4.2.1 in the latter.

A note on conventions:
Even though we have now introduced several different coordinate systems; whenever we write
\partial_{u}
or
\partial_{v}
, it will always be with respect to the double null coordinate system
(u,v,\theta,\varphi)
.
Furthermore, we will allow ourselves to use the coordinates
u,v
to also refer to objects living in the extended manifold
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{M}:=\mathcal{M}_{M}\cup\mathcal{H}^{-}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}\cup\mathcal{I}^{+}
by permitting
u,v
to attain the values
\pm\infty
.
Finally, when we talk about limits

?
as
u\to-\infty
?
or

?
as
v\to\infty
?
etc., it will always be understood that the respective other double null coordinates are kept fixed.

2.2
The double null gauge

The metric in the form (
2.5
) is an example of spacetime metrics
\bm{g}
that are cast in a
double null gauge
, i.e. a coordinate system
(\bm{u},\bm{v},\bm{\theta}^{a})
where
\bm{u},\bm{v}
are null coordinates with respect to
\bm{g}
and
(\bm{\theta}^{a})
refers to an atlas on the topological spheres
\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}
that are formed by the intersections of the loci of
\bm{u},\bm{v}
.
In such a coordinate system, the metric
\bm{g}
takes the form

\displaystyle\bm{g}=-4\bm{\Omega}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{u}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{v}+\bm{\not{g}}_{ab}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}^{a}-\bm{b}^{a}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{v})(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}^{b}-\bm{b}^{b}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{v}).

(2.6)

In the above, for any
(\bm{u},\bm{v})
,
\bm{\Omega}(\bm{u},\bm{v},\bm{\theta}^{a})
is a scalar field on
\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}
,
\bm{b}(\bm{u},\bm{v},\bm{\theta}^{a})
is a vector field tangent to
\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}
and
\bm{\not{g}}(\bm{u},\bm{v},\bm{\theta}^{a})
is a positive definite bilinear form on
\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}
which has vanishing projection in both null directions.
Note that
\bm{\not{g}}
is the metric induced by
\bm{g}
on
\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}
.

The metric (
2.6
) gives rise to an orthonormal frame
(\bm{e}_{3},\bm{e}_{4},\bm{e}_{A})
via

\displaystyle\bm{e}_{3}=d\bm{u}^{\sharp}=\bm{\Omega}^{-1}\partial_{\bm{u}},\qquad\bm{e}_{4}=d\bm{v}^{\sharp}=\bm{\Omega}^{-1}\partial_{\bm{v}}-\bm{b}^{A}\bm{e}_{A},

(2.7)

and
\bm{e}_{A}
defined such that
(\bm{e}_{A}|_{\mathcal{S}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}})
is an orthonormal frame on
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}
.
For example, in the double null gauge defined by the Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates on the Schwarzschild exterior, the orthonormal frame derived from the metric (
2.5
) is given by

\left(e_{1}=\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta},\,e_{2}=\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi},\,e_{3}=\frac{1}{\Omega}\partial_{u},\,e_{4}=\frac{1}{\Omega}\partial_{v}\right).

(2.8)

An important convention in this paper is that uppercase Latin indices
A,B,C\dots
will always refer to the indices on the spheres.

2.3
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent tensors

When working in a double null gauge, it is convenient to decompose the metric, connection and curvature components into tensor fields that are everywhere tangent to the spheres
\bm{\mathcal{S}}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}
.
A beautiful and thorough presentation of this in generality is given in Chapter 1.2 of
[
Chr09
]
.

Here, we restrict our attention to the Schwarzschild exterior in Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates, where the spheres
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
of the Eddington–Finkelstein foliation are round spheres with area-radius function given by
r=r(u,v)
and metric given by
\not{g}=r^{2}\mathring{\not{g}}
,
\mathring{\not{g}}
being the standard metric of the unit sphere.
A one-form
X
is then said to be
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent if
X(e_{3})=0=X(e_{4})
at each point, and a vector field
V
is said to be
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent if it is tangent to
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
for all
u,v
.
These definitions can easily be generalised to include covariant, contravariant and mixed-type
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tensor fields, see
[
Chr09
]
.
Moreover, it is easy to show that the musical isomorphism (i.e. the raising and lowering of indices via the metric
g
and its inverse
g^{-1}
) preserves the property of a tensor field to be
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent.
We denote the bundle of
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent
(p,q)
-tensors on
\mathcal{M}_{M}
by
\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(p,q)}\mathcal{M}_{M}
, and similarly for any spherically symmetric subset of
\mathcal{M}_{M}
.
In what follows, for any tensor bundle
\mathcal{T}
over
\mathcal{M}_{M}
, we denote by
\Gamma^{\infty}(\mathcal{T})
the space of smooth sections over
\mathcal{T}
.

A special role will be played by the tensor bundle
\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}_{stf}\mathcal{M}_{M}
, which consists of all elements of
\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}\mathcal{M}_{M}
that are symmetric and trace-free with respect to
\not{g}
.
We will also just call these tensors stf 2-tensors and, given
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}\mathcal{M}_{M})
, we will denote

(\alpha_{stf})_{AB}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{AB}+{\alpha_{BA}})-\not{g}_{AB}\not{g}^{CD}\alpha_{CD}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{AB}+{\alpha_{BA}})-\not{g}_{AB}\mathrm{tr}_{\not{g}}\alpha.

(2.9)

Let
\nabla
be the Levi–Civita connection of the Schwarzschild spacetime
(\mathcal{M}_{M},g)
.
We denote by
\not{\nabla}
the covariant derivative with respect to the metric
\not{g}
on the spheres
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
.
Note that, at each point
(u,v)
, this is related to the Levi–Civita connection
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
simply via
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}=r(u,v)\cdot\not{\nabla}
.

For any tensor field
\digamma\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(p,q)}\mathcal{M}_{M})
, we denote by
\not{\nabla}_{3}\digamma
the component of
\nabla_{3}\digamma
that is everywhere tangent to
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
. An analogous definition gives
\not{\nabla}_{4}
.
We will very often work with the weighted covariant derivatives

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{u}:=\Omega\not{\nabla}_{3}=\not{\nabla}_{\partial_{u}},

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{v}:=\Omega\not{\nabla}_{4}=\not{\nabla}_{\partial_{v}}.

(2.10)

Note the relations

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{u},\not{\nabla}_{v}]=0,

\displaystyle[\not{\nabla}_{u},r\not{\nabla}_{A}]=0=[\not{\nabla}_{v},r\not{\nabla}_{A}].

(2.11)

In the computations of this paper, the following simple fact will be used a lot:
For any
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent
(0,q)
-tensor
\Xi
, we have

\displaystyle(\not{\nabla}_{u}\Xi)(e_{A_{1}},\dots,e_{A_{q}})=\partial_{u}(\Xi(e_{A_{1}},\dots,e_{A_{q}}))

(2.12)

\displaystyle(\not{\nabla}_{v}\Xi)(e_{A_{1}},\dots,e_{A_{q}})=\partial_{v}(\Xi(e_{A_{1}},\dots,e_{A_{q}}))

In words, evaluating the transversal covariant derivative
\not{\nabla}_{u}
or
\not{\nabla}_{v}
of an
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tensor in the orthonormal frame
(e_{A},e_{B})
is the same as computing the partial derivative of the components of the tensor with respect to that frame.
In the following, whenever we write down expressions such as
\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\Xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v
or similar, we will mean this component-wise.

2.4
Norms and the
\mathcal{O}
-notation

Given
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent
(p,q)
-tensor fields
\digamma
,
\tilde{\digamma}
on
\mathcal{M}_{M}
, we define the inner product

\digamma_{1}\cdot\digamma_{2}:=(\not{g}^{-1})^{A_{1}B_{1}}\cdots(\not{g}^{-1})^{A_{p}B_{p}}\not{g}_{C_{1}D_{1}}\cdots\not{g}_{C_{q}D_{q}}\digamma_{A_{1},\dots,A_{p}}^{C_{1},\dots,C_{p}}\tilde{\digamma}_{B_{1},\dots,B_{p}}^{D_{1},\dots,D_{p}},

(2.13)

and we define the pointwise norm via

|\digamma|^{2}:=\digamma\cdot\digamma.

(2.14)

For
f\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{M})
, we then write

\digamma=\mathcal{O}(f)

(2.15)

if there exists a positive uniform constant
C
such that
|\digamma|\leq C\cdot f
.
Moreover, we write

\digamma=\mathcal{O}^{u}(f)

(2.16)

if
\digamma=\mathcal{O}(f)
and
\not{\nabla}_{v}\digamma=0
in
(u,v,\theta,\varphi)
-coordinates.

Speaking of constants,
C
will generally be a constant that is allowed to change from line to line, and instead of saying that
f\leq C\cdot g
, we will often just write
f\lesssim g
.

We will also use the
\mathcal{O}
-notation restricted to subsets of
\mathcal{M}_{M}
.
We will then also employ the
\mathcal{O}_{\infty}
-notation:
If
\digamma
is an
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent tensor field on
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v}\cap\{u\leq-1\}
, then

\digamma=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(f)

(2.17)

will mean that for all
m,\,n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists uniform positive constants
C_{m,n}
s.t.

|\not{\nabla}_{u}^{m}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{n}\digamma|\leq C_{m,n}|\partial_{u}^{m}f|.

(2.18)

(In particular, with this convention,
\digamma=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(1)
will mean that all
\not{\nabla}_{u}
-derivatives of
\digamma
vanish identically.)
If we only write
\digamma=\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{m}}({f})
, it will mean that (
2.18
) only holds for all
m\leq\tilde{m}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We define the following
L^{2}
-norm (note that this has no
r
-weight!):

\displaystyle\|\digamma(u,v,\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}:=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}|\digamma|^{2}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi.

(2.19)

We similarly define

\displaystyle\|\digamma(u,\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v_{1}\leq v\leq v_{2}\})}:=\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\digamma|^{2}(u,v,\theta,\varphi)\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\|\digamma(\cdot,v,\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v}\cap\{u_{1}\leq u\leq u_{2}\})}:=\int_{u_{1}}^{u_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\digamma|^{2}(u,v,\theta,\varphi)\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v.

(2.21)

We will often just write
\mathbb{S}^{2}
rather than
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
, and
[v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
instead of
\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v_{1}\leq v\leq v_{2}\}
. We finally define the Sobolev norm for
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v_{1}\leq v\leq v_{2}\})
via

\|\digamma\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v_{1}\leq v\leq v_{2}\})}=\|\digamma\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v_{1}\leq v\leq v_{2}\})}+\|\not{\nabla}_{v}\digamma\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v_{1}\leq v\leq v_{2}\})}+\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\digamma\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v_{1}\leq v\leq v_{2}\})}

and similarly for
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
and higher order Sobolev norms.
The corresponding spaces are defined as completions of the space of smooth tensor fields with respect to these norms.

2.5
Differential operators on the sphere

We now construct a variety of differential operators from
\not{\nabla}
.
Since
\not{\nabla}=r^{-1}\cdot\mathring{\not{\nabla}}
, we will simply provide the definitions for the corresponding differential operators on the unit-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
; lifting these definitions to differential operators acting on
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent tensors over
\mathcal{M}_{M}
is straight-forward. (Given a covariant tensor field on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we canonically identify it with a tensor field on
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
for each
(u,v)
, and we extend it to an
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent tensor field on
\mathcal{M}_{M}
by demanding that it vanishes when contracted with
e_{3}
or
e_{4}
.)

We denote the volume form associated to the unit-sphere metric
\mathring{\not{g}}
by
\mathring{\not{\varepsilon}}
:

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\varepsilon}}=\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi,

(2.22)

we use
∗
to denote the Hodge dual on
(\mathbb{S}^{2},\mathring{\not{g}})
, and we denote the unit-sphere Laplacian by
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}
(so we have that
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}=r^{2}\not{\Delta}
).

Definition 2.1
.

We define for any covariant tensor field
\Xi
the divergence via

\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\Xi_{A_{2}\dots A_{p}}=\mathring{\not{g}}^{A_{0}A_{1}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{A_{0}}\Xi_{A_{1}A_{2}\dots A_{p}},

(2.23)

and the curl via

\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\Xi_{A_{2}\dots A_{p}}=\mathring{\not{\varepsilon}}^{A_{0}A_{1}}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{A_{0}}\Xi_{A_{1}A_{2}\dots A_{p}}.

(2.24)

We then define

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}:\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})\to\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\times\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})},

\displaystyle\beta\mapsto(\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\beta,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\beta)

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}:\Gamma^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))\to\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2}),

\displaystyle\alpha\mapsto\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\beta

(2.26)

We further define the
L^{2}
-adjoints of these operators:

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}:\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\times\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\to\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2}),

\displaystyle(f,g)\mapsto-\mathring{\not{\nabla}}f+{}^{\ast}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}g

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}:\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})\to\Gamma^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2})),

\displaystyle\beta\mapsto-(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\beta)_{\mathrm{stf}}

(2.28)

We finally define
the magnetic adjoint
of
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}
to be

\displaystyle\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}:\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})\to\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})}\times\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})},

\displaystyle\beta\mapsto(\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\beta,-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\beta).

(2.29)

For the relations between these operators and the

?
\eth
?
-operators that scholars of the Newman–Penrose formalism may be more familiar with, see the Appendix
A.2
.

The following simple lemma will be used very frequently throughout the paper:

Lemma 2.1
.

We have the relationships

\displaystyle-\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-1,

\displaystyle-\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\not{\Delta}},

(2.30)

\displaystyle-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2,

\displaystyle-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+1.

Proof.

The results follow from relating the commutator of two covariant derivatives to the Riemann tensor, using that the Riemann tensor in two dimensions is determined by the Gauss curvature, and using that the Gauss curvature of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is 1.
∎

2.6
Definition of tensorial spherical harmonics

Let
Y_{\ell,m}\in\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})}
be the usual real-valued spherical harmonics with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
m=-\ell,-\ell+1,\dots,\ell
.
We now define the corresponding 1-form spherical harmonics and symmetric traceless two-tensor spherical harmonics following
[
Czi17
]
.

For this, we first recall the general fact that any smooth 1-form
\beta
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
can be written as

\beta=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)

(2.31)

for a unique pair of smooth functions
(f,g)
that is supported on
\ell\geq 1
. Moreover, the kernel of the operator
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}
is spanned by functions supported on
\ell=0
.

Similarly, any smooth symmetric traceless two-tensor
\alpha
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
can be uniquely represented as

\alpha=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g),

(2.32)

with
(f,g)
a pair of smooth functions supported on
\ell\geq 2
. Moreover, the kernel of the operator
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}
is spanned by functions supported on
\ell=0,1
.

We can now already define what it means for a one-form or a symmetric, tracefree two-tensor to be supported on angular frequency
\ell
.

Definition 2.2
.

We say that a 1-form (or a traceless, symmetric two-tensor) is supported on angular frequency
\ell
if the functions
(f,g)
in the unique representation (
2.31
) (or (
2.32
)) are supported on angular frequency
\ell
.
By definition, one-forms have no support on
\ell=0
, and stf two-tensors have no support on
\ell=0,1
.

It will, however, be convenient to also make a choice for an explicit orthonormal decomposition of the space of square-integrable one-forms and stf two-tensors on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
Y_{\ell,m}
be the usual real-valued spherical harmonics. Then we define

\displaystyle Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1}=(\ell(\ell+1))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(Y_{\ell,m},0),

\displaystyle Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1}=(\ell(\ell+1))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,Y_{\ell,m}),

(2.33)

\displaystyle Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},2}=(\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell-1)(\ell+2))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1},

\displaystyle Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},2}=(\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell-1)(\ell+2))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1}.

The superscripts
\mathrm{E}
and
\mathrm{H}
, standing for electric and magnetic part, respectively, have been chosen in view of the parity properties of these harmonics.

Definition 2.4
.

For any
\alpha=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)\in\Gamma^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
, we define its
electric part

\alpha^{\mathrm{E}}
, its
magnetic part

\alpha^{\mathrm{H}}
as well as its
magnetic conjugate

\overline{\alpha}
via

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{E}}:=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,0),

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{H}}:=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,g),

\displaystyle\overline{\alpha}:=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,-g).

(2.34)

Remark 2.1
.

Up to
r
-scaling and factors of
\sqrt{2}
, these spherical harmonics are the same as Thorne’s pure spin vector and tensor harmonics
[
Tho80
]
.
We also provide a detailed discussion relating them to the Newman–Penrose spin-weighted spherical harmonics in the Appendix
A.2
.

It follows from either
[\mathring{\not{\Delta}},\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}]=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}
or
[\mathring{\not{\Delta}},\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}]=4\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}
that, for
s=1,2
:

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\Delta}}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},s}=(-\ell(\ell+1)+s^{2})Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},s},

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\Delta}}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},s}=(-\ell(\ell+1)+s^{2}))Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},s}.

(2.35)

We note that, in view of Def.
2.2
, the same holds true more generally for 1-forms/traceless symmetric two-tensors supported on angular frequency
\ell
.

We also have the following basic observations, which follow directly from Def.
2.3
and Lemma
2.1
:

Lemma 2.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(Y_{\ell,m},Y_{\ell,m})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\ell(\ell+1)}(Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1}+Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1}),

(2.36)

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}(Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1},Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\ell(\ell+1)}(Y_{\ell,m},Y_{\ell,m}),

as well as

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1}=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},2},

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1}=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},2},

(2.37)

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},2}=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1},

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},2}=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1}.

Next, we define the projection onto these spherical harmonics in the following way:

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\beta
be a smooth 1-form. Then we define

\displaystyle\beta_{\ell,m}^{E}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\beta\cdot Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi,

\displaystyle\beta_{\ell,m}^{H}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\beta\cdot Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi

(2.38)

as well as

\beta_{\ell}:=\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}(\beta_{\ell,m}^{E}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1}+\beta_{\ell,m}^{H}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1}).

(2.39)

Similarly, let
\alpha
be a smooth symmetric traceless two-tensor. Then we define

\displaystyle\alpha_{\ell,m}^{E}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\alpha\cdot Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},2},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\ell,m}^{H}:=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\alpha\cdot Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},2}

(2.40)

as well as

\alpha_{\ell}:=\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}(\alpha_{\ell,m}^{E}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},2}+\alpha_{\ell,m}^{H}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},2}).

(2.41)

In particular, while the
\alpha_{\ell,m}^{E}
are scalars, the
\alpha_{\ell}
are traceless symmetric two-tensors.

The following standard proposition is proved in
[
Czi17
]
:

Proposition 2.1
.

The family of electric and magnetic 1-form spherical harmonics together forms a complete, orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})
, and, for any
\beta\in L^{2}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})
:

||\beta||_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}||\beta_{\ell}||_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}(\beta_{\ell,m}^{E})^{2}+(\beta_{\ell,m}^{H})^{2}.

(2.42)

Similarly, the family of electric and magnetic stf two-tensor spherical harmonics forms a complete, orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
, and, for any
\alpha\in L^{2}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
,

||\alpha||_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}||\alpha_{\ell}||_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}(\alpha_{\ell,m}^{E})^{2}+(\alpha_{\ell,m}^{H})^{2}.

(2.43)

As before, the definitions of these tensor spherical harmonics on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
can easily be extended to tensor spherical harmonics on
\mathcal{M}_{M}
.

2.7
The Kerr family

The Schwarzschild family smoothly embeds into the two-parameter family of Kerr spacetimes
(\mathcal{M}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
(see, for instance,
[
DRS16
]
).
This means that when studying
perturbations of the Schwarzschild family, one will, generically, see nearby members of the Kerr family.
We will return to this point in §
4
, where we will write down the linearised version of the Kerr solution around Schwarzschild.

3
The linearised Einstein vacuum equations around Schwarzschild in a double null gauge

3.1
The linearisation procedure

We here give a rough outline of the linearisation procedure, the purpose being that this paper can be read in a self-contained manner. For details, see
[
DHR19a
]
.

Consider a one-parameter family of metrics
\bm{g}(\epsilon)
in double null gauge (
2.6
) solving the Einstein vacuum equations on
\mathcal{M}_{M}
such that
\bm{g}(\epsilon=0)
equals the Schwarzschild metric:

\displaystyle\bm{g}(\epsilon)=-4\bm{\Omega}^{2}(\epsilon)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{u}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{v}+\bm{\not{g}}_{ab}(\epsilon)(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\theta}^{a}-\bm{b}^{a}(\epsilon)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{v})(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\theta}^{b}-\bm{b}^{b}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{v}).

(3.1)

The condition that
\epsilon=0
give the Schwarzschild metric means that
\bm{\Omega^{2}}(0)=\Omega^{2}
,
\bm{b}(0)=0
and
\bm{\not{g}}(0)=\not{g}
.
Next, we associate to this family of metrics a family of
orthonormal
frames
(\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2},\bm{e}_{3},\bm{e}_{4})
according to (
2.7
), and we introduce the following decomposition of the connection coefficients:

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{AB}:=\bm{g}(\bm{\nabla}_{A}\bm{e}_{4},\bm{e}_{B}),

\displaystyle\bm{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}:=\bm{g}(\bm{\nabla}_{A}\bm{e}_{3},\bm{e}_{B}),

(3.2)

\displaystyle\bm{\eta}_{A}:=-\frac{1}{2}\bm{g}(\bm{\nabla}_{3}\bm{e}_{A},\bm{e}_{4}),

\displaystyle\bm{\underline{\eta}}_{A}:=-\frac{1}{2}\bm{g}(\bm{\nabla}_{4}\bm{e}_{A},\bm{e}_{3}),

\displaystyle\bm{\omega}:=\frac{1}{2}g(\bm{\nabla}_{4}\bm{e}_{3},\bm{e}_{4}),

\displaystyle\bm{\underline{\omega}}:=\frac{1}{2}\bm{g}(\bm{\nabla}_{3}\bm{e}_{4},\bm{e}_{3}).

Since the metric
g
is in double null gauge, all other connection coefficients (except for those on the sphere) either vanish or are easily related to the ones above. For instance,
\bm{\xi}_{A}:=\frac{1}{2}\bm{g}(\bm{\nabla}_{4}\bm{e}_{4},\bm{e}_{A})=0
, and
\bm{\zeta}_{A}:=\frac{1}{2}\bm{g}(\bm{\nabla}_{A}\bm{e}_{4},\bm{e}_{3})=\bm{\eta}_{A}-\bm{\not{\nabla}}_{A}\bm{\Omega}
.

Similarly, we decompose the Riemann curvature tensor
\bm{R}
into the coefficients (
\bm{\not{\varepsilon}}
denoting the induced volume form on
\bm{\mathcal{S}_{\bm{u},\bm{v}}}
):

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}_{AB}:=\bm{R}_{A4B4},

\displaystyle\bm{\underline{\alpha}}_{AB}:=\bm{R}_{A3B3},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\bm{\beta}_{A}:=\bm{R}_{A434},

\displaystyle\bm{\underline{\beta}}_{A}:=\bm{R}_{A343},

\displaystyle\bm{\rho}:=\tfrac{1}{4}\bm{R}_{3434},

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}\bm{\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\bm{R}_{AB34}.

The symmetries of the Riemann tensor allow to recast the last two definitions into the form

-\bm{\rho}\bm{\not{g}}_{AB}+\bm{\sigma}\bm{\not{\varepsilon}}_{AB}=\bm{R}_{A3B4}.

(3.4)

Finally, we denote the Gauss curvature on the spheres by
\bm{K}
.

Remark 3.1
.

These decompositions are the basis of the Christodoulou–Klainerman formalism, which is essentially the same as the original Newman–Penrose formalism.
In particular, writing
\sqrt{2}m=\bm{e}_{1}+i\bm{e}_{2}
,
\sqrt{2}\overline{m}=\bm{e}_{1}-i\bm{e}_{2}
9
9

9

Note that
\overline{m}
does
not
denote the magnetic conjugate (cf. Def.
2.4
) here, but the complex conjugate. No further confusion should arise in the main body of the paper, as we won’t further mention these complex frame vector fields.
, we can define the Newman–Penrose scalars
\Psi_{i}
via:

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}

\displaystyle:=\bm{\alpha}(m,m),

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}:=\bm{\underline{\alpha}}(\overline{m},\overline{m})

(3.5)

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle:=\bm{\beta}(m)

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}:=\bm{\underline{\beta}}(\overline{m})

(3.6)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle:=-\bm{\rho}+i\bm{\sigma}

(3.7)

For a detailed dictionary between the two, see the Appendix
A.3
.

At this point, we write down the Einstein vacuum equations as well as the Bianchi equations with respect to this decomposition, and linearise each of the resulting equations with respect to the parameter
\varepsilon
(by writing
\bm{\Omega}=\Omega+\varepsilon\cdot\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
etc. and then discarding all terms of order
\varepsilon^{2}
).
In the following, we will always use the superscript
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{{}}}
to denote linearised quantities and bold font to denote nonlinear quantities.

3.2
The system of equations of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild (
3.9
)–(
3.32
)

Following the linearisation procedure outlined above gives the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild.
In fact, this system can be formulated without reference to any linearisation by viewing it as a geometric system of partial differential equations on Schwarzschild for a set of unknown quantities:

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be any spherically symmetric open subset of
\mathcal{M}_{M}
, and let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}=\left(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}},\,{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}\right),

(3.8)

where

•

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
,
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}

\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
,

•

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
, as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}

\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,1)}\mathcal{D})
, and

•

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}

\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}_{stf}\mathcal{D})
.

We say
\mathfrak{S}
is a solution to the linearised Einstein vacuum equations around Schwarzschild if the components of
\mathfrak{S}
satisfy the equations (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) everywhere on
\mathcal{D}
.

We now write down the equations of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild (
3.9
)–(
3.32
).

3.2.1
The equations governing the metric coefficients

First, we have the equations governing the metric coefficients:

2\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)=r(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}})

(3.12)

\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b})=\frac{2\Omega^{2}}{r}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}})

(3.13)

3.2.2
The equations governing the connection coefficients

Next, we have the equations governing the connection coefficients:

\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}\right)=2\Omega^{2}\left(\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}-\frac{4M}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)\right)-\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}

(3.14)

\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}\right)=2\Omega^{2}\left(\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}+r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}-\frac{4M}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)\right)+\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}

(3.15)

\partial_{u}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}=-\Omega^{2}\left(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}-\frac{4M}{r^{3}}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)\right)=\partial_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}

(3.21)

Furthermore, we have the following elliptic equations:

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}

\displaystyle=-\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}-r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2\Omega}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}

\displaystyle=\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2\Omega}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}

(3.23)

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}=-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}

(3.24)

We also have the following linearised equation for the Gaussian curvature:

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}=-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}+\frac{1}{2r}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)})-\frac{2\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right),

(3.25)

where
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}
is defined via

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}:=-\frac{1}{4r^{2}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2){\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}+\frac{1}{2}\not{\mathrm{div}}\not{\mathrm{div}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}.

(3.26)

3.2.3
The equations governing the curvature coefficients

The system of linearised gravity is completed by the linearised Bianchi equations:

\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\Omega^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}+\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}

(3.27)

\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})=-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\Omega^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}+\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}

(3.32)

3.3
The Teukolsky equations and the Regge–Wheeler equations

Having written down the full system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, we now derive from (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) a set of equations that will play a central role in this paper, among them the decoupled Teukolsky and the Regge–Wheeler equations.

3.3.1
The Teukolsky equations satisfied by
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\alpha}}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\underline{\alpha}}}

Multiply (
3.32
) by
\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}
and apply
\not{\nabla}_{v}
to it using (
3.31b
) and (
3.17b
) to obtain a decoupled wave equation for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
, known as the Teukolsky equation (recall
-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2
from (
2.30
)):

\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})\right)=r^{3}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}-6Mr^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}.

(Teuk)

Similarly, starting from (
3.27
), multiplying it by
\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}
and applying
\not{\nabla}_{u}
to it using (
3.28a
) and (
3.18a
), we obtain

\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})\right)=r^{3}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}-6Mr^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}

(
Teuk
)

These equations will be discussed in much more detail in §
9
.

3.3.2
The Regge–Wheeler equations satisfied by
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\Psi}}
and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\underline{\Psi}}}

In applications, the first order terms in (
Teuk
), (
Teuk
) (which appear if one writes the equations as
\not{\nabla}_{v}\not{\nabla}_{u}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}=\dots
) give rise to several difficulties.
Fortunately, these first-order terms can be removed by commutations with suitable vector fields.
First, we note that,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}+6M\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}},

(3.33)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},-r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})+6M(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}-r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}).

(3.34)

Eq. (
3.33
) is just (
3.32
) multiplied by
\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}
, and (
3.34
) then follows from (
3.33
) by using (
3.31a
) and (
3.17a
).

Similarly, using (
3.27
) and (
3.28b
) with (
3.18b
), we get

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}+6M\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}},

(3.35)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})+6M(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}-r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}})

(3.36)

We now define:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\psi}}:=\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}),

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\psi}}}:=\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}).

(3.37)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}:=\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}),

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}:=\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}).

An observation of purely algebraic nature (cf. Lemma
3.1
) is that if
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
satisfies (
Teuk
) (or if
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
satisfies (
Teuk
)), then the remarkably simple Regge–Wheeler equation,

\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)\Psi-\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{3}}\Psi=0,

(RW)

is satisfied for
\Psi=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}
. (See §
9
for much more details on commutations with
\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}
,
\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}
.)

As a consequence of

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}=-4\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}),

(3.38)

eq. (
RW
)
is therefore also satisfied by
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})
. In fact, in that case, (
RW
) is just the tensorialised version of the scalar Regge–Wheeler equation:

\partial_{u}\partial_{v}(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})-\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{3}}(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})=0,

(RW-scalar)

which can also be verified directly by acting with
\partial_{v}
on (
3.30a
) and using (
3.28b
).

3.3.3
The Teukolsky–Starobinsky identities and further relations

We further note the relations (which follow directly from (
Teuk
))

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}

\displaystyle=\left((\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)-2\left(1-\frac{{3}M}{r}\right)\right)\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})-\frac{6Mr^{{2}}}{\Omega^{2}}r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}

(3.39)

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega^{4}}{r^{4}}\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}

\displaystyle=(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}-6M(\not{\nabla}_{u}+\not{\nabla}_{v})(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})

(3.40)

Similarly, we have (as a consequence of (
Teuk
))

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}

\displaystyle=\left((\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)-2\left(1-\frac{{3}M}{r}\right)\right)\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})+\frac{6Mr^{{2}}}{\Omega^{2}}r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}

(3.41)

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega^{4}}{r^{4}}\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}

\displaystyle=(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}+6M(\not{\nabla}_{u}+\not{\nabla}_{v})(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})

(3.42)

From the identity (
3.38
), we can now derive the the celebrated Teukolsky–Starobinsky identities
[
TP74
]
by either acting with
(\Omega^{-2}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{2}
, applying (
3.42
) and (
3.30a
) and (
3.28a
), or by acting with
(\Omega^{-2}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{2}
, applying (
3.40
) and (
3.30b
) and (
3.31b
):

\frac{\Omega^{4}}{r^{4}}\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{4}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})\\
=(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}-4\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})+6M(\not{\nabla}_{u}+\not{\nabla}_{v})(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}),

(3.43)

\frac{\Omega^{4}}{r^{4}}\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{4}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})\\
=(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}-4\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})-6M(\not{\nabla}_{u}+\not{\nabla}_{v})(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}).

(3.44)

Remark 3.2
.

For any
\digamma\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(p,q)}\mathcal{M}_{M})
, we have
r(\Omega^{-2}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{4}(r^{4}\digamma)=r^{-4}(\Omega^{-2}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{4}(r\digamma)
.

Remark 3.3
.

The angular operator on the RHS of (
3.43
) or (
3.44
) can be rewritten:
Writing
\alpha=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)
, it follows from Lemma
2.1
that

-4\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g))=-2(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,g).

(3.45)

Conversely, one can show that for any
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
,
{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\alpha=(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)\alpha
.
Thus, we can equivalently write the angular operator on the RHS of (
3.43
) or (
3.44
) as the operator that sends
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)\mapsto(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,-g)
, or as
{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}
, i.e. we have

\frac{\Omega^{4}}{r^{4}}\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)^{4}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})={2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}-6M(\not{\nabla}_{u}+\not{\nabla}_{v})(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}).

(3.46)

In terms of the spin-weighted

?
eth
?
-operators
\eth
,
\eth^{\prime}
of the Newman–Penrose formalism, the operator
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}}
corresponds to
\eth^{\prime 4}
(or
\eth^{4}
when acting on
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
), see the appendix for details (Remark
A.3
.)

A useful lemma

For future reference, let us here already collect the following statements concerning the relation between the Regge–Wheeler and Teukolsky operators:

Definition 3.2
.

Given
\digamma\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}_{stf}\mathcal{M}_{M})
, define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Teuk}^{+2}[\digamma]

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}\not{\nabla}_{u}\digamma-\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2-\frac{6M}{r}\right)\digamma,

(3.47)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Teuk}^{-2}[\digamma]

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\digamma-\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2-\frac{6M}{r}\right)\digamma,

(3.48)

\displaystyle\mathrm{RW}[\digamma]

\displaystyle:=\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\left(\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4-\frac{6M}{r}\right)\right)\digamma.

(3.49)

Lemma 3.1
.

For any
\digamma\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}_{stf}\mathcal{M}_{M})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{RW}\left[\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{2}{(\digamma)}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{2}\mathrm{Teuk}^{+2}[\digamma],

(3.50)

\displaystyle\mathrm{RW}\left[\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{2}{(\digamma)}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{2}\mathrm{Teuk}^{-2}[\digamma],

(3.51)

as well as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Teuk}^{+2}\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\digamma\right]=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left\{\mathrm{RW}\left[\digamma\right]\right\}.

(3.52)

Proof.

The first two identities are proved in section 3 of
[
Mas22a
]
, so we only prove the last.
We first compute

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right]\not{\nabla}_{u}=\not{\nabla}_{u}\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right]-\frac{2\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}(3\Omega^{2}-2)\not{\nabla}_{v}.

(3.53)

From this, we similarly infer that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right]\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)&=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}\left[\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)-\frac{2\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}(3\Omega^{2}-2)\not{\nabla}_{v}\right]\\
&=\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{4}}{\Omega^{4}}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right]\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right]-12M\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\\
&\;\;\;\;-2(3\Omega^{2}-2)\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right].\end{split}

(3.54)

Finally, note that

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right]\frac{6M}{r}=-12M\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}+\frac{6M}{r}\left[\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right].

(3.55)

Applying the above relations to the expression

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left[\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}-\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+\left(4-\frac{6M}{r}\right)\right]

(3.56)

proves the claim.
∎

4
Pure gauge and linearised Kerr solutions

Central to the understanding of the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild is the existence of two classes of explicit solutions to it.
The first of them arises by linearising either a Schwarzschild solution with nearby mass, or a Kerr solution with mass
M
near Schwarzschild (cf. §
2.7
)–written in double null gauge–around Schwarzschild. This class is the class of linearised Kerr solutions.

The other class, the class of pure gauge solutions, arises by considering coordinate transformations (at the nonlinear level) of the variables
\bm{u},\bm{v},\bm{\theta}^{A}
such that the double null form of the nonlinear metric (
2.6
) is preserved, and then linearising the metric in these new coordinates around the Schwarzschild metric.
These solutions to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
), which represent the gauge ambiguity inherited from (
1.1
), have been derived and classified in Section 6 of
[
DHR19a
]
; we here write them down for completeness.

4.1
The linearised Kerr solutions

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the following solves (
3.9
)–(
3.32
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}=-2\mathfrak{m},\qquad\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m},\\
&\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}=-\frac{2M}{r^{3}}\mathfrak{m},\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}=\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{r^{2}},\end{split}

(4.1)

with all remaining components vanishing.
Note that this solution is entirely supported on
\ell=0
. We will refer to it as
the linearised nearby Schwarzschild solution with parameter
\mathfrak{m}
, or by
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}=\{\mathfrak{a}_{-1},\mathfrak{a}_{0},\mathfrak{a}_{+1}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Then the following is a solution to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
):

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}=\frac{4M}{r^{2}}\sqrt{2}\sum_{m=-1}^{1}\mathfrak{a}_{m}Y^{\mathrm{H},1}_{\ell 1},

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=\frac{3}{4r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}=-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}=\frac{\Omega}{r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}},

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}=\frac{6M}{r^{4}}\sum_{m=-1}^{1}\mathfrak{a}_{m}Y_{\ell 1},

with all remaining components vanishing. Note that this solution is entirely supported on
\ell=1
. We will refer to it as
the linearised Kerr solution with parameter
\mathfrak{a}
, or by
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{a}}
.

4.2
The pure gauge solutions

4.2.1
The outgoing gauge solutions

The solution below arises from an infinitesimal coordinate transformation of
\bm{v}
:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
f(v,\theta^{A})
be a smooth scalar function on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Then the following solves (
3.9
)–(
3.32
):

\displaystyle\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\Omega^{2}}\partial_{v}(f\Omega^{2}),\quad

\displaystyle{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{r}\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+\Omega^{2}\right)f,\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{r}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,0),

(4.3)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}

\displaystyle=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\left(r\partial_{v}(\tfrac{f}{r}),0\right),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,0),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{\Omega^{2}}\partial_{v}(\tfrac{\Omega^{2}}{r}f),0\right),

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\Omega}{r^{2}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,0),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{v}(\tfrac{\Omega^{2}}{r}f),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\Omega^{2}-1\right)f,

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{4}}f,\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{6M\Omega}{r^{4}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,0),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{3}}\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\right)f,

with
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
related to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
via (
3.11
), and with the remaining components
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}
vanishing. We will refer to this solution as
the outgoing gauge solution generated by
f
, or by
\mathfrak{S}_{f}
.

In practice, we will require that, as
v\to\infty
,
f(v,\theta^{A})=f_{0}(\theta^{A})v+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(v^{1-\epsilon})
for some
\epsilon>0
and some smooth, potentially identically vanishing
f_{0}(\theta^{A})
.

4.2.2
The ingoing gauge solutions

The solution below arises from an infinitesimal coordinate transformation of
\bm{u}
:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
\underline{f}(u,\theta^{A})
be a smooth scalar function on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. The following is a solution to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
):

\displaystyle\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\Omega^{2}}\partial_{u}(\underline{f}\Omega^{2}),\quad

\displaystyle{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\Omega^{2}}{r}\underline{f},\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}

\displaystyle=0,

(4.4)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}

\displaystyle=-2\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r}\,\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\underline{f},0),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}

\displaystyle=-\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{\Omega^{2}}\partial_{u}(\tfrac{\Omega^{2}}{r}\underline{f}),0\right),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}

\displaystyle={+}\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\underline{f},0),

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\Omega}{r^{2}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\left(\underline{f},0\right),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\Omega^{2}-1\right)\underline{f},\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}

\displaystyle=-2\partial_{u}(\tfrac{\Omega^{2}}{r}\underline{f}),

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}

\displaystyle=-\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{4}}\underline{f},\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}

\displaystyle={+}\frac{6M\Omega}{r^{4}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\underline{f},0),\quad

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{3}}\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\right)\underline{f},

with
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
related to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
via (
3.11
), and with the remaining components
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}
vanishing. We will refer to this solution as
the ingoing gauge solution generated by
\underline{f}
, or by
\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}
.

In practice, we will require that, as
u\to-\infty
,
\underline{f}(u,\theta^{A})=\underline{f}_{0}(\theta^{A})u+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(u^{1-\epsilon})
for some
\epsilon>0
and some smooth, potentially identically vanishing
\underline{f}_{0}(\theta^{A})
.

Remark 4.1
.

Notice that the outgoing gauge solution generated by a constant
C
is identical to the ingoing gauge solution generated by
-C
.

4.2.3
The sphere gauge solutions

The solution below arises from an infinitesimal coordinate transformation of
\{\bm{\theta}^{A}\}
:

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
q_{1}(v,\theta^{A})
,
q_{2}(v,\theta^{A})
be two smooth scalar functions on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Then the following solves (
3.9
)–(
3.32
):

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}

\displaystyle=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(q_{1},q_{2}),\qquad

\displaystyle{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}

\displaystyle=2\mathring{\not{\Delta}}(q_{1}),\qquad

\displaystyle r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\partial_{v}q_{1},\partial_{v}q_{2}),

(4.5)

with all other components vanishing. We refer to these solutions as the
sphere gauge solutions
generated by
(q_{1},q_{2})
, or by
\mathfrak{S}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
.

In practice,
q_{1},\,q_{2}
and all its derivatives will be required to be bounded.

Definition 4.1
.

A solution is said to be pure gauge if it is a linear combination of the solutions from Propositions
4.3
–
4.5
.

4.3
Solutions supported on
\ell\leq 1
are non-dynamical

The following statement is proved (in slightly modified form) in Theorem 9.2 of
[
DHR19a
]
:

Proposition 4.6
.

If a solution
\mathfrak{S}
solves (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) on an open subset of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{M}
and is supported on
\ell=0,1
, then, up to suitable addition of a pure gauge solution,
\mathfrak{S}
can be written as a linearised nearby Schwarzschild solution (Prop.
4.1
) plus a linearised Kerr solution (Prop.
4.2
).

Remark 4.2
.

The proposition cited above embodies the fact that the
\ell=0,1
components of a solution to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) are non-dynamical, and the dynamics of the system (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) are contained in the
\ell\geq 2
.
In light of this fact, any physical discussion of (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) is mostly a discussion of the
\ell\geq 2
-modes.

4.4
Solutions supported on
\ell\geq 2
with
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\alpha}}=0=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\underline{\alpha}}}
are pure gauge

The following is proved in Theorem B.1 of
[
DHR19a
]
:

Proposition 4.7
.

If a solution
\mathfrak{S}
solves (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) on an open subset of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{M}
, is supported on
\ell\geq 2
and satisfies
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}=0
, then
\mathfrak{S}
is a pure gauge solution.

Part I:

Setting up and solving the scattering problem

Having given a thorough recap of the system of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, we now have all ingredients to formulate and solve the scattering problem for this system.
As opposed to the paper
[
Mas22b
]
, which discusses the global scattering problem with data on
\mathcal{H}^{-}
and
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, the present paper discusses the semi-global scattering problem where scattering data are posed on
\mathcal{I}^{-}
and an ingoing null hypersurface
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
at finite
v=v_{1}
, restricted to negative values of
u
(see already Fig.
7
).
Here, we opt to give an entirely self-contained presentation of it with a somewhat different approach that, for instance, does not use a gauge fixing procedure and that proves a few additional statements.

We now give an overview of the next few sections:

In §
5.1
, we define a notion of
seed scattering data
for (
3.9
)–(
3.32
), define what it means for a solution to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) to be a scattering solution realising these seed data, and we write down Theorem
5.1
, which expresses the solvability of the scattering problem under fairly general assumptions on the seed data.
We then give an overview of the proof of this theorem in §
5.2
, we construct out of the seed data the remaining scattering data along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
in §
5.3
, and we discuss certain gauge considerations such as Bondi normalisation in §
5.4
.

The key to obtaining the scattering theory for (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) is the scattering theory for the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW
) developed already in
[
Mas22a
]
. We will give a self-contained account of it in §
6
, also proving a few extra statements.

In §
7
, we then present the full details of the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

With this result having been proved under fairly general assumptions and clarifying the role of seed data in the scattering problem, we will then, in §
8
, write down a few sets of explicit assumptions on a seed scattering data set such that it can be said to describe the exterior of a system of
N
infalling bodies with no incoming radiation from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. These physical seed data will be at the heart of the entire paper.

5
The general scattering data setup for (
3.9
)–(
3.32
)

We begin by fixing our notation:
We denote by
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
the incoming null hypersurface given by

{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}:=\{(u,v,\theta,\varphi)\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{M}\,|\,v=v_{1},u\leq u_{0}<0\}.

We further denote
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}:=\{(u,v,\theta,\varphi)\in\mathcal{M}_{M}\,|\,v=v_{1},u=u_{0}\}
, and we similarly denote the limiting sphere
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}:=\{(u,v,\theta,\varphi)\in{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{M}}\,|\,v=v_{1},u=-\infty\}
.
Finally, we denote the part of
\mathcal{I}^{-}
that lies to the future of
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
by
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
. See Fig.
7
.

We will denote the future domain of dependence
D^{+}({\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}})
of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
by
\mathcal{D}
.

Figure 7
:
We will pose our scattering data on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and the part of
\mathcal{I}^{-}
that lies to the future of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
(i.e.
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
).

5.1
Seed scattering data and the main theorem (Thm.
5.1
)

Definition 5.1
.

A
smooth seed scattering data set

\mathfrak{D}
is a nonuple

\displaystyle\left(\,\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\,\,\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}\,\right),

(5.1)

where
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
are specified on
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
:

\displaystyle\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}\right),\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,1)}\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}),\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}_{\mathrm{stf}}\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}),

where
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
are specified along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\in C^{\infty}({\underline{\mathcal{C}}}),\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}_{\mathrm{stf}}{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}),\qquad

and, lastly, where
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
,
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
are specified on
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\,\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\,\,\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2}),\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}\in\Gamma^{\infty}\left(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2}\right).

Definition 5.2
.

Given a smooth seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}
, we call a solution
\mathfrak{S}
to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) on
\mathcal{D}=D^{+}({\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-})

the scattering solution realising
\mathfrak{D}
if

•

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
,
r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
and
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
realise
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
as their pointwise limits at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

•

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
,
