\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
restrict to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
,

•

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
,
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}
restrict to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
,
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
on
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
.

Over the course of the next few pages, we will prove the following theorem:

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a smooth scattering data set on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}
as in Def.
5.1
.
Suppose there exist positive numbers
\epsilon,\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as well as a smooth stf two-tensor
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
such that, as
u\to-\infty
, the components of
\mathfrak{D}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}\left(r^{-\frac{1}{2}-\delta}\right),

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}\left(r^{-1-\epsilon}\right).

(5.2)

Then there exists a unique scattering solution
\mathfrak{S}
realising
\mathfrak{D}
in the sense of Def.
5.2
, the uniqueness being understood w.r.t. the class of solutions with finite Regge–Wheeler energy (cf. Prop.
7.1
).

Remark 5.1
.

We already point out that a large part of the seed scattering data does not carry physical information. For instance, by addition of pure gauge solutions (cf. §
4
), the quantities
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
can all simultaneously be set to 0.
This will be discussed in detail in §
5.4
.

Remark 5.2
.

The assumed decay rate on
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
ensures that the seed scattering data set induces finite Regge–Wheeler energy along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and cannot be improved without using different methods. This decay rate is also what corresponds to the decay assumed in
[
BZ09
]
.

The assumed decay rate on
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, on the other hand, can be weakened due to the linearity of the system: Since this is not relevant for applications, we will stick with assuming
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
to be integrable.

5.2
Overview over the proof of Thm.
5.1

Our proof of Theorem
5.1
will be divided into the following steps.

(i)

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be as in Thm.
5.1
.
From the components of
\mathfrak{D}
on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
,
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, we uniquely construct data for all the remaining components of the system (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) (
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
etc.) such that any scattering solution realising
\mathfrak{D}
must restrict to these constructed components along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
. This is done in §
5.3
(Prop.
5.1
).

(ii)

We use a uniqueness clause for the Teukolsky equation (Cor.
6.2
) to deduce the uniqueness of a solution realising
\mathfrak{D}
in §
7.1
(Prop.
7.1
).
This uniqueness clause for the Teukolsky equation, in turn, is derived in detail from uniqueness for the Regge–Wheeler equation in §
6
.

(iii)

Not having to worry about uniqueness anymore, we initiate the construction of a solution in §
7.2
. First, we define data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
for certain components of a solution to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
). In particular, we define data for
\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. Combining this with
(i)
, we now have scattering data for the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW
).
We can thus construct a unique solution
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
to (
RW
) realising these scattering data using the results of
[
Mas22a
]
(see Thm.
6.1
), of which we give a fully self-contained presentation in §
6
.

(iv)

We construct out of this solution
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
and the already defined data along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\mathcal{I}^{-}
first
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
by integrating (
3.37
), and then the entire
\ell\geq 2
-part of a solution
\mathfrak{S}
to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) by suitably integrating the remaining equations of (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) to obtain the remaining components of a solution
\mathfrak{S}
.
We finish by explicitly writing down the
\ell=0,1
-part of
\mathfrak{S}
(which is independent of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
).

Remark 5.3
.

The step to construct the entire
\ell\geq 2
-part of
\mathfrak{S}
starting from
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
is a combination of two ingredients: (1) Deriving local existence for (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) starting from local existence for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}
. (2) A certain level of a priori quantitative decay analysis as the data are posed at infinity. For instance, when defining
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
by integrating
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
twice from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
using (
3.37
), we need to first prove that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
decays sufficiently fast so that (
3.37
) is integrable.

Since the local existence proof is quite lengthy, we also give a short overview of it in §
7.2
.

5.3
Defining the full scattering data along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}

Given a seed scattering data set according to Definition
5.1
, we may uniquely prescribe data for the full system on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by suitably solving the constraint equations on each of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
:

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a seed data set satisfying the assumption of Thm.
5.1
.
Then
\mathfrak{D}
defines a unique tuple of functions,
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent one-forms and stf two-tensor fields along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}=\left(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\right)

(5.3)

such that if
\mathfrak{S}
is scattering solution realising
\mathfrak{D}
as its seed scattering data set according to Definition
5.2
, the restriction of
\mathfrak{S}
to
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
gives
\mathfrak{S_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}
.
In particular, all
\not{\nabla}_{u}
-equations of (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) except for (
3.21
) and (
3.32
), as well as the elliptic equations (
3.12
), (
3.22
)–(
3.25
) are satisfied along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by
\mathfrak{S_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}
.

If
\mathfrak{S}
is such that
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}
converge to
-\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
,
\partial_{v}\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
as
u\to-\infty
, respectively, then there additionally exist unique
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
such that the restriction of
\mathfrak{S}
to
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
also gives
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
. In particular, the equations (
3.21
) and (
3.32
) are satisfied by
\mathfrak{S}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\cup\left(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\right)
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by systematically defining the elements of
\mathfrak{S_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}
from
\mathfrak{D}
as solutions to a subset of the relevant equations of (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, and by a posteriori proving that the remaining of the
\not{\nabla}_{u}
- and elliptic equations of (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) are automatically satisfied as well.
The uniqueness clause is addressed at the end.

1)

We define
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
via
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
by integrating (
3.11a
) from
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
with
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
as data. (Condition (
5.2
) ensures that this is integrable.)

2)

We define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
via
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
by integrating (
3.16a
) from
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
with
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
as data.

3)

We then define
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by integrating (
3.9a
) with data
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
on
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
.

4)

Next, we define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
from
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
via (
3.10a
), and we similarly define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
from
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
via (
3.18a
).

5)

Given
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, we define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by integrating (
3.28a
) with data
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
at
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
.

6)

Given
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, we now define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
as solution to the Codazzi equation (
3.23
). By multiplying (
3.23
) with
\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega}
and acting with
\not{\nabla}_{u}
, we directly verify that (
3.19a
) holds along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
. Indeed, using all our previous definitions:

\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})=\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\not{\nabla}_{u}(\tfrac{r^{2}}{\Omega}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})-\frac{1}{r}\not{\nabla}_{u}(\tfrac{r^{4}}{\Omega}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})-r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}-\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(\tfrac{r^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})=-r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}+2r\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}.

(5.4)

7)

We now define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
via (
3.12
) and our definitions of
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
. This directly implies that
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})=0
.
Moreover, inserting this definition into (
3.19a
) further shows that (
3.20a
) holds along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

8)

Next, we define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
as solution to (
3.24
):
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}:=\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}(=-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})
, where the second equality follows by construction.
Acting on this equation with
\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{2}\cdot)
and using (
3.19b
) proves that (
3.30a
) holds along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
(note that
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\nabla}}f=0
for any smooth function
f
).

9)

We define the Gaussian curvature
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
via (
3.26
).

10)

We now need to define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
: First, define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
on
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
as solution to the Gauss equation (
3.25
). Then, define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by integrating (
3.29a
) from
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
with data
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
.

11)

By construction, (
3.25
) holds on
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
. We now define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
as solution to (
3.25
) along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.
Multiplying (
3.25
) by
r^{2}
and acting with
\not{\nabla}_{u}
on it then shows, utilizing all the previous definitions, that (
3.14
) holds along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

At this point, it is left to define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
(as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
).
In order to define these quantities, we need to integrate their relevant equations from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
—we thus require that these equations are integrable. Let us therefore list a few of the decay rates as
u\to-\infty
of the quantities obtained so far:

\diamond

As
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-1-\epsilon})
according to (
5.2
), we find from (
3.16a
) that
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{1-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{\epsilon,1}\log r)+1)
.
In particular, this decay rate together with (
3.9a
) implies that
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
converges as
u\to-\infty
, and thus, by definition and by (
5.2
),
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
also converges. We write:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}:=&\lim_{u\to-\infty}\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}{-}\frac{{2}r}{\Omega^{2}}\Big|_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}{+\lim_{u\to-\infty}}\left(\frac{8}{r}\int_{u}^{u_{0}}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right){-}8\int_{-\infty}^{u_{0}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.\end{split}

(5.5)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}:=

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}(u,v_{1},\theta^{A})=\frac{1}{4}\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\right)\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}.

(5.6)

\diamond

The assumption (
5.2
) gives
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-\frac{3}{2}-\delta})
via (
3.10a
) and thus
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-\frac{5}{2}-\delta})
via (
3.18a
).

\diamond

We estimate
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
via (
3.28a
) and (
3.30a
) to get
r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}\left(r^{\frac{3}{2}-\delta}(1+\delta_{\frac{3}{2},\delta}\log r)+1\right)
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}(1+\delta_{\frac{3}{2},\delta}\log r+\delta_{\frac{1}{2},\delta}\log r)+1\right)
.

\diamond

Inserting the rates above into the linearised Codazzi equation (
3.23
) implies the estimate
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}\left(r^{1-\min\left(\delta+\frac{1}{2},\epsilon\right)}(1+\log r(\delta_{\frac{3}{2},\delta}+\delta_{1,\epsilon}))+1\right)
.

\diamond

Eq. (
3.12
) now gives
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\frac{(2\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\,\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})}{r}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}\left(r^{-1-\min\left(\delta+\frac{1}{2},\epsilon\right)}(1+\log r(\delta_{\frac{3}{2},\delta}+\delta_{1,\epsilon}))+r^{-2}\right)
. We write

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}:=\lim_{u\to-\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=2\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}

(5.7)

\diamond

Equation (
3.29a
) implies
r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}-\min(\delta,\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon)}(1+\log r(\delta_{\frac{1}{2},\delta}+\delta_{\frac{3}{2},\delta}+\delta_{\epsilon,1}))+1\right)
. It follows via (
3.25
) that
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
attains a limit as
u\to-\infty
:

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}:=\lim_{u\to-\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}+4\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}.

(5.8)

We now define the remaining quantities along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
:

12)

By the decay rates above, the RHS of (
3.17a
) is integrable. We thus define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
via integration of (
3.17a
) with
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
as data for
\Omega r\cdot\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
at
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
. By construction, angular derivatives of
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
will then converge to angular derivatives of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
.

13)

Similarly, we define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
via integration of (
3.13
) with data
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
for
r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
.

14)

Next, we define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
as the solution to the Codazzi equation (
3.22
). It follows that
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
attains a limit as
u\to-\infty
:

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}:=\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}+\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}.

(5.9)

Multiplying (
3.22
) with
r\Omega
and acting with
\not{\nabla}_{u}
, we then deduce that (
3.31a
) holds along all of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

15)

We define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
via integration of (
3.21
) with
\partial_{v}\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
as data for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
at
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
.

16)

Finally, we define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by integrating (
3.32
) with data
-\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
for
r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
at
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
.

This concludes the construction. The uniqueness part (for all quantities except
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
) goes as follows: If there are two different scattering solutions realising
\mathfrak{D}
, then their difference restricts to another solution with trivial seed scattering data. By demanding that this solution satisfies the equations (
3.9
)–(
3.32
), we can then repeat the procedure above but for trivial seed scattering data to deduce that all other quantities along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
must necessarily vanish identically.

The uniqueness of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
follows similarly under the additional convergence assumption of the proposition.∎

Remark 5.4
.

We see from the construction of the other quantities that, instead of specifying
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
and
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
on
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, we can equivalently specify these quantities on
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
.

Corollary 5.1
.

The components of
\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
constructed in Proposition
5.1
satisfy, in particular,

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-\frac{3}{2}-\delta}),\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-\frac{5}{2}-\delta}),

(5.10)

\displaystyle r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{\frac{3}{2}-\delta}(1+\log r\delta_{3/2,\delta})+1),\qquad r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}(1+\log r(\delta_{3/2,\delta}+\delta_{1/2,\delta}))+1),

\displaystyle r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{\frac{1}{2}-\min(\delta,\epsilon+\frac{1}{2})}(1+\log r(\delta_{3/2,\delta}+\delta_{1/2,\delta}+\delta_{\epsilon,1}))+1).

Proposition
5.1
allows us to also define transversal derivatives along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
to any order. In particular, we have:

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a seed data set satisfying the assumption of Thm.
5.1
, and let
\mathfrak{S}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
be as in Prop.
5.1
.
Define the following tuple of
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent stf-tensors along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}+6M\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},

(5.11)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}+6M\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})+6M(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}-r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}),

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle:=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},-r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})+6M(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}-r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})

Then any smooth scattering solution realising
\mathfrak{D}
will have
\tfrac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v}{r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}}
,
(\tfrac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{2}{r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}}
,
\tfrac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})
and
(\tfrac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})
restricting to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, respectively.

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}(1+\delta_{1/2,\delta}\log r)+1)=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},

\displaystyle r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{\frac{3}{2}-\delta}(1+\delta_{3/2,\delta}\log r)+1).

(5.12)

Proof.

The first part of the statement follows directly from (
3.33
)–(
3.36
) and Proposition
5.1
.

For (
5.12
), however, the rates derived in Proposition
5.1
would naively only give (ignoring
\log
-terms)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}-\min\left(\delta,\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon\right)}+1\right)=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}.

(5.13)

To show that these rates are in fact independent of
\epsilon
, one first confirms that (
3.41
) is satisfied along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, and then integrates this equation from
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
together with the knowledge that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-\frac{5}{2}-\delta})
.
This shows (
5.12
) for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
. The result for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
then follows from (
3.38
) and the rate (
5.10
) for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
.
∎

Finally, we deduce a corollary pertaining to lower angular modes. This result will be used for the construction of the
\ell=0,1
-part of the solution in §
7.3
.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=0,1}
be a smooth seed scattering data set satisfying the assumptions of Thm.
5.1
that is supported only on
\ell=0,1
. Then, in addition to the limit
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
defined in (
5.6
), the following limits exist as well:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}.

(5.14)

Moreover, specifying
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=0,1}=\{\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}\}
is equivalent to specifying the corresponding
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\ell=0,1}=\{\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}\}
. (Recall that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
are supported on
\ell\geq 2
since they are stf two-tensors.)

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=0,1}
is supported on
\ell=0,1
, we have that
\not{\nabla}_{u}\frac{r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\Omega}=0
, and so
r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
takes a limit. The claim for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
is similar. Finally, in order to compute the limit of
r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, we write

\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{4}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},

(5.15)

where we used (
3.24
) and (
3.23
), together with the fact that
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=0=(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})_{\ell<2}
.

In order to see the equivalence of
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
, we note that by
\not{\nabla}_{u}\frac{r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}{\Omega}=0
, specifying
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
is equivalent to specifying
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
. Similarly, (
3.9a
) implies that specifying
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
is equivalent to specifying
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
.
Finally, via (
3.29a
), specifying
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
is equivalent to specifying
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
, which, in turn, is equivalent to specifying
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
by the Gauss equation (
3.25
).
∎

5.4
Bondi normalisation of seed scattering data

We now describe how to
Bondi normalise
a seed scattering data set. This serves the purpose of identifying the physical degrees of freedom contained in a seed scattering data set.

Definition 5.3
.

A smooth seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}
is said to be
Bondi-normalised
if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

The lapse and the shift vector vanish at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
:
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=0=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}.

•

The limit
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, as well as the induced limit
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
vanishes at
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
(cf. (
5.6
)). (This automatically forces
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
to vanishes as well, cf. (
5.5
), (
5.8
)).

Note that the vanishing of
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
is automatically enforced by the vanishing of the other quantities.
We have the following proposition:

Proposition 5.2
.

For any smooth seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}
, there exist smooth functions
f(v,\theta^{A})
,
\underline{f}(u,\theta^{A})
and
(q_{1}(v,\theta^{A}),q_{2}(v,\theta^{A}))
such that the
\ell\geq 2
-part of
\mathfrak{D}-\mathfrak{D}_{f}-\mathfrak{D}_{\underline{f}}-\mathfrak{D}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
is Bondi-normalised, where
\mathfrak{D}_{f}
,
\mathfrak{D}_{\underline{f}}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
denote the seed scattering data belonging to the pure gauge solutions
\mathfrak{S}_{f}
,
\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
.

An analogous statement is true for
\ell<2
. (In this case, one also has to subtract a linearised Schwarzschild and a linearised Kerr solution.) In fact, given seed scattering data supported on
\ell<2
, the explicit scattering solution to these data is written down in §
7.3
.

Proof.

We first find
\underline{f}
supported on
\ell\geq 2
by demanding that

\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{-3}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2)\underline{f}=(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})_{\ell\geq 2}.

(5.16)

(Recall that
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2
is invertible on
\ell\geq 2
). This still leaves us with considerable freedom for
\underline{f}
.

Next, we find
f
such that

\partial_{v}f=(2\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}})_{\ell\geq 2}+(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2)^{-1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})_{\ell\geq 2}.

(5.17)

Notice that
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell\geq 2}-\mathfrak{D}_{f}-\mathfrak{D}_{\underline{f}}
now has vanishing Gaussian curvature
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}
at
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
and vanishing lapse
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. It follows that the corresponding
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
vanishes as well (by (
5.8
)).

Finally, we fix
(q_{1},q_{2})
such that

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=\left.2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(q_{1},q_{2})\right|_{v=v_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\partial_{v}q_{1},\partial_{v}q_{2}).

(5.18)

It follows that
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell\geq 2}-\mathfrak{D}_{f}-\mathfrak{D}_{\underline{f}}-\mathfrak{D}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
has vanishing limits for the corresponding
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
.
The vanishing of
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
follows by (
5.6
).
∎

Remark 5.5
.

The proof above shows that even after Bondi-normalising the seed scattering data, we still have a considerable amount of gauge freedom left: Changing
f
by a function independent of
v
or changing
\underline{f}
by a function that grows slower than
u
does not affect the Bondi--normalisation of a seed scattering data set.
10
10

10

Contained in this freedom is the (
\ell\geq 2
-part of the) BMS group at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, see
[
Mas22b
]
for a more detailed discussion.

In particular, we can use this remaining gauge freedom to infer from the proof of Prop.
5.2
:

Corollary 5.4
.

For any smooth seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}
, there exist smooth functions
f(v,\theta^{A})
,
\underline{f}(u,\theta^{A})
and
(q_{1}(v,\theta^{A}),q_{2}(v,\theta^{A}))
such that the
\ell\geq 2
-part of
\mathfrak{D}-\mathfrak{D}_{f}-\mathfrak{D}_{\underline{f}}-\mathfrak{D}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
is Bondi-normalised, and, in addition
(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})_{\ell\geq 2}=0
and
\left(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}\right)_{\ell\geq 2}=0
.

Again, the analogous statement for
\ell<2
is shown in §
7.3
.

6
Scattering for the Regge–Wheeler equation and for the Teukolsky equation

The main ingredient for our construction of a scattering solution to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) is the scattering theory for the Regge–Wheeler and Teukolsky equations developed in
[
Mas22a
]
.
This, in turn, relies heavily on the fact that the Regge–Wheeler equation admits a conserved energy.

6.1
Energy conservation for the Regge–Wheeler equation

Definition 6.1
.

For any
u,U_{1},U_{2},v,V_{1},V_{2}
with
U_{1}\leq U_{2}
,
V_{1}\leq V_{2}
, define

\displaystyle E_{u}[\Psi](V_{1},V_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v\in[V_{1},V_{2}]\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\left[|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi|^{2}+(3\Omega^{2}+1)|\Psi|^{2}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v}[\Psi](U_{1},U_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v}\cap\{u\in[U_{1},U_{2}]\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{u}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\left[|\not{\nabla}_{u}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi|^{2}+(3\Omega^{2}+1)|\Psi|^{2}\right)\right].

Define furthermore

\displaystyle E_{u}[\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi](V_{1},V_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v\in[V_{1},V_{2}]\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\left[|\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(|\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\Psi|^{2}+(3\Omega^{2}+1)|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi|^{2}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v}[\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi](U_{1},U_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v}\cap\{u\in[U_{1},U_{2}]\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{u}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\left[|\not{\nabla}_{u}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(|\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\Psi|^{2}+(3\Omega^{2}+1)|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi|^{2}\right)\right].

Finally, define, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle E^{(n)}_{u}[\Psi](V_{1},V_{2}):=\begin{cases}E_{u}[\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{\frac{n}{2}}\Psi](V_{1},V_{2}),&\text{if }\frac{n}{2}\in\mathbb{N},\\
E_{u}[\mathring{\not{\nabla}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\Psi)](V_{1},V_{2}),&\text{if }\frac{n-1}{2}\in\mathbb{N},\end{cases}

as well as

\displaystyle\underline{E}^{(n)}_{v}[\Psi](U_{1},U_{2}):=\begin{cases}\underline{E}^{(n)}_{v}[\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{\frac{n}{2}}\Psi](U_{1},U_{2}),&\text{if }\frac{n}{2}\in\mathbb{N},\\
\underline{E}^{(n)}_{v}[\mathring{\not{\nabla}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\Psi)](U_{1},U_{2}),&\text{if }\frac{n-1}{2}\in\mathbb{N}.\end{cases}

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\Psi
be a smooth solution to the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW
), and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\Psi
satisfies

\displaystyle E^{(n)}_{U_{1}}[\Psi](V_{1},V_{2})+\underline{E}^{(n)}_{V_{1}}[\Psi](U_{1},U_{2})=E^{(n)}_{U_{2}}[\Psi](V_{1},V_{2})+\underline{E}^{(n)}_{V_{2}}[\Psi](U_{1},U_{2}).

(6.1)

Later on, we will also make use of the following

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\Psi
be a smooth solution to (
RW
), and define, for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
:

\underline{E}^{\delta^{\prime}}_{v}[\Psi](U_{1},U_{2}):=\int_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v}\cap\{u\in[U_{1},U_{2}]\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\,|u|^{\delta^{\prime}}\left[|\not{\nabla}_{u}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi|^{2}+(3\Omega^{2}+1)|\Psi|^{2}\right)\right]

as well as
{E}^{\delta^{\prime}}_{u}[\Psi](V_{1},V_{2})=|u|^{\delta^{\prime}}{E}_{u}[\Psi](V_{1},V_{2})
.
Then, if
U_{2}<0<\delta^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle E^{\delta^{\prime}}_{U_{1}}[\Psi](V_{1},V_{2})+\underline{E}^{\delta^{\prime}}_{V_{1}}[\Psi](U_{1},U_{2})\leq E^{\delta^{\prime}}_{U_{2}}[\Psi](V_{1},V_{2})+\underline{E}^{\delta^{\prime}}_{V_{2}}[\Psi](U_{1},U_{2}).

(6.2)

Proof.

Multiply (
RW
) by
|u|^{\delta^{\prime}}\not{\nabla}_{T}
; the arising bulk term will have a good sign in view of
U_{2}<0<\delta^{\prime}
.
∎

6.2
Scattering for the Regge–Wheeler equation

We now give a compact, fully self-contained presentation of the scattering problem for the Regge–Wheeler equation adapted to our setting. Theorem
6.1
is a statement adapted from
[
Mas22a
]
, but it’s proved in an alternative way; all other statements in §
6
are new.

Theorem 6.1

(Adapted from
[
Mas22a
]
)
.

For
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{I}^{-}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\})
and
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
with
E_{v_{1}}[\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u_{0})<\infty
, there exists a unique solution
\Psi
to the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW
) such that for any
v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\left\|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,\cdot,\cdot)-\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\right\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}=0,\qquad\Psi|_{\mathcal{C}}=\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}.

(6.3)

This solution satisfies

\lim_{u\to-\infty}E_{u}[\Psi](v_{1},v)=\|\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}.

(6.4)

In addition, if
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
are smooth, and if
E^{(n)}_{v_{1}}[\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u_{0})<\infty
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\Psi
is smooth as well and

\lim_{u\to-\infty}E_{u}^{(n)}[\Psi](v_{1},v)=\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{s}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{\frac{n-s}{2}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2},

(6.5)

where
s=1
if
n
is odd and
s=0
if
n
is even.

We first specify and prove the uniqueness clause of Thm.
6.1
:

Proposition 6.1
.

Assume that
\Psi
is a solution to the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW
) such that

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\left\|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,v,\cdot)\right\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}=0,\qquad\Psi|_{\mathcal{C}}=0.

(6.6)

Assume furthermore that
\Psi|_{\mathcal{C}_{u_{0}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}}\in H^{1}_{loc}(\mathcal{C}_{u_{0}})
,
\Psi|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}\}}\in H^{1}_{loc}(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}\})
. Then
\Psi=0
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\Psi}\in H^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})
such that
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\widetilde{\Psi}=\Psi
. Then
\widetilde{\Psi}
also satisfies the Regge–Wheeler equation with
\widetilde{\Psi}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=0
.
Applying Hardy’s inequality on
\mathcal{C}_{u}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}
for any
v_{2}\geq v_{1}
,
u\leq u_{0}
, together with Poincaré’s inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
gives

\displaystyle\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}|\widetilde{\Psi}|^{2}\leq\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\widetilde{\Psi}|^{2}\leq\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}{\Psi}|^{2},

\displaystyle\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\widetilde{\Psi}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\widetilde{\Psi}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}{\Psi}|^{2}.

Therefore, for any
v_{2}\geq v_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\left[|\not{\nabla}_{v}\widetilde{\Psi}|^{2}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}|\widetilde{\Psi}|^{2}+\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\widetilde{\Psi}|^{2}\right]\Big|_{\mathcal{C}_{u}}=0.

Energy conservation for Regge–Wheeler implies
\widetilde{\Psi}=0
, thus
\Psi=0
. (Here, we used that
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}
is invertible on stf
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tensors, but the argument clearly also works for scalar functions by dealing with the spherically symmetric part in the same manner.)
∎

Remark 6.1
.

In view of Hörmander’s propagation of singularities theorem applied to the Regge–Wheeler equation when expressed in the variables
(v,r^{-1})
, we expect that the above uniqueness statement extends beyond the class of solutions to Regge–Wheeler with finite global energy, e.g. to the class of locally square-integrable solutions.

Proof of the existence clause of Thm.
6.1
.

We provide an argument that avoids the use of the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem and instead directly constructs the solution.

We begin by assuming that
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
,
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
are smooth and that
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
is compactly supported.
Let
\{u_{n}\}_{(n\in\mathbb{N})}\subset\mathbb{R}
be a dyadic sequence with
u_{n}\to-\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and with
\{u\leq u_{1}\}
beyond the support of
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
.
We define a sequence
\{{\Psi_{n}}\}_{(n\in\mathbb{N})}
of smooth solutions to the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW
), where
{\Psi_{n}}
is defined on
[u_{n},u_{0}]\times[v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
, by taking
\Psi_{n}
to arise from characteristic data
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}(u,\theta^{A})
at
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cap\{u\in[u_{n},u_{0}]\}
and
\int_{v_{1}}^{v}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\,\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})
on
\mathcal{C}_{u_{n}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}
. (As part of the construction, we will also extend each
\Psi_{n}
to
u\leq u_{n}
later on.)
Note that the energy norm of
\Psi_{n}
on the initial outgoing cone
\mathcal{C}_{u_{n}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}
is then bounded by:

\displaystyle E_{u_{n}}[\Psi_{n}](v_{1},v_{2})\leq\left(1+\frac{(v_{2}-v_{1})^{2}}{r(u_{n},v_{1})^{2}}\right)\|\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}+\frac{(v_{2}-v_{1})^{2}}{r(u_{n},v_{1})^{2}}\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}.

(6.7)

The strategy now is to show that the restrictions of
\Psi_{n}
to the null cones
\mathcal{C}_{u_{0}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}\}
converge as well, so that we can construct the limiting solution as the backwards solution arising from the limiting data
\Psi|_{\mathcal{C}_{u_{0}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}}
,
\Psi|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}\}}
.

First, we use the energy estimate to bound

\displaystyle E_{u_{0}}[\Psi_{n}-\Psi_{n+1}](v_{1},v_{2})+\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi_{n}-\Psi_{n+1}](u_{n},u_{0})\leq E_{u_{n}}[\Psi_{n}-\Psi_{n+1}](v_{1},v_{2}).

(6.8)

In order to estimate the RHS, we first write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{n}(u_{n},v,\theta^{A})-\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},v,\theta^{A})&=\int_{v_{1}}^{v}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}-\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},v,\theta^{A})\\
&=\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n+1},v,\theta^{A})-\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},v,\theta^{A}),\end{split}

(6.9)

and we then estimate, using the equation (
RW
) :

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n+1},\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}\\
=&\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\left|\int_{u_{n+1}}^{u_{n}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{u}\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}(\bar{u},\bar{v})\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-(3\Omega^{2}+1)\right)\Psi_{n+1}(\bar{u},\bar{v},\theta^{A})\right|^{2}\\
\lesssim&\frac{1}{r(u_{n},v_{1})}\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\int_{u_{n+1}}^{u_{n}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{u}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r^{2}}|\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\Psi_{n+1}|^{2}\\
\lesssim&\frac{(v_{2}-v_{1})}{r(u_{n},v_{1})}\left[\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}+\frac{(v_{2}-v_{1})^{2}}{r(u_{n},v_{1})^{2}}\|\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}\right],\end{split}

(6.10)

where we used the energy estimate and (
6.7
) in the last line.
This gives an estimate for the
\not{\nabla}_{v}
-derivative part of the energy
E_{u_{n}}[\Psi_{n}-\Psi_{n+1}](v_{1},v_{2})
. The other terms can be estimated similarly (without having to invoke (
RW
)), and thus, by (
6.10
) and (
6.9
), we obtain (we hide
(v_{2}-v_{1})
-weights inside
\lesssim
)

\displaystyle E_{u_{n}}[\Psi_{n}-\Psi_{n+1}](v_{1},v_{2})\lesssim_{v_{2}-v_{1}}\frac{1}{r(u_{n},v_{1})}\sum_{|\gamma|\leq 2}\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{\gamma}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}.

(6.11)

Next, we bound

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi_{n+1}](u_{n+1},u_{n})=E_{u_{n+1}}[\Psi_{n+1}](v_{1},v_{2})-E_{u_{n}}[\Psi_{n+1}](v_{1},v_{2})

(6.12)

by first estimating (notice that the estimate below is still valid for
u_{n}
replaced by any
u^{\prime}\in[u_{n+1},u_{n}]
)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n+1},\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}-|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}

(6.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\Big[|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n+1},\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},\bar{v},\theta^{A})|

\displaystyle\cdot|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n+1},\bar{v},\theta^{A})+\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},\bar{v},\theta^{A})|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n+1},\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}}

\displaystyle\cdot\sqrt{\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n+1},\bar{v},\theta^{A})+\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n+1}(u_{n},\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{v_{2}-v_{1}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{r(u_{n},v_{1})}}\left[\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}+\frac{1}{r(u_{n},v_{1})^{2}}\|\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}\right],

where we used the estimates (
6.7
) and (
6.10
).
We deal with the remaining terms in the difference
E_{u_{n+1}}[\Psi_{n+1}](v_{1},v_{2})-E_{u_{n}}[\Psi_{n+1}](v_{1},v_{2})
similarly, hence we arrive at:

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi_{n+1}](u_{n+1},u_{n})\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{r(u_{n},v_{1})}}\sum_{|\gamma|\leq 2}\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{\gamma}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}.

(6.14)

We now extend
\Psi_{n}
to the region
(-\infty,u_{n}]\times[v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
We could to this by defining
\Psi_{n}
to be the backwards solution to (
RW
) with data
\Psi_{n}|_{\mathcal{C}_{u_{n}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{u_{n}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}
and constant data
\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v
along
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{n}\}
, but then the extension would only be continuous.

Instead, we provide a
smooth
extension of
\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}}
along
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}
:
Let
h(u)
be a smooth cutoff function which cuts off to 0 on
u\geq 1
and is equal to
1
on
u\leq 0
, and let for any
n>0

h_{n}:=h\left(\frac{u-u_{n}}{u_{n-1}-u_{n}}\right)
, so that
h_{n}
cuts off on
u\geq u_{n-1}
and is equal to 1 on
u\leq u_{n}
.
We apply Seeley’s extension theorem
[
See64
]
to extend
\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\cdot h_{n}
to the region
u\leq u_{n}
. Denote this extension by
\mathtt{E}(\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\cdot h_{n})
, in short just
\mathtt{E}
. Note that the extension satisfies
[
See64
]

\displaystyle\|\mathtt{E}(\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\cdot h_{n})\|_{H^{k}((-\infty,u_{n}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\cdot h_{n}\|_{H^{k}([u_{n},\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}

(6.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\|_{H^{k}([u_{n},u_{n-1}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}

for any
k\geq 0
. Let
\tilde{h}_{n}
be the cutoff function given by
\tilde{h}_{n}:=h(u_{n}-u)
, which cuts off on
u\leq u_{n}-1
and is equal to
1
on
u\geq u_{n}
. We have by (
6.15
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|r^{-1}\tilde{h}_{n}\cdot\mathtt{E}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([u_{n}-1,u_{n}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}\leq&\frac{1}{r(u_{n},v_{2})^{2}}\|\tilde{h}_{n}\cdot\mathtt{E}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([u_{n}-1,u_{n}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}\\
\lesssim&\frac{1}{r(u_{n},v_{2})^{2}}\|\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([u_{n},u_{n-1}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}\\
\lesssim&\|r^{-1}\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([u_{n},u_{n-1}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}.\end{split}

(6.16)

In the same way, we have

\displaystyle\|r^{-1}\tilde{h}_{n}\cdot\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathtt{E}\|^{2}_{L^{2}((u_{n}-1,u_{n})\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|r^{-1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([u_{n},u_{n-1}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})},

(6.17)

\displaystyle\|\not{\nabla}_{u}(\tilde{h}_{n}\cdot\mathtt{E})\|^{2}_{L^{2}([u_{n}-1,u_{n}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\not{\nabla}_{u}\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\geq u_{n}\}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([u_{n},u_{n-1}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}

We can thus define data for
\Psi_{n}
on
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{n}\}
via

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{n}\}}:=\tilde{h}_{n}\cdot\mathtt{E}(\Psi_{n}|_{v_{2}}\cdot h_{n}).

(6.18)

Combining, (
6.16
), (
6.17
) and using (
6.13
) shows

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}}](-\infty,u_{n})\lesssim_{v_{2}-v_{1}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r(u_{n},v_{2})}}\left(\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}+\|\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}\right).

(6.19)

The estimates (
6.8
), (
6.11
), (
6.14
), and (
6.19
) lead to

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi_{n+1}-\Psi_{n}](-\infty,u_{0})+E_{u_{0}}[\Psi_{n+1}-\Psi_{n}](v_{1},v_{2})\to 0

(6.20)

as
n\to\infty
, and thus the restrictions
\Psi_{n}|_{\mathcal{C}_{u_{0}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}}
,
\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}\}}
converge with respect to the energy norms
E_{u_{0}}[\cdot](v_{1},v_{2})
,
E_{v_{2}}[\cdot](-\infty,u_{0})
to some limits
\Psi|_{\mathcal{C}_{u_{0}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}}
,
\Psi|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}\}}
.
(Notice that the above estimates carry over also to the commuted energies
\underline{E}^{(n)}_{v_{2}}[\Psi_{n+1}-\Psi_{n}](-\infty,u_{0})+E^{(n)}_{u_{0}}[\Psi_{n+1}-\Psi_{n}](v_{1},v_{2})
.)

Let now
\Psi
be the backwards solution to Regge–Wheeler (
RW
) arising from the limiting data
\Psi|_{\mathcal{C}_{u_{0}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\}}
,
\Psi|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}\cap\{u\leq u_{0}\}}
, then
\Psi
satisfies

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi](-\infty,u_{0})+E_{u_{0}}[\Psi](v_{1},v_{2})<\infty,

(6.21)

and we have that
\Psi|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
. We also have

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}=0

(6.22)

by the following argument: for a given
u
, let
n
be such that
u\in[u_{n},u_{n-1})
. Then we have

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}

(6.23)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n}(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n}(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{v_{2}-v_{1}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{r(u_{n},v_{1})}}\left[\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}+\frac{1}{r(u_{n},v_{1})^{2}}\|\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n}(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}.

In the last line, we used (
6.13
). The convergence (
6.22
) is now implied by the convergence of
\Psi_{n}
to
\Psi
with respect to the energy norms.
The statement (
6.4
) is proved similarly.

Now, assume that
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\in L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})
,
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
such that
\underline{E}_{v_{1}}[\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u_{0})<\infty
.
There exists a sequence
\{\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-},n}\}_{(n\in\mathbb{N})}
of smooth data on
[v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
that approximates
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
in
L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})
and a sequence
\{{\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}_{,n}\}_{(n\in\mathbb{N})}
of smooth, compactly supported data on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
that approximates
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
in the norm given by
E_{v_{1}}[\,\cdot\,](-\infty,u_{0})
.
Let
\Psi_{n}
be the sequence of smooth solutions to Regge–Wheeler (
RW
) arising from data
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-},n}
on
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
,
{\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}_{,n}
on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.
Then
\Psi_{n}|_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}}
converges to
\Psi_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}}
with
\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}}](-\infty,u_{0})<\infty
and
\Psi_{n}|_{{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{0}}}
converges to
\Psi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{0}}}
with
E_{u_{0}}[\Psi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{0}}}](v_{1},v_{2})<\infty
by energy conservation.
Let
\Psi
be the backwards solution to (
RW
) arising from characteristic data
\Psi|_{{\mathcal{C}}_{u_{0}}}
,
\Psi_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}}
. Then we have for any
u

\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}\\
\leq\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n}(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}\\
+\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi_{n}(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-},n}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}\\
+\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-},n}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}.

(6.24)

Choosing
n
large enough ensures that the first and third terms above are small. Choosing
u
large enough and using (
6.22
) then implies

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})-\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}=0,

(6.25)

and the remaining statement that (
6.4
) holds can be proved similarly.

The final claim that the solution is smooth if the data are smooth and if the energies
E_{v_{1}}^{(n)}[\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u_{0})
are finite for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
follows by commuting with angular derivatives and Sobolev embedding (which automatically also proves (
6.5
)).
∎

6.3
Three additional results concerning improved decay and uniqueness

We can directly infer from Thm.
6.1
the following

Proposition 6.2
.

Assume that we are in the second clause of Thm.
6.1
(with smooth data).
Then we have that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{n}\Psi|(u,v)\lesssim r^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i\leq n+2}E^{(i)}_{v_{1}}[\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u)+\|\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{s}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{\frac{i-s}{2}}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}},

(6.26)

where
s=1
if
i
is odd and
0
if
i
is even.
Furthermore, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,

\not{\nabla}_{v}^{m}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{n}\Psi\to\not{\nabla}_{v}^{m-1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{n}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\quad\text{as }u\to-\infty.

(6.27)

uniformly on compact
v
-intervals.

Remark 6.2
.

The convergence (
6.27
) would follow in the same way as (
6.5
) if we also assumed the energy to remain finite for an arbitrary amount of
\not{\nabla}_{T}=\not{\nabla}_{u}+\not{\nabla}_{v}
-commutations. Here, we avoid the use of
\not{\nabla}_{T}
-commutations.

Proof.

The first statement is proved by first estimating, via the fundamental theorem of calculus:

\displaystyle\left|\Psi(u,v,\theta^{A})-\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}(u,\theta^{A})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{v_{1}}^{v}|\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v},

(6.28)

from which it follows via Cauchy–Schwarz that

\left\|\Psi(u,v,\cdot)-\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}(u,\cdot)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}\leq\int_{v_{1}}^{v}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\cdot\int_{v_{1}}^{v}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi|^{2}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\\
\leq(v-v_{1})\cdot E_{u}[\Psi](v_{1},v)\leq(v-v_{1})\cdot\left[E_{v_{1}}[\Psi](-\infty,u)+\lim_{u\to-\infty}E_{u}[\Psi](v_{1},v)\right],

(6.29)

where we also used the energy estimate of Lemma
6.1
in the last estimate.
We similarly estimate the initial data term
\left\|\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}
against its energy by considering the fundamental theorem of calculus for
r^{-1}\Psi
:

\|r^{-1}\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}\lesssim r^{-1}\int_{-\infty}^{u}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|r\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-1}\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})|^{2}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim E_{v_{1}}[\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u).

(6.30)

(The boundary term at
u=-\infty
can be seen to vanish in the same manner.)
Estimate (
6.26
) now follows by commuting and applying the standard Sobolev inequality on the sphere.

Finally, in order to prove the uniform convergence of
\not{\nabla}_{v}^{m}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{n}\Psi
to
\not{\nabla}_{v}^{m-1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{n}\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
along compact intervals in
v
, we invoke the equation (
RW
) itself:
Inserting the pointwise estimate (
6.26
) for
n=0
and integrating (
RW
) in
u
, it follows that
\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi
converges uniformly (on compact
v
-intervals) to a limit as
u\to-\infty
. By (
6.5
), this limit has to agree with
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
. The commuted result follows inductively.
∎

Using the
u
-weighted energy estimate of Lemma
6.2
, we can further show:

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose that
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=0
. Then, provided that
\underline{E}^{\delta^{\prime}}_{v_{1}}[\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u_{0})<\infty
for some
\delta^{\prime}>0
, we have for any
v\geq v_{1}
,
u\leq u_{0}
and for any
0\leq\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\underline{E}^{\delta}_{v}[\Psi](-\infty,u)\leq\underline{E}^{\delta}_{v_{1}}[\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u)

(6.31)

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}E_{u}^{\delta}[\Psi](v_{1},v)=0.

Proof.

In view of (
6.2
), in order to prove the result, it suffices to show that

\lim_{u\to-\infty}E^{\delta}_{u}[\Psi](v_{1},v)

vanishes. To show this, let
\Psi_{N}
be the solution to (
RW
) arising from data
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\cdot\chi_{N}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and trivial data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
(
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=0
). Here, the
\chi_{N}
are translates of cut-offs such that
\chi_{N}=0
for
u\leq-N
.
Apply now (
6.2
) in the region bounded by
\mathcal{I}^{-}
,
u=u_{-\infty}
,
v=v_{1}
, and
v=v_{2}
, for
N>-u_{-\infty}
:

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{u_{-\infty}}[\Psi_{N}](v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\underline{E}^{\delta}_{v_{1}}[\Psi_{N}](-\infty,u_{-\infty})\lesssim\underline{E}^{\delta}_{v_{1}}[\Psi](-\infty,u_{-\infty}).

Since
E^{\delta}_{u_{-\infty}}[\Psi_{N}](v_{1},v_{2})=|u_{-\infty}|^{\delta}E_{u_{-\infty}}[\Psi_{N}](v_{1},v_{2})
, and since
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\cdot\chi_{N}
clearly converges to
\bm{\Uppsi}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
in energy, have

E^{\delta}_{u_{-\infty}}[\Psi](v_{1},v_{2})\lesssim\underline{E}^{\delta}_{v_{1}}[\Psi](-\infty,u_{-\infty}).

Sending
u_{-\infty}\to-\infty
then completes the proof.
∎

We also have the following enhanced uniqueness statement for solutions to (
RW
) of finite energy:

Corollary 6.1
.

Assume
\Psi
is a solution to (
RW
) on
[v_{1},v_{2}]\times(-\infty,u_{0})\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
for
v_{2}>v_{1}
such that
\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi](-\infty,u_{0})<\infty
for some
v_{2}>v_{1}
and
\|\Psi\|_{H^{1}(\mathcal{C}_{u_{0}}\cap\{v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]\})}<\infty
. Assume further that
\Psi|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=0
and that
\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,v,\theta^{A})\to 0
as
u\to-\infty
for
v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]
. Then
\Psi=0
.

Proof.

Since
\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi](-\infty,u_{0})<\infty
, we have that
\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi
converges in
L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})
as
u\to-\infty
by a slight modification of the argument of (
6.23
) in Theorem
6.1
, and so the family
\{\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,v,\theta^{A})\}_{u\in(-\infty,u_{0})}
is uniformly integrable in
L^{2}([v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})
. As
\lim_{u\to-\infty}\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi(u,v,\theta^{A})=0
by assumption, Vitali’s convergence theorem says that

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi|^{2}=0.

(6.32)

Theorem
6.1
now implies
\Psi=0
.
∎

6.4
Two uniqueness results for the Teukolsky equation (
Teuk
)

We now study uniqueness for (
Teuk
).

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\alpha
be a solution to (
Teuk
) on
(u,v,\theta^{A})\in(-\infty,u_{0}]\times[v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that
\underline{E}_{v_{2}}[\Psi](-\infty,u_{0})<\infty
, where
\Psi=\left(r^{2}\Omega^{-2}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\alpha)
. If
\alpha
satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r\alpha(u,v,\theta^{A})=0,\qquad\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\alpha)|_{\mathcal{C}}=0,

(6.33)

then
\alpha=0
.

Proof.

Recall that
\Psi
defined out of
\alpha
via
\Psi=\left(r^{2}\Omega^{-2}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\alpha)
satisfies the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW
) in view of Lemma
3.1
.
Let
\psi=\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\alpha)
.
We import the following estimates for arbitrary
\epsilon>0
from Propositions 12.1.1 and 12.1.2 of
[
DHR19a
]
(adapted to the region near
\mathcal{I}^{-}
):
11
11

11

This estimate derives solely from energy conservation for
\Psi
and the relations (
3.37
) between
\alpha
,
\psi
and
\Psi
. One takes the relation
r^{-2}\Omega^{2}\Psi=\not{\nabla}_{u}\psi
and multiplies it by
r^{-\epsilon}\psi
, integrates by parts in the region
[u,u_{0}]\times[v_{1},v_{2}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
, and the spacetime estimate for
\psi
follows by integrated local energy decay for
\Psi
. The spacetime estimate can then be used to estimate the flux term by a similar procedure. An identical argument applies to
\alpha
once the estimate for
\psi
is obtained.

\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{v}\sin\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi\,\left[|r\Omega^{2}\alpha(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}+|\psi(u,\bar{v},\theta^{A})|^{2}\right]\\
