\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}

Definition 7.10
.

Recall that the kernel of
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}
is spanned by
\ell=0,1
. Define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
to be the unique solutions to (
3.17a
) and (
3.18b
).

\displaystyle-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}})+\Omega^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}},\qquad-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}=\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}})-\Omega^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}.

(7.67)

Corollary 7.9
.

The one-forms
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
satisfy (
3.20a
), (
3.19b
) and (
3.24
). Moreover, we have
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
as well as

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},

(7.68)

where
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
is defined in Def.
7.2
. This convergence is uniform on compact
v
-intervals and commutes with
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}
and
\not{\nabla}_{v}
-derivatives.

Proof.

We prove (
3.19b
) by multiplying the first of (
7.67
) (i.e. (
3.17a
)) with
\Omega^{-2}r
and then acting with
\not{\nabla}_{v}
, using (
3.17b
), (
3.32
) as well as the second of (
7.67
) (i.e. (
3.18b
)).

The proof of (
3.20a
) is analogous.

In order to prove (
3.24
), we take the
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,
of (
3.17a
). From there, using (
7.32
), (
7.50
) and Corollary
7.8
, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\left(r^{2}\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}\right)=\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\right)\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{r}\left(r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}+\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}\right).

(7.69)

We then utilise (
3.30a
) and (
7.33
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\right)\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\right)r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}},

(7.70)

which implies
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}
since
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2
is invertible on
\ell\geq 2
.

The claim for
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
follows in the same way, using equations (
3.18b
), (
7.50
), (
3.30b
), Lemma
7.8
and Corollary
7.8
.

The claim that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}
restricts to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
follows from the definition, the analogous statements for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
as well the fact that the first of (
7.67
) is satisfied along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by Prop.
5.1
.
Furthermore, it is easy to see that
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}
tends to 0 as
u\to-\infty
.

The uniform convergence of
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
follows from its definition and the uniform convergence of
\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{S}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}
to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{S}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
, respectively. By integrating (
3.20a
), we then infer that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
.
∎

In particular, since we have now shown that
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}})=0
, the following definition is well-defined:

Definition 7.11
.

Define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
via
2\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)=r(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}})
, and define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}=\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}=\partial_{u}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)
.

Corollary 7.10
.

Equations (
3.19a
) and (
3.20b
) are satisfied.
Moreover,
\lim_{u\to-\infty}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}=\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
(this convergence being uniform on compact
v
-intervals and commuting with
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}^{n}
and
\not{\nabla}_{v}^{m}
), and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
.

Proof.

Equation (
3.19a
) follows directly from (
3.20a
), and the definition of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
. Equation (
3.20b
) follows similarly.
The convergence of
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
to
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
follows from the limiting behaviour of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
, and the convergence of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
to
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
then follows straight-forwardly (using that we are supported on
\ell\geq 2
).
The restriction of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
to
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
follows from
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
restricting to
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
by definition and Prop.
5.1
.
∎

Step
(X)
: Constructing
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
.

Definition 7.12
.

We define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
according to (
3.22
) and (
3.23
), i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\Omega}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}=\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}+\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}+r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\Omega}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}=\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}-\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}.

(7.71)

By construction and Prop.
5.1
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
restrict to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, respectively.

Remark 7.3
.

These definitions are well-defined since we already know that the
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,
of the respective RHS’s vanish by (
7.32
), (
7.50
) and
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}=r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}^{\prime}=-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
.

Corollary 7.11
.

The quantities
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
defined above satisfy equations (
3.16
), and
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}=0
,
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
, these convergences being uniform on compact
v
-intervals and commuting with
\not{\nabla}_{v}
- and
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}
-derivatives.

Proof.

Equation (
3.16a
) follows by acting on the first of (
7.71
) with
\not{\nabla}_{u}(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega}\cdot)
and using (
3.17a
), (
3.20a
) as well as (
3.31a
).
Equation (
3.16b
) follows analogously.

The limiting behaviour follows from the definitions and the previous estimates.
∎

Step
(XI)
: Constructing
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}
and proving all equations featuring
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}

Before we can define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}
, we need to prove the following

Lemma 7.9
.

We have

\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}=2\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,-r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})+6M\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}-r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}).

(7.72)

Proof.

First, note that this equation holds along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by construction (cf. Proposition
5.1
and Corollary
5.2
).
Next, we confirm that
\not{\nabla}_{v}\eqref{eq:construct:pedanticsrho}
holds by computing the
\not{\nabla}_{v}
-derivative of the LHS (
7.72
) using the definition of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
and the Teukolsky equation (
Teuk
) (the result is (
3.39
)), and using (
3.30b
), (
3.17b
) and (
3.18b
) for the RHS.
∎

The above lemma enables the following definition:

Definition 7.13
.

Define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}
to be the unique solution to

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\left(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},-r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}\right)+6M\left(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}-r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}\right).

(7.73)

By construction and by Prop.
5.1
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
.

Remark 7.4
.

Definition
7.13
implies, via (
3.38
):

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\left(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}\right)+6M\left(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}-r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}\right).

(7.74)

Lemma 7.10
.

The quantity
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}
defined above satisfies equations (
3.31a
), (
3.28b
) as well as (
3.29
).

Proof.

We first prove (
3.31a
): We take the definition (
7.73
), and rewrite its LHS as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}=(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})
. We now rewrite this as the
\tfrac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}
-derivative of the RHS of (
3.33
). Equation
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\eqref{eq:lin:be3}
then follows by taking into account the already established equation (
3.17a
), and the result follows from the invertibility of
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}
.

Equation (
3.28b
) follows similarly, starting from (
7.74
).

Next, we prove (
3.29b
).
Using (
3.31b
) and (
3.32
), we compute:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}\right)=-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\,(r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}})+6M\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}-2(3\Omega^{2}-2)r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}.

(7.75)

Therefore, by (
3.31a
) and (
3.19b
) we have

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\partial_{v}\left(-r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}\right)=\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+1\right)r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}+6M\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}-2(3\Omega^{2}-2)\,r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}+6M\left(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}-\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}\right).

(7.76)

Taking the divergence of both sides and using
\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+1\right)\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}=\left(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2\right)\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}
and equation (
3.22
), we get

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\partial_{v}\,(r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho})=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}}+3M\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}.

(7.77)

This shows that (
3.29b
) is satisfied by the invertibility of
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}
on
\ell\geq 2
.

The argument to prove (
3.29a
) is analogous.
∎

Lemma 7.11
.

The equations (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) are satisfied by
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}
.

Proof.

Let’s first prove (
3.14
):
We differentiate the definition of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}
, (
3.22
):

\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)})=\not{\nabla}_{u}(\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}})+\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}})+\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{2}\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\beta}})\\
=-2\Omega^{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}-\Omega^{3}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}+\not{\nabla}_{u}(\Omega^{2})r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}+r\Omega^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}-\Omega^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(-r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho},r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})-\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}},

(7.78)

where we used (
3.17a
), (
3.20a
) and (
3.31a
).
We now use the fact that

-2\Omega^{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=-\Omega^{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+2\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}=\Omega^{2}(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}-{}^{\ast}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}+2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}})=\Omega^{2}(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}-{}^{\ast}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}+2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}),

as well as equations (
3.12
) and (
3.23
) to conclude that
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\eqref{eq:lin:trx3}
holds.
Eq. (
3.15
) follows similarly.
∎

Lemma 7.12
.

The equations (
3.21
) are satisfied by
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
, respectively.

Proof.

We compute
\not{\nabla}_{u}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}=\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)=\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\omega}}
by using (
3.12
), (
3.20a
), (
3.19b
) to compute
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\partial_{v}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)
, and then using (
3.28b
), (
3.31a
), (
3.20a
) and (
3.19b
) to compute
\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\left(\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}{\Omega}\right)
.
∎

Step
(XII)
: Constructing the remaining metric components
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
,
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}

Definition 7.14
.

Define
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
to be the solution to
\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b})=2r^{-1}\Omega^{2}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}})
(i.e. (
3.13
)) with data
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
for
r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

Definition 7.15
.

Define
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
as solutions to (
3.9b
) and (
3.10b
) with
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
as data along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Lemma 7.13
.

The equations (
3.9a
) and (
3.10a
) are satisfied by
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
.

Proof.

We present the proof for (
3.9a
); the proof for (
3.10a
) is similar.
Since (
3.9a
) is satisfied along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, it suffices to prove that
\partial_{v}\eqref{eq:lin:trg3}
holds. We prove the latter by computing
\partial_{v}\partial_{u}{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}=\partial_{u}\partial_{v}{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
via (
3.14
) and (
3.13
), and by computing
\partial_{v}(2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)})
via (
3.15
). The result then follows.
∎

Corollary 7.12
.

The following convergence is uniform in
v
on compact
v
-intervals:

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}=\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}.

(7.79)

These limits commute with
\not{\nabla}_{v}
-and
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}
-derivatives.

From the definition of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
and the decay rates of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}
, it is easy to see that
r^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
converges uniformly in
v
to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}
as
u\to-\infty
, and it thus follows that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}
,
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
as well as
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}
tend to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
,
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
, respectively.

The final equation of (
3.9
)–(
3.32
) that we have to prove is the Gauss equation:

Lemma 7.14
.

The Gauss equation (
3.25
) is satisfied, with
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}
defined in (
3.26
).

Proof.

We compute the
\partial_{v}
-derivative of the Gauss equation using (
3.9b
), (
3.10b
), (
3.29b
), (
3.16b
), (
3.15
) and (
3.11b
). This shows that
\partial_{v}\eqref{eq:lin:K}
is satisfied. The result now follows, as (
3.25
) is satisfied along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
by Prop
5.1
.
∎

We have now constructed a solution to (
3.9
)–(
3.32
), and we have shown that it satisfies Definition
5.2
. This concludes the proof of Prop.
7.2
.
∎

7.3
Construction of the
\ell<2
-part of the solution to the scattering problem

We now present the construction of the
\ell<2
-part of the solution:
This construction will be entirely explicit:

Proposition 7.4
.

Given a smooth seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=0}
supported on
\ell=0
, let
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
be as in Prop.
5.1
(see (
5.6
)), and let
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
be as in Cor.
5.3
.
Then
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{S}_{f}+\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}
is a scattering solution realising
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=0}
, with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{M}+3\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}

(7.80)

\displaystyle\underline{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{6M\Omega^{2}}(-r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}-2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}+6M\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\int_{v_{1}}^{v}2(\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}+\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}+\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{4M})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}.

The decomposition
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{S}_{f}+\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}
is unique up to the ambiguity addressed in Remark
4.1
.

Proof.

It is easy to directly verify that this is a scattering solution realising
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=0}
.
Still, we find it insightful to provide the construction:
We begin by realising that only the ingoing gauge solution
\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}
and the nearby Schwarzschild solution
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}
have non-trivial limits for
r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
. If we write
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{-1}\underline{f}=\underline{f}_{0}
, we then find the system of equations

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=-6M\underline{f}_{0}-2M\mathfrak{m},

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=2\underline{f}_{0}+\mathfrak{m},

(7.81)

which is solved by
\mathfrak{m}=\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{M}+3\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
,
\underline{f}_{0}=-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}-\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{2M}
.
We have thus already determined
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Next, we fully fix
\underline{f}
by demanding that

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{4}}\underline{f}-\frac{2M\mathfrak{m}}{r^{3}}=-\frac{6M\Omega^{2}}{r^{4}}\underline{f}-\frac{2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{r^{3}}-\frac{6M\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{r^{3}}.

(7.82)

Finally, we fix
f
by noticing that both
\mathfrak{m}
and
\underline{f}
generate a limit of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, by demanding that
f|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=0
and by

\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{v}f+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}+\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{2M}\right)-\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{2M}-\frac{3\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{2}.

(7.83)

It is left to show that this is indeed a scattering solution realising
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=0}
:
By Corollary
5.3
, it suffices to show that the constructed solution realises
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
,
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
,
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
:
Now, by construction, the solution
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{S}_{f}+\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}
realises
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
as well as
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
. Moreover, since it realises
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, we can deduce from (
3.29a
) that it also realises
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
(and thus
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
) by Prop.
5.1
.
Finally, we deduce from (
3.16a
) that
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{S}_{f}+\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}
also realises
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
.
∎

Proposition 7.5
.

Given a smooth seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=1}
supported on
\ell=1
, let
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
be as in Prop.
5.1
, and let
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
be as in Cor.
5.3
.
Then
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{a}}+\mathfrak{S}_{f}+\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}+\mathfrak{S}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
is a scattering solution realising
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=1}
for

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{m}

\displaystyle=-3M\sqrt{2}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})^{\mathrm{H},1}_{\ell=1,m},

(7.84)

\displaystyle(q_{1},q_{2})_{\ell=1,m}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{3}{2M}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}-\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}\right),0\right))_{\ell=1,m}+\int_{v_{1}}^{v}\sqrt{2}((\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}})^{\mathrm{E},1}_{1,m},(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}})^{\mathrm{H},1}_{1,m})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v^{\prime},

\displaystyle f_{\ell=1,m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6M\sqrt{2}}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})^{\mathrm{E},1}_{\ell=1,m}+\int_{v_{1}}^{v}2\left(\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}-\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{6M}\right)_{\ell=1,m}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v^{\prime},

\displaystyle\underline{f}_{\ell=1,m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{r^{4}}{6M\Omega^{2}}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})_{\ell=1,m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\cdot 6M}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})^{\mathrm{E},1}_{\ell=1,m}.

Proof.

We uniquely determine
\mathfrak{a}
directly from Prop.
4.2
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
. (Note that none of the pure gauge solutions affect the magnetic part of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}
.)

Now, we choose a preliminary definition of
\underline{f}_{p}
by demanding that
\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}_{p}}
restricts to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
:

\underline{f}_{p}=-\frac{r^{4}}{6M\Omega^{2}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}+\tilde{\underline{f}}(\theta^{A}).

(7.85)

We will determine
\tilde{\underline{f}}(\theta^{A})
later.
This choice for the ingoing gauge function induces a limit for
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. This limit is independent of
\tilde{\underline{f}}(\theta^{A})
. Since the outgoing gauge function always induces a vanishing limit for
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, we can thus fix
q_{1}(v=v_{1},\theta^{A})
by demanding that
\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}_{p}}+\mathfrak{S}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
restrict to
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
:

\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=\frac{3}{2M}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}-4q_{1}(v=v_{1},\theta^{A}).

(7.86)

We now fully fix
(q_{1},q_{2})
by demanding that
q_{2}(v=v_{1},\theta_{A})=0
and that
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\partial_{v}q_{1},\partial_{v}q_{2})=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
.

Next, we fix
\partial_{v}f
by demanding that the limit
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
is met:

\frac{\partial_{v}f}{2}=\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}-\frac{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}}{12M},

(7.87)

where the second term comes from
\underline{f}_{p}
.
We fully fix
f
by demanding that the limit
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
is attained, i.e.:

f_{\ell=1,m}(v=v_{1})=-\frac{1}{6M\sqrt{2}}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})^{\mathrm{E},1}_{\ell=1,m}.

(7.88)

Finally, we fix
\tilde{\underline{f}}
by demanding that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
is attained.

It is left to show that the constructed solution realises
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell=1}
. By construction,
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{a}}+\mathfrak{S}_{f}+\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}}+\mathfrak{S}_{(q_{1},q_{2})}
realises
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
as limits at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, and it realises
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
,
\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
as well as
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
. Moreover, in view of
\not{\nabla}_{u}\frac{r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}_{\ell=1}}{\Omega}=0
, it follows that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
are also attained by (
3.29a
) and (
3.16a
). The result now follows from Corollary
5.3
(as in the case
\ell=0
).
∎

8
The physical scattering data

The previous sections featured a general discussion of how to set up and solve the scattering problem for a general class of seed scattering data.

In the present section, we specify this discussion to physically motivated seed data.
We will first define the condition of no incoming radiation, and then give and physically justify various definition of seed scattering data describing the far-region of a system of
N
infalling bodies, and finally collect a few basic consequences arising from these definitions that will be useful for the analysis in the later parts of this paper.

8.1
The condition of no incoming radiation

We first define the condition of no incoming radiation from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
(this definition is stated without any prior gauge fixing!):

Definition 8.1
.

A set of smooth seed scattering data
\mathfrak{D}
is said to satisfy the
no incoming radiation condition
if the following condition is satisfied:

\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},

(8.1)

where
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
is defined in (
5.6
). This is equivalent to demanding that

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f_{x},0),

\displaystyle(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2)f_{x}=2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}

(8.2)

for some
f_{x}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}})
with
\partial_{v}f_{x}=0
.

The property of a seed scattering data set to have no incoming radiation cannot be changed by addition of a pure gauge solution (Prop.
4.3
–Prop.
4.5
). We are therefore justified in calling it a
gauge invariant condition
.

Conversely, we recall from §
5.4
that the other quantities of
\mathfrak{D}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{b}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
and
\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
, can be set to 0 by addition of pure gauge solutions.
Since
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
can also be set to zero, the no incoming radiation condition therefore
completely eliminates the radiative, physical degrees of freedom at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

Note that, in particular, if
\mathfrak{D}
is as in Thm.
5.1
and satisfies the no incoming radiation condition, then all the radiation fields defined in Def.
7.1
vanish. For later reference, we highlight that this in particular implies that
r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
,
\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
and
\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}=\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{2}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})
all attain vanishing limits at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. Note that the no incoming radiation condition is strictly stronger than demanding that the limit of
\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}
vanishes.

Remark 8.1
.

The definition of no incoming radiation here is written down in such a way as to be gauge invariant.
Most readers will be more familiar with a version of the no incoming radiation condition in the nonlinear theory where a gauge has already been fixed:
provided one works in a gauge where the spheres at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
are round (i.e.
\bm{r}^{2}\bm{K}\to 1
as
\bm{u}\to-\infty
), the no incoming radiation condition is captured by the requirement that the Bondi mass along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
be constant. In view of the mass growth formula (this is simply the time reversal of the Bondi mass loss formula
[
BVdBM62
,
Sac62b
,
CK93
]
),

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{M}_{\mathrm{Bondi}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{v}}=\lim_{\bm{u}\to-\infty}\frac{1}{{16\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\bm{r}\hat{\bm{\chi}}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\Omega},

(8.3)

this clearly requires
\bm{r}\hat{\bm{\chi}}=0
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.
This is exactly what our condition restricts to in the case of Bondi-normalised data (which have
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=0
).

Remark 8.2
.

Clearly, it would not be enough to replace the condition of no incoming radiation (
8.1
) with a weaker condition such as demanding that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=0
along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, as this would be consistent with a constant
{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
at past null infinity, which would correspond to a constant growth in energy.
However, it is interesting to note that condition (
8.1
) is, in fact, equivalent to demanding that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=0
and
\lim_{u\to-\infty}\frac{r^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})=0
, as can be seen from (
3.32
) and Definition
7.1
. In other words, the no incoming radiation condition forces
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=o(r^{-2})
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.
The additional requirement of finite Regge–Wheeler energy then means that we must have
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=o(r^{-5/2})
.

8.2
Seed data describing the far-field region of the hyperbolic
N
-body problem

The link to the physical scenario of
N
infalling masses (with negligible internal structure, i.e. point masses) moving along approximately hyperbolic orbits in the infinite past is made by introducing Post-Newtonian approximations to the Post-Minkowskian multipolar expansion framework; this has been described in detail in §2 of
[
Keh24
]
.
In short, the argument in the Post-Minkowskian setting consists of writing down a general expression of an outgoing vacuum solution to the Einstein equations, which takes the following form for the curvature components of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
:
12
12

12

Note that this is exactly analogous to the statement that the general fixed-angular frequency solution
\phi_{\ell}
to the Minkowskian wave equation
\Box_{\eta}\phi=0
takes the form

\phi_{\ell}=r^{\ell}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\partial_{u}^{\ell}\left(\frac{f_{\ell,m}(u)Y_{\ell,m}}{r^{\ell+1}}\right)+\partial_{v}^{\ell}\left(\frac{\bar{f}_{\ell,m}(v)Y_{\ell,m}}{r^{\ell+1}}\right),

(8.4)

with the
\partial_{v}
-part of the solution vanishing because it is demanded that the solution be of purely outgoing type.

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\ell+2)!}{(\ell-2)!}r^{\ell-2}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\not{\nabla}_{u}^{\ell-2}\left(\frac{I_{\ell,m}(u)Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},2}-S_{\ell,m}(u)Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},2}}{r^{3+\ell}}\right),

(8.5)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=r^{\ell-2}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\not{\nabla}_{u}^{\ell+2}\left(\frac{I_{\ell,m}(u)Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},2}+S_{\ell,m}(u)Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},2}}{r^{\ell-1}}\right).

(8.6)

for some real-valued functions
I_{\ell,m}
,
S_{\ell,m}
depending only on
u
.
Notice that (
8.5
) and (
8.6
) are exactly related via the Teukolsky–Starobinsky identity (
3.43
) with
M=0
.

These expressions, which are valid in the vacuum region of spacetime, are then matched to expressions for the matter region of spacetime (i.e. the region of the
N
bodies), which, in turn, are derived under a number of approximations and matching to the Newtonian theory.
The result is that the
I_{\ell,m}
acquire the interpretation of the
\ell
-th Newtonian mass multipole moment, and the
S_{\ell,m}
acquire the interpretation of the
\ell
-th Newtonian current multipole moment.
Finally, these moments are computed using the Newtonian theory (or perturbations thereof). Since, schematically, the
\ell
-th mass multipole moment goes like
m\cdot r^{\ell}
, where
m
and
r
denote mass and size of the system, and since, for a system of masses following hyperbolic orbits, the size grows linearly in time, we thus find that
I_{\ell,m}(u)\sim|u|^{\ell}
as
u\to-\infty
.
The same rate is found for
S_{\ell,m}
, and it can thus be seen that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}
, within this framework, is predicted to decay like
|u|^{2}/r^{5}\sim r^{-3}
near past null infinity.
Similarly, one finds that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}
decays like
r^{-4}
near past null infinity, and that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell}
decays like
r^{-2}
near past null infinity.

On the other hand, for
parabolic orbits
, for which the size grows like
r^{2/3}
, we find that
I_{\ell,m}(u)\sim|u|^{2\ell/3}
. Thus, in the case of parabolic orbits, we have that both
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell=2}^{\mathrm{E}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell=2}^{\mathrm{E}}
decay like
|u|^{-11/3}
towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, with all other angular modes decaying faster. We refer the reader to §2 of
[
Keh24
]
for details.

We will now assume that this general information obtained from a perturbative framework around Minkowski is valid up until some finite advanced time, and we implement it in the context of linearised gravity around Schwarzschild in the following way, focusing first on the more important case of hyperbolic orbits.

Definition 8.2
.

A seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}
satisfying the no incoming radiation condition is said to
describe the far-field region of a system of
N
infalling masses following approximately hyperbolic Keplerian orbits
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(I)

There exists
\delta>0
and some
\underline{\mathscr{B}}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}_{stf}({\underline{\mathcal{C}}}))
with
\not{\nabla}_{u}\underline{\mathscr{B}}=0
(and supported on all angular modes) such that

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-6\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-4}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-4-\delta}).

(8.7)

(II)

The limit
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}
exists and is non-vanishing (and is supported on all angular modes).

(III)

The limit
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{A}
exists and is non-vanishing (and is supported on all angular modes).

Here, the quantities
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
are as defined in Prop.
5.1
.

Remark 8.3
.

The factor
-6
is introduced for later notational convenience.
Condition
(III)
already partially follows from the previous conditions: The no incoming radiation condition implies that
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=0=\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, and condition
8.7
implies that the limits
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}})=(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})
exist. By condition
(II)
, and equations (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), we moreover have that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}\neq 0
.
It then follows by construction ((
3.32
) and (
3.31a
)) that
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})
.
Thus, condition
(III)
only adds the additional requirement that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}\neq 0
.

8.3
Seed data describing the far-field region of the parabolic
N
-body problem

Even though the seed data of Def.
8.2
, corresponding to hyperbolic orbits, will be the protagonist of this work, we still, for the sake of completeness, provide an analogous definition for the case of parabolic orbits:

Definition 8.3
.

A seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}
satisfying the no incoming radiation condition is said to
describe the far-field region of a system of
N
infalling masses following approximately parabolic Keplerian orbits
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(I)

There exists some
\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,2)}_{stf}({\underline{\mathcal{C}}}))
with
\not{\nabla}_{u}\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}=0
(and supported only on electric
\ell=2
angular modes) such that

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}r^{-11/3}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-4}).

(8.8)

(II)

The limits
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
vanish.

Here, the quantities
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
are as defined in Prop.
5.1
.

8.4
Seed data describing the massless
N
-body problem

Finally, we turn our attention to the case of massless

?
graviton
?
scattering. We can model this by simply posing compactly supported data for the linearised gravitational field on
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. The reader may want to think of these data as being localised around
N
different points on along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.
In our context, we can simply model compactly supported radiation along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
by posing trivial data along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, and then posing compactly supported data for the incoming radiation measured by

\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}-\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}

(8.9)

along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, cf. Def.
8.1
.
For simplicity, we assume our data to be Bondi-normalised, so that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\mathtt{K}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=0
and incoming radiation is measured solely by
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
.
Using the results of the previous section, it is then straight-forward to show the following

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a Bondi-normalised smooth seed scattering data set with trivial data on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and compactly supported data for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
such that there exists some
v_{2}
such that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(v)=0
for all
v\geq v_{2}
.
Then the corresponding solution satisfies along
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}
the following:

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v,

(8.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to-\infty}r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{v_{1}}^{v}\int_{v_{1}}^{v^{\prime}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)\overline{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v^{\prime\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v,

(8.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to-\infty}r^{4}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\int_{v_{1}}^{v}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)\overline{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v,

(8.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to-\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v.

(8.13)

Furthermore,
r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
admits an asymptotic expansion in powers of
1/r
towards
\mathcal{I}^{-}
along
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{v_{2}}
.

We thus make the following definition:

Definition 8.4
.

A Bondi-normalised seed scattering data set
\mathfrak{D}
satisfying the no incoming radiation condition is said to describe the exterior of a compactly supported gravitational perturbation along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
if it arises from compactly supported
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
and trivial data on a null cone to the past of the support of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}
as in Proposition
8.1
. In particular, the rates of Prop.
8.1
hold for such data.

Remark 8.4
.

In particular, a seed scattering data set as in Def.
8.4
is like a seed scattering data set as in Def.
8.2
for which
\underline{\mathscr{B}}
vanishes!
As we will see later on (and as was already discussed in the introduction), this implies that solutions arising from either definition will exhibit essentially the same asymptotic behaviour towards and along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

Remark 8.5
.

We also highlight that upon comparing the expression (
8.13
) with the Post-Newtonian prediction for infalling masses (
8.5
), we could view the integral on the RHS of (
8.13
), when restricted to fixed
\ell
-modes, as the massless and relativistic analogue of the Newtonian multipole moments.

8.5
A preliminary description of solutions arising from seed data describing the far-field region of the hyperbolic
N
-body problem

We now offer a preliminary description of solutions arising from seed data as in the previous definitions. We will give the most detailed description for the case of hyperbolic orbits, as this is more general than parabolic orbits and also includes the case of Def.
8.4
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a smooth seed scattering data set as in Def.
8.2
. By Prop.
5.2
, we can without loss of generality assume it to be Bondi-normalised in the sense of Def.
5.3
. Moreover, by Cor.
5.4
, we can additionally assume that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=0
and that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}=0
.

By Theorem
5.1
, there exists a unique scattering solution
\mathfrak{S}
realising
\mathfrak{D}
.
The data along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
induced by
\mathfrak{S}
satisfy:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=-6\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-4}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-4-\delta}),

(8.14)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\underline{\upbeta}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=-6\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-4}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{1}r^{-4}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-4-\delta}),

(8.15)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}r^{-3}+6\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-4}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{2}r^{-4}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-4-\delta}),

(8.16)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}r^{-3}+6\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-4}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{3}r^{-4}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-4-\delta}),

(8.17)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upbeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})r^{-3}-3\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-4}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{4}r^{-4}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-4-\delta}),

(8.18)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{A}r^{-3}+2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-4}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{5}r^{-4}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-4-\delta}),

(8.19)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=2\mathscr{A}+12\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-1}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{6}r^{-1}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-1-\delta}),

(8.20)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=2\overline{\mathscr{A}}+12\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-1}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{7}r^{-1}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-1-\delta}),

(8.21)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=-12\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-2}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{8}r^{-2}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-2-\delta}),

(8.22)

where we recall the notation
\overline{\mathscr{A}}
to denote the magnetic conjugate (cf. Def.
2.4
), where
\not{\nabla}_{u}\mathscr{C}_{n}=0
for
n=1,\dots,11
—the precise value of these constants will not play a role in this paper—and where

\mathscr{A}=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})=\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},-\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}).

(8.23)

Similarly, we have for the connection coefficients:

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\underline{\upchi}\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=0=\mathrm{tr}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{\mathtt{g}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upomega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathtt{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\Omega}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}},

(8.24)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}r^{-2}+6\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-3}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-3-\delta}),

(8.25)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}r^{-2}+6\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-3}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{8}r^{-3}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-3-\delta}),

(8.26)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=(-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}-1)\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}r^{-2}-6\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-3}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{9}r^{-3}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-3-\delta}),

(8.27)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\mathtt{\Omega}\mathrm{tr}\upchi\right)}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=(2\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}+2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}})r^{-2}+12\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-3}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{10}r^{-3}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-3-\delta}),

(8.28)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\upomega}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle=-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}r^{-2}-3\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathscr{A}r^{-3}\log r+\mathscr{C}_{11}r^{-3}+\mathcal{O}_{\infty}(r^{-3-\delta}).

(8.29)

On the other hand, all radiation fields defined in Def.
7.1
,
7.2
vanish, and we additionally have the following limits at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upsigma}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}},

\displaystyle\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\underline{\upchi}}}}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}.

(8.30)

Proof.

Equations (
8.14
)–(
8.19
) follow from Prop.
5.1
and by consecutively integrating (
3.28a
)–(
3.32
).
Equations (
8.20
)–(
8.22
) follow from Cor.
5.2
.

The last equality in (
8.23
) follows from (
3.24
) and (
3.23
).

Equations (
8.24
)–(
8.29
) also follow from Prop.
5.1
and by consecutive integration: Equation (
8.24
) follows from (
3.16a
) and (
3.9a
), equation (
8.26
) follows from (
3.22
), equation (
8.27
) follows from (
3.17a
), and so on.

Finally, (
8.30
) follows from inspection of the proof of Theorem
5.1
. (Consider the
\not{\nabla}_{v}
-derivatives of the
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\rho}
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}
via (
3.29b
), (
3.30b
) and (
3.18b
). It follows from the proof of the theorem that these converge uniformly to some limit at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, which, by virtue of all limits in Definitions
7.1
and
7.2
vanishing, vanish as well.)
∎

We can also prove a result analogous to that of Prop.
8.2
for the case of parabolic orbits. We leave the details to the reader and content ourselves with only stating the following:

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a smooth scattering data set as in Def.
8.3
. Then the corresponding
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
satisfies:

\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\frac{81}{80}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par}}r^{-11/3}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-4}).

(8.31)

Similarly, we evidently have that Prop.
8.2
applies to the data of Def.
8.4
when setting
\underline{\mathscr{B}}=0
.

Part II:

Asymptotic analysis of the Teukolsky equations and the Regge–Wheeler equations

In the previous part of this paper, we carefully set up the scattering problem for linearised gravity around Schwarzschild, with scattering data posed on an ingoing null cone
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\mathcal{I}^{-}
, see Fig.
7
, and we showed in §§
5
–
7
how to construct the corresponding scattering solution in the domain of dependence
\mathcal{D}:=D^{+}({\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}})
.
Finally, in §
8
, we introduced three notions of scattering data describing the exterior of a system of
N
infalling bodies—we will henceforth refer to these data as

?
the physical data
?
—and gave a preliminary characterisation of the corresponding scattering solutions.

We will now set the foundations for finding the asymptotic properties in all of
\mathcal{D}
for general scattering solutions, and we will apply this in particular to the scattering solutions arising from the physical data of §
8
.
The key to this is an asymptotic analysis of the Teukolsky equations and the Regge–Wheeler equation (introduced in §
3.3
)—this is the content of the present part of the paper.
Since both the Teukolsky and the Regge–Wheeler equations decouple from the rest of the system of equations (
3.9
)–(
3.32
), this part of the paper will have to make very little reference to the previous part of the paper, and it can essentially be viewed as independent work on the Teukolsky equations on Schwarzschild.
In fact, we will present the results of this part of the paper so as to be valid for the Teukolsky equations of any spin.

Overview of the structure of this part of the paper:

In §
9
, we take the Teukolsky equations (
Teuk
), (
Teuk
), re-write them in a unified way as equations for Teukolsky quantities
\alpha^{[s]}
of general spin
s
(
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
corresponding to
s=+2
,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
corresponding to
s=-2
), and derive from them an infinite set of approximate/asymptotic conservation laws satisfied by angular modes of
\alpha^{[s]}
. We deduce similar conservation laws for the Regge–Wheeler equation and provide definitions of the generalised Newman–Penrose charges
\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[s]}]
(cf.
[
MZ22b
,
GK22
]
).

In the remaining sections, we then consider a scattering setup for the Teukolsky equation for
\alpha^{[s]}
with scattering data on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
, and we use the aforementioned conservation laws to derive asymptotic expressions for angular modes of the resulting scattering solutions:

First, in §
10
, we prescribe
\alpha^{[s]}
to decay with some general polynomial decay along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, and we prescribe a condition along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
that, in the case
s\geq 0
is the no incoming radiation condition, but, in the case
s<0
, is slightly stronger than the no incoming radiation condition of Def.
8.1
.
This will make it easier for us to later highlight the somewhat different behaviour of
\alpha^{[s]}
for negative
s
. Under this setup, we prove a result for the general asymptotic behaviour throughout
\mathcal{D}
of fixed angular modes of
\alpha^{[s]}
.

In §
11
, we then apply these results to
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
(
s=+2
) with the data that we have physically motivated in §
8
, e.g. with
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
decaying as in (
8.19
). Due to the presence of the logarithmic term in the decay (
8.19
), this requires a very minor modification of the result of §
10
.

We can similarly apply the results of §
10
to deduce the asymptotic behaviour of solutions to the Regge–Wheeler equation arising from physical initial data ((
8.20
) and (
8.21
)). This is done in §
12
.

In §
13
, we then apply the results of §
10
in the case
s=-2
to find the asymptotics of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
for the physical data of §
8
(eq. (
8.14
)). Again, a minor modification is necessary to account for the fact that our assumption in §
10
is slightly stronger than the no incoming radiation condition.
Furthermore, we will find that, for the special data of Def.
8.2
, a cancellation occurs, and the leading-order asymptotic results of §
10
are no longer sufficient: To be precise, the leading-order results of §
10
only imply that
\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}=C\cdot|u|^{-2}+...
, but this constant
C
is found to be vanishing.

We find the next-to leading-order decay of
\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
by introducing a decomposition of the Teukolsky equation that is similar to a Post–Minkowskian expansion and which allows us to find the next-to leading-order asymptotics of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
by solving a few recurrence relations. This is the content of §
14
.

Finally, with all the results of the previous sections having been obtained for angular modes of
\alpha^{[s]}
, we comment (but do not yet resolve) in §
15
on the issue of summing these fixed angular frequency estimates obtained in the previous sections. The resolution of this problem is the subject of upcoming work
[
KK24
]
.

9
The approximate conservation laws

In this section, we will derive an infinite set of
approximate
conservation laws (which imply exact
asymptotic
conservation laws, cf.
[
Keh24
]
) for
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
starting from the Teukolsky equations (
Teuk
), (
Teuk
).
Let us first fix some notation: Since
\Omega
only appears with even integer powers in this part of the paper, we denote

D:=\Omega^{2}=1-\frac{2M}{r}.

(9.1)

Recall that
\partial_{v}r=D=-\partial_{u}r
.

9.1
The Teukolsky equations for general spin
s
satisfied by
\alpha^{[s]}

We begin by massaging the equation (
Teuk
) into a form that is more convenient for our purposes:
First, we rewrite

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{v}(r^{4}D^{-2}\not{\nabla}_{u}(rD\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{v}(\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}D^{-1}))-\not{\nabla}_{v}(D^{2}r^{-4}\partial_{u}(r^{4}D^{-2})(r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}D^{-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{u}(\not{\nabla}_{v}(r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}D^{-1}))-\partial_{v}\partial_{u}\log(r^{4}D^{-2})(r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}D^{-1})-\partial_{u}\log(D^{-2}r^{4})\not{\nabla}_{v}(r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}D^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r^{4}}{D^{2}}\not{\nabla}_{u}(\frac{D^{2}}{r^{4}}\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}}{D}))-\partial_{v}\partial_{u}\log(r^{4}D^{-2})\frac{r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}}{D}

We further have

\partial_{v}\partial_{u}\log(D^{-2}r^{4})=\frac{4D}{r^{2}}-\frac{24MD}{r^{3}}.

Using the two equations above, we rewrite the Teukolsky equation (
Teuk
) as follows:

\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-4}D^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}\frac{r^{5}D\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}}{D^{2}})=\frac{D}{r^{2+4}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2+4)(r^{5}D\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}})-\frac{30MD}{r^{3+4}}r^{5}D\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}

(9.2)

Next, we suggestively write (
Teuk
) as

\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{4}D^{-2}\not{\nabla}_{v}(D^{2}\frac{r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}}{D}))=\frac{D}{r^{2-4}}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)\frac{r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}}{D}-\frac{6MD}{r^{3-4}}\frac{r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}}{D},

(9.3)

and introduce the notation

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}:=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+s,\quad\alpha^{[s]}:=\begin{cases}r^{5}D^{-1}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}},&\quad s=2,\\
rD\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}},&\quad s=-2\end{cases}

(9.4)

The two equations (
9.2
) and (
9.3
) can then be formulated in a unified way as

\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\not{\nabla}_{v}\alpha^{[s]})=\frac{D^{s+1}}{r^{2+2s}}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}\alpha^{[s]}-\frac{2MD^{s+1}}{r^{3+2s}}(1+s)(1+2s)\alpha^{[s]},\quad s=\pm 2.

(Teuk:s)

We note that the operator
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}
has eigenvalues

\Lambda^{[s]}_{\ell}:=-\ell(\ell+1)+s(s+1)=-(\ell-s)(\ell+s+1),\quad\ell\geq 2.

(9.5)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{s}\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{s}\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{s}\not{\nabla}_{v}\alpha^{[s]}\right)-s\left(\partial_{v}\partial_{u}\log\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right){\alpha^{[s]}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2s}}{D^{s}}\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})-\frac{2MD}{r^{3}}((1+s)(1+2s))(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})\right)-s\left(\frac{2D}{r^{2}}-\frac{12MD}{r^{3}}\right){\alpha^{[s]}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2s}}{D^{s}}\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[-s]}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})-\frac{2MD}{r^{3}}((1-s)(1-2s))(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})\right),

(Teuk’:s)

where we used (
Teuk:s
) in the third line as well as the relations
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}-2s=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[-s]}
and
(1+s)(1+2s)-6s=(1-s)(1-2s)
in the fourth.

Remark 9.1
.

Note that if a scalar function
\phi
solves
\Box_{g}\phi=0
, then
\alpha^{[0]}=r\phi
satisfies (
Teuk:s
) for
s=0
.
Similarly, the Teukolsky equation for
s=\pm 1
has the interpretation of describing the dynamics of electromagnetic perturbations on Schwarzschild,
\alpha^{[\pm 1]}
then being a 1-form.
We can also make sense of (
Teuk:s
) for
|s|\geq 2
by regarding
\alpha^{[s]}
as a symmetric, trace-free
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
-tangent
(0,|s|)
-tensor, though the physical interpretation of the equation for
s>2
is not clear.

All computations presented in the remainder of this and the next section are valid for arbitrary integer values of
s
. Nevertheless, the main interest will lie in the cases
s=\pm 2
.

9.2
The commuted Teukolsky equations

We now prove a simple commutation formula, from which we will later derive an approximate conservation law.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\alpha^{[s]}
be a smooth solution to (
Teuk:s
). Then

\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N}\alpha^{[s]}\right)\\
=\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\left(a^{[s]}_{N,j}+b^{[s]}_{N,j}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}-c^{[s]}_{N,j}\frac{2M}{r}\right)(2M)^{j}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N-j}\alpha^{[s]}.

(9.6)

Similarly, we have

\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N-s}\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{N}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})\right)\\
=\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N-s+1}\sum_{j=0}^{1}(-1)^{j}\left(a^{[-s]}_{N,j}+b^{[-s]}_{N,j}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[-s]}-c^{[-s]}_{N,j}\frac{2M}{r}\right)(2M)^{j}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{N-j}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]}).

(9.7)

The constants
a^{[s]}_{N,j},b^{[s]}_{N,j},c^{[s]}_{N,j}
are given explicitly by

a^{[s]}_{N,0}=N(N+1+2s),\quad b^{[s]}_{N,0}=1,\quad c^{[s]}_{N,0}=(1+s)(1+2s)+3N(N+1+2s)

(9.8)

and

\displaystyle a^{[s]}_{N,1}=N(N+s)(N+2s),

\displaystyle b^{[s]}_{N,1}=0=c^{[s]}_{N,1}.

(9.9)

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.

We first prove (
9.6
). Note that for
N=0
, it reduces to (
Teuk:s
). We now establish an inductive relation to prove it for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Observe:

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\right)\right)-\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\partial_{v}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+1+s}\right)\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{N+s+1}\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-\partial_{v}\log\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{N+s+1}\right)\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\right)

\displaystyle-\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\partial_{v}\log\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{-N-s-1}\right)\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{N+s+1}\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{N+s+1}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}.

Since
\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log r=\frac{D}{r^{2}}-\frac{4MD}{r^{3}}
, and since
\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log D=\frac{4MD}{r^{3}}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\log\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{N+s+1}\right)=(N+s+1)\left(\frac{2D}{r^{2}}-\frac{12MD}{r^{3}}\right).

In conclusion, we thus obtain

\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+2}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+2}\alpha^{[s]}\right)\\
=\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{N+s+1}\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\right)\right)\\
