+\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+2}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N+1}\alpha^{[s]}\left(2(N+1+s)-6\cdot\frac{2M}{r}(N+1+s)\right).

(9.10)

We now assume (
9.6
) to hold for some fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Using the inductive relation (
9.10
), we then arrive at (
9.6
) with
N
replaced by
N+1
, where

\displaystyle a^{[s]}_{N+1,0}=a^{[s]}_{N,0}+2(s+N+1),\quad b^{[s]}_{N+1,0}=b^{[s]}_{N,0},\quad c^{[s]}_{N+1,0}=c^{[s]}_{N,0}+6(N+1+s)

and

\displaystyle a^{[s]}_{N+1,1}=a^{[s]}_{N,1}+c^{[s]}_{N,0}.

It is left to solve these recurrence relations, whose initial values are provided by (
Teuk:s
):
a^{[s]}_{0,0}=0=a^{[s]}_{0,1}
,
b^{[s]}_{0,0}=1
,
c^{[s]}_{0,0}=(1+s)(1+2s)
, the result being

\displaystyle a^{[s]}_{N,0}=N(N+1+2s),\quad b^{[s]}_{N,0}=1,\quad c^{[s]}_{N,0}=(1+s)(1+2s)+3N(N+1+2s)

(9.11)

and

\displaystyle a^{[s]}_{N,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}c^{[s]}_{i,0}=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}(1+2s)(1+s)+6si+3i(i+1)

(9.12)

\displaystyle=(N-1)N(N+1)+3s(N-1)N+(1+s)(1+2s)N

\displaystyle=N(N^{2}-1+3sN-3s+1+3s+2s^{2})=N(N^{2}+3sN+2s^{2})=N(N+s)(N+2s).

This concludes the proof of (
9.6
).

Let’s now move on to prove (
9.7
). The case
N=0
is given by (
Teuk’:s
).
We then obtain the inductive relationship:

\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N-s+2}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{N+2}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})\right)\\
=\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N-s+1}\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\right)^{N-s+1}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(r^{2s}\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+1}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{N+1}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})\right)\right)\\
+\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N-s+2}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{u}\right)^{N+1}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})(N-s+1)\left(2-6\cdot\frac{2M}{r}\right).

(9.13)

Equation (
9.7
) then follows in the same way as equation (
9.6
) did.
∎

9.3
The approximate conservation laws satisfied by
\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}

Consider a solution
\alpha^{[s]}
to (
Teuk:s
) supported only on angular frequency
\ell
. For such solutions, we have
(a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}+b^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]})\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=0
, so there is a cancellation in equation (
9.6
) for
N=\ell-s
.
The remaining term with a bad
r
-weight, the
a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1}
-term, can then be removed by iteratively subtracting suitable multiples of (
9.6
) with
N=\ell-s-i
,
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell-s\}
. This leads to the following class of approximate conservation laws:

Corollary 9.1
.

Let
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
be a smooth solution to (
Teuk:s
) that is supported on some fixed
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq|s|}
.
Define

\tilde{a}^{[s]}_{N,0,\ell}:=a^{[s]}_{N,0}-b^{[s]}_{N,0}\Lambda^{[s]}_{\ell}=(N+s-\ell)(N+s+\ell+1).

(9.14)

If
x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}
is any collection of constants with
x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}=0
if
i>\ell-s
, then, as a direct corollary of Proposition
9.1
,

\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-s}x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}(2M)^{i}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{\ell-s-i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right)\right)\\
=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-s}\left(2(i+1)x^{[s]}_{i+1,\ell}\frac{2M}{r}\left(1-\frac{3M}{r}\right)+x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}\cdot\tilde{a}^{[s]}_{\ell-s-i,0,\ell}+x^{[s]}_{i-1,\ell}a^{[s]}_{\ell-s-i+1,1}-x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}\frac{2M}{r}\cdot c^{[s]}_{\ell-s-i,0}\right)\\
\cdot\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{\ell+1}(2M)^{i}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{\ell-s-i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}.

(9.15)

We now fix the constants
x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}
via

x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}:=(-1)^{i}\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{a^{[s]}_{\ell-s-j,1}}{\tilde{a}^{[s]}_{\ell-s-j-1,0,\ell}}=\frac{1}{i!}\frac{(2\ell-i)!}{(2\ell)!}\frac{(\ell-s)!\ell!(\ell+s)!}{(\ell-s-i)!(\ell-i)!(\ell+s-i)!}

(9.16)

such that the
a
-terms in (
9.15
) vanish. (Note that
x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}=x^{[-s]}_{i,\ell}
, so, in particular
x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}=0
for
i\geq\ell-|s|
.) Finally, define

\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}:=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-|s|}x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}(2M)^{i}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{\ell-s-i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}.

(9.17)

This quantity then satisfies

\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}\right)\\
=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-|s|}\left(2(k+1)x^{[s]}_{k+1,\ell}\frac{2M}{r}\left(1-\frac{3M}{r}\right)-x^{[s]}_{k,\ell}\frac{2M}{r}\cdot c^{[s]}_{\ell-s-k,0}\right)\\
\cdot\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{\ell+1}(2M)^{k}\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{D}\right)^{\ell-s-k}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}.

(9.18)

Remark 9.2
.

An analogous result holds with
u
and
v
interchanged.

9.4
The modified Newman–Penrose charges

The approximate conservation laws (
9.18
) of the previous subsection imply the conservation of weighted limits of
\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
. The following corollary mainly serves the purpose of providing us with some nomenclature for these limits. We therefore state some fairly strong assumptions that guarantee the existence and the conservation of these limits.

Corollary 9.2
.

Let
\alpha^{[s]}
be a smooth solution to (
Teuk:s
) arising from scattering data on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}
, and let
p\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
.
Suppose that the following bound holds in the domain of dependence
\mathcal{D}
of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}
:

\left|\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq C(u)\frac{\log^{q}r}{r^{p}}

(9.19)

for some constant that is allowed to depend on
u
. If there exists a
u^{\prime}\leq u_{0}
such that the limit
\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{r^{p}}{\log^{q}r}\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}(u^{\prime},v)
exists, then the limit

\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[s]}](u):=\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{r^{p}}{\log^{q}r}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}(u,v)

(9.20)

exists for all
u\leq u_{0}
and is independent of
u
,
\not{\nabla}_{u}\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[s]}]=0
. We call this limit the
(p,q)
-modified Newman–Penrose charge of
\alpha^{[s]}
.

Furthermore, if we only assume (
9.19
) for
s=+2
, and assume that
\left|\not{\nabla}_{v}^{2}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-2}\alpha^{[+2]}\right|=o\left(\frac{\log^{q}r}{r^{p}}\right)
and that
\alpha^{[+2]}
and
\alpha^{[-2]}
are related via (
3.44
), then

\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[-2]}]=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[+2]}].

(9.21)

9.5
The Regge–Wheeler equations of general spin
s
satisfied by
\Psi^{[s]}
and their conservation laws

We observe that if
s<0
, then the commuted equation (
9.6
) for
N=|s|=-s
gives the Regge–Wheeler equation (cf. (
RW
)), and if
s\geq 0
, then the same holds for (
9.7
) with
N=|s|=s
.
To be precise, we define

\displaystyle\Psi^{[s]}:=\begin{cases}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{|s|}\alpha^{[s]},&\,\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,s\leq 0,\\
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{s}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]}),&\,\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,s>0,\end{cases}

(9.22)

and this quantity satisfies

\not{\nabla}_{u}\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi^{[s]}=\frac{D}{r^{2}}\left((\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}-\Lambda_{0}^{[s]})-c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{0,0}\frac{2M}{r}\right)\Psi^{[s]},

(RW:s)

where
\Lambda_{0}^{[s]}=-s(s+1)
is the lowest eigenvalue of
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}
(eq. (
9.5
)), and where
c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{0,0}:=c^{[-|s|]}_{|s|,0}=1-s^{2}
.

Starting from (
RW:s
), we can then, just as before, derive its commuted versions along with the resulting conservation laws:

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\Psi^{[s]}
be a smooth solution to the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW:s
). Then

\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N}\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N}\Psi^{[s]}\right)\\
=\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+1}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\left(a^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,j}+b^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,j}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}-\Lambda_{0}^{[s]})-c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,j}\frac{2M}{r}\right)(2M)^{j}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{N-j}\Psi^{[s]},

(9.23)

with
a^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,0}=a^{[0]}_{N,0}=N(N+1)
,
b^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,0}=b^{[0]}_{N,0}=1
,
c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,0}=3N(N+1)+(1-s^{2})
and
a^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,1}=(N-s)N(N+s)
, and
b^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,1}=0=c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,1}
.

Furthermore, for
x^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{i,\ell}:=x^{[s]}_{i,\ell}
, the quantity

\mathrm{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{[s]}_{\ell}:=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-|s|}x^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{i,\ell}(2M)^{i}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{\ell-i}\Psi_{\ell}

(9.24)

satisfies (note that
c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,0}=c^{[s]}_{N-s,0}
for
N\geq s
)

\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{[s]}_{\ell}\right)\\
=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-|s|}\left(2(k+1)x^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{k+1,\ell}\frac{2M}{r}\left(1-\frac{3M}{r}\right)-x^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{k,\ell}\frac{2M}{r}\cdot c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{\ell-k,0}\right)\\
\cdot\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{\ell+1}(2M)^{k}\left(\frac{r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}}{D}\right)^{\ell-k}\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}.

(9.25)

Finally, provided the limit exists, we denote

\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\Psi](u):=\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{r^{p}}{\log^{q}r}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{[s]}_{\ell}(u,v)

(9.26)

and call it the modified Newman–Penrose charge for
\Psi^{[s]}
.

Remark 9.3
.

If
s\leq 0
, we see immediately that
\mathrm{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{[s]}_{\ell}=\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
.
For
s>0
, a similar statement is valid only to leading order, up to application of some angular operator of order
2s
.
For instance, for
s=2
, we have (cf. eq. (
3.40
))

\mathrm{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{[s]}_{\ell}=(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-4)\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}-6M(\not{\nabla}_{u}+\not{\nabla}_{v})\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}.

In particular,
\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\Psi^{[2]}](u)=(\ell-1)\ell(\ell+1)(\ell+2)\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[2]}](u)
since the limit of
\frac{r^{p}}{\log^{q}r}\not{\nabla}_{v}(-6M)(\not{\nabla}_{u}+\not{\nabla}_{v})\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
will vanish (for the same reason why
\mathbf{I}^{p,q}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[2]}](u)
is conserved).

10
Asymptotic analysis of
\alpha^{[s]}
arising from general scattering data

Having derived a class of approximate conservation laws (eq. (
9.18
)) satisfied by angular modes
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
of solutions
\alpha^{[s]}
to the Teukolsky equation (
Teuk:s
) with arbitrary integer spin
s
, we now use these to derive precise asymptotic expressions for fixed angular modes
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
of scattering solutions
\alpha^{[s]}
arising from scattering data prescribed on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
. As the following quantity will frequently appear throughout the remaining sections, we now introduce the notation

r_{0}(u):=r(u,v=v_{1}).

(10.1)

A note on conventions

In the asymptotic computations to follow, we will often derive asymptotic decay/growth rates for expressions without computing the precise coefficients. For these situations, we will always just denote the coefficients by
\mathscr{E}
.

10.1
Definition of scattering solutions and the no incoming radiation condition

As we will study scattering solutions to (
Teuk:s
) in this part of the paper, let us first define what constitutes seed scattering data, i.e. data that determine a scattering solution provided they are sufficiently regular.
These definitions are only relevant for the present section for a self-contained treatment of the Teukolsky equation of general spin. On the other hand, in the sections to follow, we will always apply our results directly to the scattering solutions constructed in the previous part of the paper (cf. §
8
).

Definition 10.1
.

Seed scattering data for (
Teuk:s
) consist of specifying
\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and
\mathtt{P}^{[s]}_{\mathcal{I^{-}}}
along
\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}}
.
If
s<0
, they additionally consist of the specifications of the
|s|
tensor fields
\not{\nabla}_{v}^{i}\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
along
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
, with
i=1,\dots,|s|
.

We say that
\alpha^{[s]}
is a scattering solution corresponding to these seed data if it solves (
Teuk:s
), if
\alpha^{[s]}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
, if in the case
s<0
the restrictions of the transversal derivatives to
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
satisfy
\not{\nabla}_{v}^{i}\alpha^{[s]}|_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}=\not{\nabla}_{v}^{i}\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}_{1}}}
for
i=1,\dots,|s|
, and if
\lim_{u\to-\infty}\left\|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi^{[s]}-\mathtt{P}^{[s]}\right\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}=0
for any
v\geq v_{1}
, where
\Psi^{[s]}
is related to
\alpha^{[s]}
via (
9.22
).

Note that the transversal derivatives may alternatively be specified on any other sphere along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Throughout this entire part of the paper, we shall only study scattering solutions to (
Teuk:s
) that satisfy the no incoming radiation condition: In view of the discussion in §
8.1
(cf. Rem.
8.2
), we make the following

Definition 10.2
.

Seed scattering data to (
Teuk:s
) satisfy the no incoming radiation condition if
\mathtt{P}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}^{[s]}=0
, i.e. if

\lim_{u\to-\infty}\|\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi^{[s]}\|_{L^{2}([v_{1},v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}=0.

(10.2)

If
s>0
, we moreover require that:

\lim_{u\to-\infty}\|(D^{-1}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{i-1}(r^{-2s}D^{s}{\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}})\|_{L^{2}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})}}=0,\quad i=1,\dots s.

(10.3)

10.2
The main theorem (Thm.
10.1
)

The seed scattering data for which we will prove the main result of this section are as follows: We take
\mathtt{P}^{[s]}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=0
. Along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, we specify
\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
smooth via

\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{A}^{[s]}r_{0}^{-p+s}+\mathcal{O}_{\max(s+1,0)}(r_{0}^{-p-\epsilon}),

(10.4)

for some
\epsilon>0
,
\mathscr{A}^{[s]}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\not{\Pi}\,{T}^{(0,|s|)}_{stf}{\underline{\mathcal{C}}})
with
\not{\nabla}_{u}\mathscr{A}^{[s]}=0
, and
p
satisfying

p>\max\left(-s-1,-\tfrac{1}{2}\right).

(10.5)

The range for
p
guarantees that the seed data satisfy the no incoming radiation condition.
From now on, we will drop the superscript
[s]
from
\mathscr{A}^{[s]}
.

For
s<0
, we recall that it is additionally necessary to specify all transversal derivatives up to order
-s
along
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
.
For the present section, we will simplify our lives by assuming that along
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
:
13
13

13

The restriction on the decay rate (
10.5
) is necessary to ensure that this determines the transversal derivatives along all of
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, cf. Prop.
10.1
.

r^{-2s}\not{\nabla}_{v}^{i}\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}}=0,\quad\text{for }i=1,\dots,-s,\quad\text{if }s<0.

(10.6)

Recall that if
s<0
, the no incoming radiation condition only states that a weighted derivative of order
-s+1
vanishes along
\mathcal{I}^{-}
(cf. (
10.2
)). Condition (
10.6
) has the effect that in addition all lower-order derivatives (up to order
-s
) vanish as well.
This is a simplifying assumption that is independent of the condition of no incoming radiation. We will remove it in §
13
, where the case
s<0
is discussed in the context of the physical data of §
8
. (Notice that the

?
physical
?
decay rate of
\alpha^{[-2]}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
is given by
r_{0}^{-3}=r_{0}^{-1-2}
(cf. (
8.14
)), which marginally violates the condition (
10.4
) that
p>-s-1=1
.)

The content of §
10
is the proof of the following

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then there exists a unique smooth scattering solution
\alpha^{[s]}
to (
Teuk:s
) that satisfies the no incoming radiation condition (
10.2
)–(
10.3
), restricts along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
to
\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}
as specified in (
10.4
), and, if
s<0
, satisfies (
10.6
) in the sense of Definition
10.1
.
Moreover, for
\ell\geq|s|
, each angular mode
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
of this solution has the following asymptotic expression throughout
\mathcal{D}=D^{+}({\underline{\mathcal{C}}}\cup\mathcal{I}^{-}_{v\geq v_{1}})
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=&\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0}^{s-p}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\max(\ell-s,\lceil p-s\rceil)}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}(S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}+\mathcal{O}^{u}(r_{0}^{-\epsilon})+\mathcal{O}^{u}(Mr_{0}^{-1}))\right)\\
&-(-1)^{\ell-s}M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}\frac{(\ell-s)!(s+p)!}{(\ell+s)!}(\ell+p+1)\cdot r^{s-1-p}\cdot F_{\ell,p,s}(r,r_{0}),\end{split}

(10.7)

where the constants
S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}
are defined in (
10.64
) for
n\leq\ell-s
and are defined to vanish for
n>\ell-s.
If
\ell-p\geq 0
, then
F_{\ell,p,s}(r,r_{0})
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(s-p-2)!\cdot\{(\ell-p)&\\
+\mathcal{O}((\tfrac{r_{0}}{r})^{\lceil p\rceil-p}+\tfrac{r_{0}}{r}(1+\delta_{s-2-p,0}\log r/r_{0})+r^{-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{s-p-1,\epsilon}\log r/r_{0}))\},&1+p-s\notin\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\\
\frac{(-1)^{p+1-s}}{(p+1-s)!}((\ell-p)\log r/r_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\log r/r_{0}+r^{-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{\epsilon,1}\log r/r_{0})),&1+p-s\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}.\end{cases}

(10.8)

If, on the other hand,
\ell-p<0
, then
F_{\ell,p,s}(r,r_{0})
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathscr{E}_{\ell,p,s}+\mathcal{O}((\tfrac{r_{0}}{r})^{\lceil p\rceil-p}+\tfrac{r_{0}}{r}(1+\delta_{s-2-p,0}\log r/r_{0})+r^{-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{s-p-1,\epsilon}\log r/r_{0}))\},&1+p-s\notin\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\\
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}_{\ell,p,s}\log r/r_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\log r/r_{0}+r^{-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{\epsilon,1}\log r/r_{0})),&1+p-s\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}.\end{cases}

(10.9)

for some constants
\mathscr{E}_{\ell,p,s}
,
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}_{\ell,p,s}
that we do not determine.

Moreover, if
\ell-p\geq-1
, then the solution has finite, non-zero and conserved
\ell
-th Newman–Penrose charge
\mathbf{I}^{p^{\prime},q}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[s]}]
for
p^{\prime}=2-\ell+p
,
q=\delta_{\ell-p,-1}
; its value can be read off from Proposition
10.4
.

Remark 10.1
.

The uniqueness asserted in Theorem
10.1
is with respect to the class of
smooth
solutions
\alpha^{[s]}
with finite Regge–Wheeler energy, cf. §
6
.
The requirement that the solution be smooth (or sufficiently regular) can be dropped if
s<0
or if
s\in\{0,2\}
. For the other cases, one can also drop the requirement by simply generalising the arguments of §
6.4
to general
s>0
. Since the cases
s=\pm 2
are the main focus of the paper, we leave this to the interested reader.

Remark 10.2
.

It should be straight-forward to prove that (
10.8
) is valid for the entire range of
p
.
We leave this as an exercise to the interested reader. (This amounts to explicitly computing the coefficients
\mathscr{E}
in (
10.45
)–(
10.47
).)

Remark 10.3
.

The
\ell
-dependent constants hiding in the
\mathcal{O}
terms in (
10.7
) grow too fast to directly extract an asymptotic expression for the entire solution
\alpha^{[s]}=\sum_{\ell}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
.
We return to this point in §
15
.
In general, we expect the estimates of Thm.
10.1
to be summable provided that the initial data have sufficient angular regularity. But since we are not making any statements on the summability of the estimates here, the regularity of the error term specified in (
10.4
) suffices.

The theorem allows to also directly infer statements about solutions to the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW:s
):

Corollary 10.1
.

Prescribe scattering data for
\Psi^{[s]}
satisfying
\Psi^{[s]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{A}r_{0}^{-p}+\mathcal{O}_{1}(r_{0}^{-p-\epsilon})
together with the no incoming radiation condition (
10.2
). Then the resulting scattering solution satisfies the estimate (
10.7
) with
\alpha^{[s]}
replaced by
\Psi^{[s]}
and with
s
on the RHS of (
10.7
) and in (
10.8
), (
10.9
) replaced by 0.

We will prove this corollary at the end of this section.

10.3
Overview of the proof

The proof consists of the following steps:

a.

First, in §
10.4
, we compute sufficiently many transversal derivatives
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{n}\alpha^{[s]}
along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
(in an a priori fashion) by inductively integrating equation (
9.6
) from
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
.

b.

We then establish the existence of a scattering solution and prove a preliminary pointwise decay estimate (this decay is sharp near
\mathcal{I}^{-}
but fails to be sharp near
\mathcal{I}^{+}
) for
\alpha^{[s]}
and its derivatives using the Regge–Wheeler energy estimate (Lemma
6.1
) in §
10.5
.
Note that control over the Regge–Wheeler energy along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
is obtained from tangential
\not{\nabla}_{u}
-derivatives along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
in the case
s\geq 0
, whereas, in the case of
s<0
, it is obtained from
transversal

\not{\nabla}_{v}
-derivatives. Cf. Section
9.5
.

c.

Starting from this preliminary decay estimate, we next use the approximate conservation law (
9.18
) to derive an asymptotic expression for the quantity
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
in §
10.6
.

d.

The asymptotic estimate for
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
allows us to obtain an asymptotic estimate for
\not{\nabla}_{v}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\right)^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}
.
We then integrate this latter estimate up to
\ell-s
terms from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, using the estimates obtained in step a, to obtain asymptotic estimates for all lower order derivatives, including
\alpha^{[s]}
itself. This final step is carried out in §
10.7
.

In principle, we could then use all the asymptotic estimates for the lower-order derivatives obtained in step d above to arrive at a more precise asymptotic estimate for
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
and iterate, but we will not do this here.

Remark 10.4
.

In the following, all the estimates arising from integrating along characteristics (e.g.
\int(\not{\nabla}_{v}\alpha^{[s]})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v
) will be comparing the components of
\alpha^{[s]}
in the parallelly propagated frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
; we will be using that, in view of (
2.12
),

\not{\nabla}_{u}(D^{\ell}r^{-2\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}(D^{-1}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]})_{A\dots B}=\partial_{u}(D^{\ell}r^{-2\ell}\partial_{v}(D^{-1}r^{2}\partial_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{A\dots B}).

(10.10)

For fixed angular modes
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
, another way of viewing this is to write
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}(\alpha^{[s],\text{E}}_{\ell,m}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},|s|}+\alpha^{[s],\text{H}}_{\ell,m}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},|s|})
,
then we are just comparing the scalar functions
\alpha^{[s],\text{E}}_{\ell,m}(u,v)
,
\alpha^{[s],\text{H}}_{\ell,m}(u,v)
at different points in spacetime.

10.4
Computing transversal derivatives along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}

The following is to be understood as an a priori estimate:

Proposition 10.1
.

Assume that a scattering solution
\alpha^{[s]}
attaining the data of Theorem
10.1
exists, and assume additionally that
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{-2s}\not{\nabla}_{v}^{n}\alpha^{[s]}=0
for any
n>\max(0,-s)+1
. Then we have the following expressions for transversal derivatives along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
if
N\leq\ell-s
:

\left.(D^{-1}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{N}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0}^{N-p+s}\underbrace{\frac{(s+p)!}{(N+s+p)!}\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}\tilde{a}^{[s]}_{i,0,\ell}}_{:=C^{\nearrow}_{(N,p)}}+\mathcal{O}(r^{N-p+s-\epsilon}+Mr^{N-p+s-1}).

(10.11)

The product on the RHS can be computed as

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}\tilde{a}^{[s]}_{i,0,\ell}=(-1)^{N}\frac{(\ell-s)!(N+s+\ell)!}{(\ell-s-N)!(\ell+s)!}.

(10.12)

In particular,

C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}=(-1)^{\ell-s}\frac{(s+p)!(\ell-s)!(2\ell)!}{(\ell+p)!(\ell+s)!}.

(10.13)

On the other hand, if
N>\ell-s
, then we have that

\left.(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{N}\alpha^{[s]}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=M\cdot\mathscr{E}r_{0}^{N-p+s-1}+M\mathcal{O}(r^{N-p+s-1-\epsilon}+Mr^{N-p+s-2})

(10.14)

for some constants
\mathscr{E}
whose precise values won’t matter.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by inductively integrating (
9.6
) from
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
. The estimate (
10.11
) holds true for
N=0
. Assume now that
N<\ell-s
is fixed, and assume (
10.11
) to hold for all
n\leq N
. Then we can integrate (
9.6
) from
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
, where we use the assumption that
\lim_{u\to-\infty}r^{-2s}\not{\nabla}_{v}^{i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=0
for any
i\geq 0
, to obtain:

\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s}\not{\nabla}_{v}(D^{-1}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{N}\alpha^{[s]}\\
=\int\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{N+s+1}\tilde{a}^{[s]}_{N,0,\ell}\cdot\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0}^{N+s-p}\frac{(s+p)!}{(N+s+p)!}\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}\tilde{a}^{[s]}_{i,0,\ell}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u+\mathcal{O}(r^{N+1+s-p-\epsilon}+Mr^{N+s-p}).

This proves (
10.11
). Equation (
10.12
) is a direct computation.
The last claim follows in a similar fashion, using now that
\tilde{a}^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0,\ell}=0
.
∎

10.5
A robust preliminary decay estimate based on energy conservation

Proposition 10.2
.

There exists a unique smooth scattering solution in
\mathcal{D}
attaining the data described in Theorem
10.1
. This solution satisfies the assumptions of Prop.
10.1
and moreover satisfies the following pointwise bounds throughout
\mathcal{D}
:

\displaystyle|\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}|\leq\ell^{1+\max{(-s,0)}}Cr_{0}^{-p-\frac{1}{2}}\max(r^{s+\frac{1}{2}},r_{0}^{s+\frac{1}{2}})

(10.15)

for some constant
C
independent of
\ell
.
If
p>0
, we have the following sharper estimate for any
N\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\left|\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq\ell^{1+\max{(-s,0)}}C\max\left(r_{0}^{s-p},r^{s-p},\delta_{p,s}\cdot(\log\tfrac{r}{r_{0}}+1)\right)

(10.16)

for another constant
C
independent of
\ell
.

Proof.

This proof mostly just recollects the ideas from §
6
:

Recall that, given a smooth solution
\alpha^{[s]}
to the Teukolsky equation (
Teuk:s
), we know that the derived quantity
\Psi^{[s]}
, defined in (
9.22
), solves the Regge–Wheeler equation (
RW:s
).
By assumption (
10.4
) in the case
s\geq 0
, and by assumption (
10.6
) and Prop.
10.1
in the case
s<0
, we then know that
\Psi^{[s]}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathcal{O}_{1}(r^{1/2-\epsilon^{\prime}})
for some
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
; in particular, it has finite Regge–Wheeler energy
E_{v_{1}}[\Psi^{[s]}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}](-\infty,u_{0})
as defined in Def.
6.1
.
This means that our scattering theory for the Regge–Wheeler equation from §
6
applies and that it provides us a with a unique scattering solution
\Psi^{[s]}
(cf. Thm
6.1
).
14
14

14

Note that the statements of §
6
also apply to the Regge–Wheeler equation of general spin (
RW:s
).

We can then reconstruct out of
\Psi^{[s]}
a unique solution
\alpha^{[s]}
by appropriately integrating the definition (
9.22
) from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
in the case
s<0
.
On the other hand, if
s>0
, then we must specify in addition the values of
X^{(i)}:=\lim_{u\to-\infty}(D^{-1}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u})^{i}(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]}))
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
for
i=0,\dots,s-1
. It turns out that, as a consequence of these limits vanishing at
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
(cf. (
10.3
)), the unique choice for these limits is
X^{(i)}\equiv 0
; we will defer the proof of this statement to the end of the proof.

It is moreover standard to show that all derivatives
r^{-2s}\not{\nabla}_{v}^{N}\alpha^{[s]}\to 0
as
u\to-\infty
for any
N\geq 0
(cf. (
6.27
) of Prop.
6.2
).

We now move on to prove the estimate (
10.15
):
By the Regge–Wheeler
T
-energy identity (cf. Lemma
6.1
), we have that for any
v\geq v_{1}
:

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v}[\Psi^{[s]}](-\infty,u)\leq\underline{E}_{v_{1}}[\Psi^{[s]}](-\infty,u)+\lim_{u^{\prime}\to-\infty}{E}_{u^{\prime}}[\Psi^{[s]}](v_{1},v).

(10.17)

The limit on the RHS vanishes by assumption (
10.2
).

We now focus on fixed angular modes: Either directly by the initial data assumption in the case
s\geq 0
, or by Prop.
10.1
if
s<0
, it follows that, along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
,

\displaystyle\underline{E}_{v_{1}}[\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}](-\infty,u)

\displaystyle=\int_{v=v_{1},u^{\prime}\in(-\infty,u)}|\not{\nabla}_{u}\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}|^{2}+\frac{D\ell(\ell+1)|\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}|^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{6MD|\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}|^{2}}{r^{3}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

(10.18)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{2+\max(-2s,0)}Cr_{0}^{-2p-1}

for some constant
C
that is allowed to change from line to line and doesn’t depend on
\ell
.
We can exploit the positive potential term in (
10.18
) to further estimate for any
v\geq v_{1}
:

\int_{v^{\prime}=v,u^{\prime}\in(-\infty,u)}r^{2}|\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-1}\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell})|^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\lesssim\underline{E}_{v}[\Psi^{[s]}](-\infty,u).

(10.19)

Now, applying a simple fundamental theorem of calculus argument together with Cauchy–Schwarz and the previous three estimates, we obtain (this is essentially the estimate (
6.26
)):

|r^{-1}\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}|\lesssim r^{-1/2}\sqrt{\underline{E}_{v}[\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}](-\infty,u)}\leq\ell^{1+\max(-s,0)}Cr_{0}^{-p-1/2}r^{-1/2}.

(10.20)

Integrating this
|s|
times either from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
or from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
(in which case we also use the vanishing of the corresponding limits), we then obtain the lazy estimate

|\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}|\leq\ell^{1+\max{(-s,0)}}C\max(r^{s+1/2},r_{0}^{s+1/2})r_{0}^{-1/2-p}.

(10.21)

This proves (
10.15
).

Let us now assume that
p>0
. In order to then remove the factor
\sqrt{r/r_{0}}
in (
10.20
), we need to consider the
u
-weighted version of the energy estimate given in Lemma
6.2
. Recall from Prop.
6.3
that the quantity

\underline{E}^{1+\delta}_{v}[\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}](-\infty,u)\\
:=\int_{v=const,u^{\prime}\in(-\infty,u)}|u^{\prime}|^{1+\delta}\left(|\not{\nabla}_{u}\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}|^{2}+\frac{D\ell(\ell+1)|\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}|^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{6MD|\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}|^{2}}{r^{3}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}

(10.22)

decays in
v
for any
\delta>-1
.
Since for any
p>0
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that
\underline{E}^{1+\delta}_{v_{1}}[\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}](-\infty,u)
is finite at
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, we can then repeat the previous argument, replacing (
10.20
) with

|\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}|\leq|u|^{-\delta/2}\sqrt{\underline{E}^{1+\delta}_{v_{1}}[\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}](-\infty,u)}\leq\ell^{1+\max(-s,0)}Cr_{0}^{-p}.

(10.23)

Estimate (
10.16
) then again follows by appropriate integration of (
9.22
), this time keeping precise track of the weights, using also the integral formulae from Lemma
B.1
.

Let’s now finally return to the issue (arising only for
s>0
) of showing that the limits
X^{(i)}:=\lim_{u\to-\infty}(D^{-1}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{u})(r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]})
must vanish. Clearly, taking them to vanish produces a smooth solution restricting correctly to the data, so the question reduces to a uniqueness statement, i.e. to showing that the limits
X^{(i)}
vanish for trivial scattering data. Here, we content ourselves with proving uniqueness within the class of smooth (finite-energy) solutions (cf. Remark
10.1
).
For trivial scattering data, the resulting solution will have
\Psi^{[s]}=0
, and so, in general, we have (by integrating
\Psi^{[s]}=0
)

\displaystyle r^{-2s}D^{s}\alpha^{[s]}=\sum_{i=0}^{s-1}\frac{X^{(i)}(v,\theta^{A})}{i!r^{i}}.

(10.24)

We now first apply to this expression the formula (
9.7
) with
N=s-1
—this is simply the formula for
\not{\nabla}_{v}\Psi^{[s]}
. By the no incoming radiation condition, we thus get

(a^{[-s]}_{s-1,0}+\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[-s]})X^{(s-1)}=2Ma^{[-s]}_{s-1,1}X^{(s-2)}.

(10.25)

On the other hand, applying (
9.7
) with
m-1
for
m<s
, we obtain the identity

\not{\nabla}_{v}X^{(m)}=(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[-s]}+a^{[-s]}_{m-1,0})X^{(m-1)}-2Ma^{[-s]}_{m-1,1}X^{(m-2)},

(10.26)

of which a simple iterate gives

\not{\nabla}_{v}^{m}X^{(m)}=P^{(m)}X^{(0)}

(10.27)

for some
m
-th order constant coefficient differential operator, where both
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[-s]}
and
\not{\nabla}_{v}
attain order at most
\lceil m/2\rceil
.
We now act with
\not{\nabla}_{v}^{s-1}
on (
10.25
) using (
10.27
) to obtain

\left((a^{[-s]}_{s-1,0}+\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[-s]})P^{(s-1)}-2Ma^{[-s]}_{s-1,1}\not{\nabla}_{v}P^{(s-2)}\right)X^{(0)}=0.

(10.28)

For each fixed angular frequency, this is a homogeneous ODE of order
\lceil\frac{s-1}{2}\rceil
, and we can easily see from (
Teuk’:s
) that the initial data at
v=v_{1}
vanish, so
X^{(0)}
vanishes everywhere (for all
v\geq v_{1}
). We similarly deduce that all other
X^{(i)}
vanish for
i<s
.
∎

10.6
Deriving asymptotic expressions using the approximate conservation laws

Equipped with the pointwise estimate (
10.15
) derived from the Regge–Wheeler energy estimate, we shall now use the approximate conservation law (
9.18
) to derive asymptotic expressions for
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
.
Appealing to (
9.18
), while providing us with a simple and intuitive understanding for these asymptotic expressions, means that we cannot directly make statements uniform in
\ell
, we will therefore stop keeping track of
\ell
-dependencies of constants in estimates. See already §
15
.

10.6.1
A preliminary estimate on
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}

We start by proving the following estimate:

Proposition 10.3
.

The solution from Theorem
10.1
satisfies throughout
\mathcal{D}
:

\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq M\cdot C\max(r^{\ell-p},r_{0}^{\ell-p}).

(10.29)

Proof.

As a first step, notice that, repeating the computations of Prop.
10.1
, estimate (
10.15
) directly implies

\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{N}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq C_{N}r_{0}^{-1/2-p}r^{s+N+1/2},\quad\forall N\in\mathbb{N},

for some constants
C_{N}
.
We now write this as

\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{N}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq f_{N}(v)\max(r^{N+s-p},r_{0}^{N+s-p}),\quad\forall N\in\mathbb{N},

(10.30)

for
f_{N}(v)
continuously depending on
v
, and we will show show that there exists a sufficiently large global constant
C_{\mathrm{bs}}
(depending also on
M
), to be determined later, such that

\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell+1-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq C_{\mathrm{bs}}\max(r^{\ell+1-p},r_{0}^{\ell+1-p}).

(10.31)

Consider the set
X:=\{v\in[v_{1},\infty)\text{ s.t. \eqref{eq:al:bs1} holds }\forall v^{\prime}\leq v,u\leq u_{0}\}
.
If
C_{\mathrm{bs}}
is sufficiently large, then either
\sup_{X}=\infty
, or
\sup_{X}=v_{2}>v_{1}
is finite. If the first case holds, we are done, so let us assume the second case.

In view of (
10.11
), integrating (
10.31
) immediately implies

\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1-i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq C\cdot C_{\mathrm{bs}}\max(r^{\ell+1-i-p},r_{0}^{\ell+1-i-p})

(10.32)

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,\ell-s+1\}
.
Inserting these estimates for
i=1,2
into the approximate conservation law (
9.18
), we then obtain that

\displaystyle\left|r^{2}D^{\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\lesssim r^{2\ell+2}\int\frac{1}{r^{3+2\ell}}C_{\mathrm{bs}}\max(r^{\ell-p},r_{0}^{\ell-p})\lesssim C_{\mathrm{bs}}\max(r^{\ell-p},r_{0}^{\ell-p}),

(10.33)

where we used that the boundary term at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
vanishes.
Hence, recalling the definition of
\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
, (
9.17
) and using the estimates (
10.31
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\lesssim C_{\mathrm{bs}}\max(r^{\ell-p},r_{0}^{\ell-p}).

(10.34)

Estimate (
10.34
) improves (
10.31
) within
X
, provided that
C_{\mathrm{bs}}
(and thus
v_{2}
) was chosen sufficiently large. We can now integrate (
10.30
) with
N=\ell+2-s
from
v=v_{2}
to
v=v_{2}+\delta
for a sufficiently small distance
\delta>0
to show that
(v_{2}+\delta)\in X
, a contradiction. Thus,
\sup_{X}=\infty
, and (
10.34
) holds throughout
\mathcal{D}
.
∎

10.6.2
An asymptotic estimate for
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}

Next, we upgrade (
10.29
) to an asymptotic expression for
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
:

Proposition 10.4
.

The solution from Theorem
10.1
satisfies the following estimates throughout
\mathcal{D}
:
If
\ell-p>-1
, then

\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}=2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}(2x^{[s]}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0})\cdot\frac{(\ell-p)!(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell+2)!}\cdot r^{\ell-p}\\
+M\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r_{0}+M(1+\delta_{\ell-p,0}\log r/r_{0})}{r^{1-(\ell-p)}}+\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p+1}}{r}+\frac{\delta_{\ell-p-\epsilon,-1}\log(r/r_{0})+1}{r^{\epsilon-(\ell-p)}}\right).

(10.35)

If
\ell-p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
, then we have more precisely:

\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}=2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}(2x^{[s]}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0})\cdot\frac{(\ell-p)!(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell+2)!}\cdot r^{\ell-p}\\
\cdot\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-p}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{i}(\ell+1+p+j)\right)+M\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{M(1+\delta_{\ell-p,0}\log r/r_{0})}{r^{1-(\ell-p)}}+\frac{\delta_{\ell-p-\epsilon,-1}\log(r/r_{0})+1}{r^{\epsilon-(\ell-p)}}\right).

(10.36)

Note that
\frac{(2x^{[s]}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0})}{(2\ell+2)!}=-\frac{1}{2(2\ell)!}
.

If
\ell-p=-1
, then

\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}=2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}(2x^{[s]}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0})\cdot\frac{\log(r/r_{0})}{r}+M\cdot\mathcal{O}(r^{-1}).

(10.37)

If
\ell-p<-1
, then

\left|\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq MC\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p+1}}{r}.

(10.38)

Finally, we have that

\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}=(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}+2Mx^{[s]}_{1,\ell}\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\cdot r_{0}^{\ell-p}\\
+M\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{\ell-p-\epsilon}+\max(r^{\ell-p-1},r_{0}^{\ell-p-1})(1+\delta_{l-p,1}\log r/r_{0}))

(10.39)

Proof.

Integrating the estimate (
10.29
) from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}-\left.(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\right|\lesssim M\cdot C\max(r^{\ell-p-1},r_{0}^{\ell-p-1})(1+\delta_{\ell-p,1}(\log r/r_{0})),

(10.40)

and integrating (
10.40
) from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, using also the estimates (
10.11
) to estimate terms on
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, gives

\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq Cr_{0}^{\ell-p-i}+M\cdot C\max(r^{\ell-p-i-1},r_{0}^{\ell-p-i-1})(1+\delta_{\ell-p-i,1}\log r/r_{0}).

(10.41)

(Here, we used Lemma
B.7
and the fact that
\frac{1}{r}\log\frac{r}{r_{0}}\lesssim\frac{1}{r_{0}}
.)
We can now prove the proposition by re-inserting the improved estimates (
10.40
), (
10.41
) into (
9.18
):

\not{\nabla}_{u}(r^{-2\ell}D^{\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell})=\frac{2M}{r^{2\ell+3}}(2x^{[s]}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0})(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\\
+\frac{M^{2}}{r^{2\ell+3}}\mathcal{O}(\max(r^{\ell-p-1},r_{0}^{\ell-p-1})(1+\delta_{\ell-p,1}\log r/r_{0})).

Integrating in
u
, we thus obtain that

\left|r^{-2\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}-\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{2Mr_{0}^{\ell-p}}{r^{2\ell+3}}(2x^{[s]}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right|\\
\leq\frac{M^{2}C(1+\delta_{\ell-p,0}\log r/r_{0})}{r^{\ell+3+p}}+\frac{MC(1+\delta_{\ell-p-\epsilon,-1}\log\tfrac{r}{r_{0}})}{r^{\ell+2+p+\epsilon}},

(10.42)

where we used Lemmata
B.1
–
B.4
to estimate the RHS (note, in particular, that
\int r^{-N}\log r/r_{0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\leq r^{-N+1}
for any
N\geq 1
by Lemma
B.4
).

We now need to evaluate the integral on the LHS of (
10.42
).
If
\ell-p<-1
, then (
10.38
) follows immediately.
If
\ell-p=-1
, then (
10.37
) follows from Lemma
B.2
.
If
\ell-p>-1
, then (
10.35
) follows from Lemma
B.1
, (
B.1
).

The refined statement (
10.36
) for
\ell-p\in\mathbb{N}
follows from (
B.2
), which implies:

\int\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p}}{r^{2\ell+3}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u=\frac{(\ell-p)!(\ell+1+p)!}{(2\ell+2)!}\frac{1}{r^{\ell+2+p}}\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-p}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{i}\frac{(\ell+1+p+i)!}{(\ell+1+p)!}+\mathcal{O}(M/r)\right).

∎

10.6.3
Asymptotics for
\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
and higher derivatives

We now compute the asymptotics for
\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
. Even though we could just deduce them from the asymptotics of
\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
, we find it easier to compute them directly.

Proposition 10.5
.

The solution from Theorem
10.1
satisfies the following estimates throughout
\mathcal{D}
:
If
\ell-p>0
, then

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=-M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{(\ell-p)!(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell)!}r^{\ell-p}+M\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{\ell-p}+(r_{0}+M)r^{\ell-p-1})\\
+M^{2}\mathcal{O}(\max(r^{\ell-p-1},r_{0}^{\ell-p-1}))+M\mathcal{O}(\max(r^{\ell-p-\epsilon},r_{0}^{\ell-p-\epsilon})(1+\delta_{\ell-p,\epsilon}\log r/r_{0})).

(10.43)

If
\ell-p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}
, then we have more precisely:

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=-M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}+a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1}}{\ell+1}r_{0}^{\ell-p}-M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{(\ell-p)!(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell)!}r^{\ell-p}\\
\cdot\left(1-\delta_{\ell-p,0}+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-p}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{i}(\ell+1+p+j)\right)+M\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{M}{r^{1-(\ell-p)}}+\frac{\delta_{\ell-p,\epsilon}\log(r/r_{0})+1}{r^{\epsilon-(\ell-p)}}\right).

(10.44)

If
\ell-p<0
, then, for all
1\leq i<\lceil p-\ell\rceil+1
, the limit of
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
as
v\to\infty
exists, and

\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\mathscr{E}\cdot Mr_{0}^{\ell-p+i-1}+M\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{\ell-p+i-2}+r_{0}^{\ell-p+i-1-\epsilon}),

(10.45)

for some constants
\mathscr{E}
whose values we don’t determine.
For
1\leq i\leq\lceil p-\ell\rceil
, we have

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}+M\mathcal{O}(r^{-1}r_{0}^{\ell-p+i}),

(10.46)

and, for
i=\lceil p-\ell\rceil+1
, we have

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\\
+M\cdot\mathscr{E}r^{\ell-p-1+\lceil p-\ell\rceil}(1+\delta_{p-\ell,\lceil p-\ell\rceil}\log r/r_{0})+M\mathcal{O}(r^{-1}r_{0}^{\ell-p+1}).

(10.47)

Proof.

The first part of the proof is very similar to the proof of Proposition
10.4
, with the difference that now we want to integrate (
9.6
) rather than (
9.18
).
Recall the estimate (
10.40
): Integrating it, we get

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}+\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\\
+M\mathcal{O}(\max(r^{\ell-p-2},r_{0}^{\ell-p-2})(1+\delta_{\ell-p,2}\log r/r_{0})).

(10.48)

We insert these estimates into (
9.6
), with
N=\ell-s
, to obtain

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{u}\left(\frac{D^{\ell}}{r^{2\ell}}\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right)

(10.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2M}{r}\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{\ell+1}(c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}+a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1})\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

\displaystyle+2Ma^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1}\left(\frac{D}{r^{2}}\right)^{\ell+1}\left(\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}+\frac{1}{r_{0}}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\right)

\displaystyle+M^{2}r^{-2\ell-2}\mathcal{O}(\max(r^{\ell-p-2},r_{0}^{\ell-p-2})(1+\delta_{\ell-p,2}\log r/r_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2M(c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}+a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1})C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p}}{r^{2\ell+3}}+a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1}(C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s-1,p)}+C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)})\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p-1}}{r^{2\ell+2}}

\displaystyle+M^{2}r^{-2\ell-2}\mathcal{O}(\max(r^{\ell-p-2},r_{0}^{\ell-p-2})(1+\delta_{\ell-p,2}\log r/r_{0})+M\mathcal{O}(\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p-1-\epsilon}}{r^{2\ell+2}}).

Now, since

\displaystyle\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p}}{r^{2\ell+3}}=\partial_{u}\left(\frac{1}{2\ell+2}\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p}}{r^{2\ell+2}}\right)+\frac{\ell-p}{2\ell+2}\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p-1}}{r^{2\ell+2}}+M\mathcal{O}(\tfrac{r_{0}^{\ell-p-2}}{r^{2\ell+2}}),

and since
C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s-1,p)}=-\frac{\ell+p}{2\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}
, integrating (
10.49
) gives

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=-2M\frac{c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}+a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1}}{2(\ell+1)}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}r_{0}^{\ell-p}\\
-2MC^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\left(\frac{\ell-p}{2(\ell+1)}(c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}+a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1})-a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1}(1-\frac{\ell+p}{2\ell})\right)r^{2\ell+2}\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p-1}}{r^{2\ell+2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\\
+M^{2}\mathcal{O}(\max(r^{\ell-p-1},r_{0}^{\ell-p-1}))+M\mathcal{O}(\max(r^{\ell-p-\epsilon},r_{0}^{\ell-p-\epsilon})(1+\delta_{\ell-p,\epsilon}\log r/r_{0})).

(10.50)

Note that the second line in the estimate above vanishes if
\ell-p=0
. (The integral on its own would produce a logarithmic leading order term in that case!) Indeed, we have the following simple expression:

\left(\frac{\ell-p}{2(\ell+1)}(c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}+a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1})-a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1}(1-\frac{\ell+p}{2\ell})\right)=\frac{\ell-p}{2(\ell+1)}(c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}+a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1})-\frac{\ell+1}{\ell}a^{[s]}_{\ell-s,1})\\
=\frac{\ell-p}{2(\ell+1)}((1+s)(1+2s)+3(\ell-s)(\ell+1+s)-(\ell-s)(\ell+s))=\frac{(\ell-p)(2\ell+1)}{2}.

(10.51)

The statements (
10.43
) and (
10.44
) then follow from Lemma
B.1
.

In order to prove the remaining statements, we proceed inductively. Assume that
\ell-p<0
.
Our induction assumption is the following:
For all
1\leq i<\lceil p-\ell\rceil
, the limit
\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}(u,v)
exists, and we have the estimates

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}\cdot Mr_{0}^{\ell-p+i-1}+M\mathcal{O}(Mr_{0}^{\ell-p+i-2}+r_{0}^{\ell-p+i-1-\epsilon}),

(10.52)

\displaystyle(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}+M\mathcal{O}(r^{-1}r_{0}^{\ell-p+i}).

(10.53)

We first establish the case
i=1
: If
\ell-p<0
, then (
10.50
) implies that
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=M\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{\ell-p})
. We moreover have that
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\ell-p})
from (
10.40
). Inserting both of these estimates into (
9.6
) with
N=\ell-s+1
and integrating in
u
, we then obtain that

\displaystyle\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=M\mathcal{O}\left(r^{2\ell+2}\int\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p}}{r^{2\ell+4}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq M\mathcal{O}\left(\max\left(\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p+1}}{r^{2}},r^{\ell-p-1}(1+\delta_{\ell-p,-1}\log r/r_{0})\right)\right).

Since
\ell-p<0
, the RHS is integrable in
v
, and hence
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
attains a limit at
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
If
1<\lceil p-\ell\rceil
, then (
10.52
) and (
10.53
) follow directly by integrating the above from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, also taking into account (
10.14
).
On the other hand, if
0>\ell-p\geq-1
, then we obtain that
15
15

15

Here, we use that, for any
q>0
,

-(q-1)\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\frac{\log(r^{\prime}/r_{0})}{r^{\prime q}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}=\frac{\log(r/r_{0})}{r^{q-1}}+\frac{1}{q-1}\left(\frac{1}{r^{q-1}}-\frac{1}{r_{0}^{q-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}\cdot Mr_{0}^{\ell-p}+M\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{\ell-p+1-2}+r_{0}^{\ell-p+1-1-\epsilon}),

(10.54)

\displaystyle(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}+M\mathcal{O}(r^{\ell-p}(1+\delta_{\ell-p,-1}\log r/r_{0})).

(10.55)

Inserting the expressions (
10.54
) and (
10.55
) back into (
9.6
) with
N=\ell-s+1
then gives that

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\lim_{v\to\infty}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\\
+\mathscr{E}\cdot Mr^{\ell-p}(1+\delta_{\ell-p,-1}\log r/r_{0}))+M\mathcal{O}(r^{-1}r_{0}^{\ell-p+1}).

(10.56)

We have at this point proved (
10.45
)–(
10.47
) for
i=1
.

Let now
\ell-p<-1
, and let us inductively assume that there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq n<\lceil p-\ell\rceil-1
such that (
10.52
) and (
10.53
) hold for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
We will show that (
10.52
) and (
10.53
) then also hold for
i=n+1
.
For this, we insert the estimates (
10.52
), (
10.53
) into (
9.6
) with
N=\ell-s+n
and integrate in
u
; this gives:

\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+n}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=r^{2\ell+2n+2}M\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\int\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p+n-1}}{r^{2\ell+2n+2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right)=M\cdot\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{\ell-p+n}).

(10.57)

We now insert this estimate into (
9.6
) with
N=\ell-s+n+1
, which similarly gives that

\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+n+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=r^{2\ell+2n+4}M\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\int\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p+n}}{r^{2\ell+2n+4}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right)=M\cdot\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{\ell-p+n+1}).

(10.58)

Integrating (
10.58
) from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
(where we use estimate (
10.14
)) then establishes that
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+n+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
attains a limit at

, which satisfies (

). Integrating (
10.58
) from
\mathcal{I}^{+}
then shows that (
10.53
) holds as well, thus completing the inductive argument.

In order to show the final missing part of the proposition, namely to show that (
10.45
) holds for
i=\lceil p-\ell\rceil+1
and that (
10.47
) holds, we proceed in the same way in which we proved (

)–(
10.56
).
∎

10.6.4
Asymptotic estimates for
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{j}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
for
j\leq\ell-s

We now compute the asymptotic expressions for all the lower order derivatives.
In particular, this requires explicitly solving the Minkowskian problem, i.e. it requires explicitly solving the Teukolsky equation for
M=0
.
We begin with the following simple observation:

Proposition 10.6
.

Let
j\in\{0,\dots,\ell-s\}
. The following identity holds for any smooth
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
:

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}(u,v)=\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\\
+\underbrace{\int_{v_{1}}^{v}\frac{D}{r^{2}}\cdots\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{(j)}}\frac{D}{r^{2}}}_{\text{$j+1$ integrals}}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v_{(j+1)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v_{1}.

(10.59)

Notice that the second line in (
10.59
) vanishes for
M=0
.

Proof.

Fundamental theorem of calculus and Lemma
B.5
.
∎

We now make use of Proposition
10.6
to obtain an asymptotic estimate for all lower-order derivatives, and, in particular, for
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
itself. We first turn our attention to the second line of (
10.59
).

Proposition 10.7
.

Let
j\in\{0,\dots,\ell-s\}
. The solution of Theorem
10.1
satisfies the following estimates:
If
\ell-p>0
and if
j<\ell-p-1
, then

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\\
-M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{(\ell-p)(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell)!}(\ell-2-p-j)!r^{\ell-1-p-j}\\
\cdot(1+\mathcal{O}((r_{0}/r)^{\ell-1-p-j}+r_{0}/r(1+\delta_{\ell-2-p,j}\log r/r_{0})+r^{-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{\ell-p-1-\epsilon,j}\log r/r_{0}))).

(10.60)

If
\ell-p>0
and
j=\ell-p-1
, then

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\\
-M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{(\ell-p)(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell)!}\left(\log(r/r_{0})+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}/r\log r/r_{0}+r^{-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{\epsilon,1}\log r/r_{0}))\right).

(10.61)

If
j>\ell-p-1
, then

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\\
+M\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{(\ell-1-p)-j}+(\ell-p)r^{\ell-1-p-j}(1+\delta_{\ell-p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\neq 0}}\log r/r_{0})).

(10.62)

Proof.

The proof follows from taking the expression (
10.59
) and computing the integrals in the second line of (
10.59
) by inserting the relevant estimates from Proposition
10.5
and then applying Lemmata
B.5
–
B.7
.

As an example, to prove (
10.60
), we insert (
10.43
) into (
10.59
), and we then compute the arising integral using Lemma
B.5
and then (
B.9
) of Lemma
B.7
.
∎

Remark 10.5
.

Starting from the more precise estimate (
10.44
) in the case of
\ell-p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we can also show in exactly the same way in which we proved the estimates of Proposition
10.7
that (setting
\epsilon=\infty
for simplicity)

\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-s}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\\
+Mr_{0}^{s-p-1}\sum_{n=0}^{\ell-s+1}(Q_{n}+S^{\prime}_{n}\log r_{0}/r+\mathcal{O}(Mr_{0}^{-1}))\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}

for some (potentially vanishing) constants
Q_{n}
,
S^{\prime}_{n}
that we do not yet determine. We will return to this point later, when we need to compute higher-order asymptotics (cf. §
13
).

We now turn our attention to the first line of (
10.59
):

Proposition 10.8
.

Let
j\in\{0,\dots,\ell-s\}
. The solution of Theorem
10.1
satisfies the following estimates:

\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0}^{\ell-j-p}\sum_{n=0}^{j}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\cdot(S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}+\mathcal{O}_{u}(r_{0}^{-\epsilon}+Mr_{0}^{-1})),

(10.63)

where the constants
S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}
are defined for
0\leq n\leq j\leq\ell-s
via

S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}:=\frac{(-1)^{\ell-s+n}(s+p)!(\ell-s)!(2\ell-j+n)!}{n!(\ell+s)!(\ell+p)!}\binom{\ell-p}{\ell-p-j+n}.

(10.64)

Note that

S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}=0\iff\,\,\,(\ell-p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,n<j+p-\ell).

(10.65)

Proof.

For simpler notation, we consider the case where
M=0
and
\epsilon=\infty
.
Inserting the expressions from Proposition
10.1
, we have

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

(10.66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}(-1)^{\ell-s-j}r_{0}^{\ell-j-p}\frac{(s+p)!(\ell-s)!}{(\ell+s)!}\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{(-1)^{i}}{i!}\left(1-\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{i}\frac{(2\ell-j+i)!}{(\ell-j+i+p)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}(-1)^{\ell-s-j}r_{0}^{\ell-j-p}\frac{(s+p)!(\ell-s)!}{(\ell+s)!}\sum_{n=0}^{j}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\sum_{i=0}^{j}\binom{i}{n}\frac{(-1)^{i+n}}{i!}\frac{(2\ell-j+i)!}{(\ell-j+i+p)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0}^{\ell-j-p}\sum_{n=0}^{j}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\cdot S_{\ell,p,j,n,s},

where
S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}
are constants that we now determine by observing that the expression above, for
j=\ell-s
, solves (
Teuk:s
) with
M=0
. This is because it is exactly the first line of (
10.59
). In other words, it solves equation (
14.3
) from §
14
.

We can thus appeal to Proposition
14.1
:
16
16

16

For the reader who cannot be bothered to skip to section
14
, all we are doing here is inserting the above expression into the Teukolsky equation with
M=0
and solving the resulting recurrence relations for the coefficients
S_{\ell,p,j=\ell-s,n,s}
.

Since
(r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=(r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}
, we infer the value of
S_{\ell-s}(=S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n=\ell-s,s})
in (
14.8
) via (
14.9
):
S_{\ell-s}=\frac{(s+p)!}{(\ell+p)!}\frac{(2\ell)!}{(\ell+s)!}
. Equation (
10.64
) thus follows for
j=\ell-s
.

In order to compute the constants
S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}
for
j<\ell-s
, we simply observe that

S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}=(-1)^{\ell-s-j}\frac{(n+\ell-s-j)!}{n!}S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n+\ell-s-j,s},

as can be seen by differentiating the expression (
10.63
), which, for
M=0
, is exactly the LHS of (
10.59
).

Considering now the case
M\neq 0\neq\epsilon
gives the error terms
\mathcal{O}(Mr_{0}^{-1}+r_{0}^{-\epsilon})
.
∎

10.7
Asymptotic expansion for
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
and the proofs of Thm.
10.1
and Cor.
10.1

We combine the results of the previous sections into the following proposition:

Proposition 10.9
.

The scattering solution
\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}
described in Theorem
10.1
satisfies the following expansion throughout
\mathcal{D}
: If
\ell-p\geq 0
, then

\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0}^{s-p}\sum_{n=0}^{\max(\ell-s,\lceil p-s\rceil)}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}(S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}+\mathcal{O}_{u}(r_{0}^{-\epsilon}+Mr_{0}^{-1}))\\
-M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell)!}\cdot r^{s-1-p}\\
\cdot\begin{cases}(s-p-2)!\{(\ell-p)&\\
+\mathcal{O}((\tfrac{r_{0}}{r})^{\lceil p\rceil-p}+\tfrac{r_{0}}{r}(1+\delta_{s-2-p,0}\log r/r_{0})+r^{-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{s-p-1,\epsilon}\log r/r_{0}))\},&1+p-s\notin\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}\\
\frac{(-1)^{p+1-s}}{(p+1-s)!}((\ell-p)\log r/r_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\log r/r_{0}+r^{-\epsilon}(1+\delta_{\epsilon,1}\log r/r_{0})),&1+p-s\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}.\end{cases}

(10.67)

If, on the other hand,
\ell-p<0
, then we can define
S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}
to be zero whenever
n>j
, and the first two lines of the above formula still apply. The form of the third line also still applies, that is, the third line still applies if the constants are replaced by some undetermined coefficients.

Proof.

If
\ell-s\leq\ell-p-1
, i.e., if
s\geq p+1
and
\ell>p+1
, then the result directly follows from setting
j=\ell-s
in (
10.60
) or (
10.61
).

If
\ell-p>0
and
\ell-s>\ell-p-1
, then we integrate either (
10.60
) or (
10.61
), with
j=\ell-\lceil p+1\rceil
,
\lceil p+1\rceil-s
times from
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(instead of from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}})
, using (
10.62
) to estimate the boundary terms on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

If
\ell-p\leq 0
, then we integrate instead the expressions from Proposition
10.5
an appropriate amount of times from
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
∎

This concludes the proof of Thm.
10.1
.
We finish the section by proving its Corollary
10.1
:

Proof of Cor.
10.1
.

In order to prove the corollary, we repeat the proof of Theorem
10.1
, but instead of using the approximate conservation law (
9.18
) for
\alpha^{[s]}
, we appeal to the approximate conservation law (
9.25
) for
\Psi^{[s]}
.
Of course, if
s=0
, then
\Psi^{[s]}=\alpha^{[s]}
and the result trivially follows.
But if we now consider the Regge–Wheeler equation for general spin
s\neq 0
, we can simply exploit that the coefficients
a^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,0}
and
b^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{N,0}
as well as the eigenvalues of
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}_{[s]}-\Lambda_{0}^{[s]}
in (
9.25
) are independent of
s
, so the contribution in the first line of (
10.67
), which is entirely Minkowskian, is as for
s=0
.

The contribution in the last two lines of (
10.67
), on the other hand, is generated by integrating (
9.18
) or (
9.25
) to obtain the leading-order behaviour of
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
or
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{[s]}_{\ell}
, respectively. This, in turn, is now governed by the coefficient
2x^{[s]}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}
in the case of
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}
(cf. Prop.
10.4
), or, in the case of
\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{\mathbf{\Psi}}^{[s]}_{\ell}
, by the coefficient
2x^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{\ell,0}
. But since
2x^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s],\mathrm{RW}}_{\ell,0}=2x^{[s]}_{1,\ell}-c^{[s]}_{\ell-s,0}=-(\ell+1)(2\ell+1)
is independent of
s
, the result is then identical to that of
s=0
.

∎

11
Asymptotics for
\alpha^{[2]}=r^{5}\Omega^{-2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\alpha}}
arising from the physical data of §
8

We now apply the results of the previous section to find the asymptotics of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
arising from the physical data described in §
8
(cf. e.g. Prop.
8.2
). Recall from (
8.19
) that, in the case of
hyperbolic
orbits (Def.
8.2
) these data satisfy the following expansion along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
:

r^{-s}\upalpha^{[s=2]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\frac{r^{3}}{\Omega^{2}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{A}+\mathscr{B}\frac{\log r_{0}}{r_{0}}+\frac{\mathscr{C}}{r_{0}}+\mathcal{O}_{3}(r_{0}^{-1-\delta}),

(11.1)

with

\mathscr{B}=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}},\qquad\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{5}

(11.2)

The presence of the logarithmic term in (
11.1
) necessitates a minor modification of the proof of Theorem
10.1
. The description of this modification and the result of it is the content of this section.

In contrast, the treatment of the case of
parabolic
orbits (cf. Def.
8.3
) is already included in Theorem
10.1
, using (
8.31
), we therefore won’t discuss it beyond the statement of Thm.
11.2
below.

11.1
The main theorem (Thm.
11.1
)

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
be the unique, smooth scattering solution to (
Teuk
) arising from the physical data of Def.
8.2
from §
8
, i.e. arising from (
10.2
) with
s=2
(no incoming radiation) and (
11.1
) in the sense of Def.
10.1
.
Then each angular mode
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}
of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
satisfies the following asymptotic expansion throughout
\mathcal{D}
:

\displaystyle\frac{r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}}{\Omega^{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\ell-2}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\{\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0}^{2}S_{\ell,0,\ell-2,n,2}

\displaystyle+\mathscr{B}_{\ell}r_{0}\log r_{0}S_{\ell,1,\ell-2,n,2}

(11.3)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}^{u}(M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r_{0})+\mathcal{O}^{u}(\mathscr{B}_{\ell}r_{0})+\mathcal{O}^{u}(\mathcal{C}_{\ell}r_{0})+\mathcal{O}^{u}(r_{0}^{1-\delta})\}

\displaystyle-\frac{(-1)^{\ell}2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}r}{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}-\frac{(-1)^{\ell}{3}M\mathscr{B}_{\ell}\log^{2}r}{\ell(\ell+1)}

\displaystyle+M\log r(O(r_{0}\mathscr{A}_{\ell})+\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{B}_{\ell})+\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C}_{\ell})),

where the constants
S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}
are defined in (
10.64
).

Moreover, the following limits are conserved along
\mathcal{I}^{+}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}^{2-\ell,0}_{\ell}[\alpha^{[2]}]=\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2-\ell}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[2]}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell+1}2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}\frac{(\ell-2)!(\ell+1)}{(\ell+2)!},

(11.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{4}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell+1}\frac{2M\mathscr{A}_{\ell}}{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}.

(11.5)

For parabolic orbits, we content ourselves with the following

Theorem 11.2
.

Let
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
be the unique, smooth scattering solution to (
Teuk
) arising from the physical data of Def.
8.3
from §
8
, i.e. arising from (
10.2
), (
10.3
) with
s=2
and (
8.31
) (so
p=2/3
in (
10.4
)).
Then each angular mode
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}
of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}
satisfies

\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{\frac{14}{3}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell+1}\sqrt{3}\pi\overline{\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathrm{par},\ell}}(\ell+\tfrac{5}{3})(\ell-\tfrac{2}{3}).

(11.6)

11.2
Proof of Theorem
11.1

Proof.

This is essentially an application of Theorem
10.1
with
s=2
and a superposition of
(p=0,\epsilon=1+\delta)
and
(p=1,\epsilon=\infty)
.
The only modification comes from the logarithmic term in (
11.1
),
\mathscr{B}r_{0}^{-1}\log r_{0}
.
Throughout the rest of this proof, let us therefore assume scattering data satisfying the no incoming radiation condition (Def.
10.2
) and

\displaystyle r^{-s}\upalpha^{[s]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\mathscr{B}r_{0}^{-p}\log r_{0}+\mathscr{C}r_{0}^{-p},

\displaystyle p>\max(-1/2,-s+1).

(11.7)

For convenience, we keep the exponent
p
general for now and only restrict to
p=1
in the end.
In order to avoid case distinctions, we will in the propositions below also make the additional assumption that
\ell>p
.

For logarithmically decaying data as in (
11.7
), we first need an analogue of Proposition
10.1
:

Proposition 11.1
.

For
N\leq\ell-s
, transversal derivatives along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
satisfy:

\left.{(D^{-1}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{N}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=C^{\nearrow}_{(N,p)}r_{0}^{N-p+s}\log r_{0}\cdot\left(\mathscr{B}_{\ell}+\frac{\mathscr{B}_{\ell}}{\log r_{0}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{k+s+p}+\frac{\mathscr{C}_{\ell}}{\log r_{0}}\right)\\
+\mathcal{O}(Mr_{0}^{N-p+s-1}\log r_{0}).

(11.8)

Proof.

The proof goes by induction as in Proposition
10.1
, using that

\int_{-\infty}^{u}r_{0}^{-k-s-p-1}\log r_{0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=\frac{r_{0}^{-k-s-p}}{k+s+p}\left(\log r_{0}+\frac{1}{k+s+p}\right)+M\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-k-s-p-1}\log r_{0}).

∎

Analogously to how we proved Proposition
10.3
, we then show that

\left|(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}\right|\leq MC\max(r^{\ell-p}\log r,r_{0}^{\ell-p}\log r_{0}),

(11.9)

and, integrating this from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, we obtain a logarithmically adorned version of (
10.40
).

We can now prove analogues of Propositions
10.4
and
10.5
:

Proposition 11.2
.

Suppose that
\ell-p>0
.
Then

\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{r^{p-\ell}}{\log r}\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\mathrm{A}^{[s]}_{\ell}=-M\mathscr{B}_{\ell}C^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{(\ell-p)!(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell)!},

(11.10)

and

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s+1}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}=-MC^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{(\ell-p)!(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell)!}r^{\ell-p}\log r\\
\cdot\left(\mathscr{B}_{\ell}\left(1+\frac{1}{\log r}\left(d_{2\ell+3,\ell-p}+\sum_{k={1}}^{\ell-s}\frac{1}{k+s+p}\right)\right)+{\mathscr{C}_{\ell}}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}/r)\right),

(11.11)

where
d_{2\ell+3,\ell-p}
is defined in Lemma
B.1
. If
p\in\mathbb{N}
, then
d_{2\ell+3,\ell-p}=-\sum_{k=\ell-p+1}^{\ell+p+1}\frac{1}{k}
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds in the same manner as the proof of Proposition
10.4
, with the difference that we now need to compute the integral

\displaystyle\int\left(\mathscr{B}_{\ell}\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p}\log r_{0}}{r^{2\ell+3}}+\left(\mathscr{B}_{\ell}\sum_{k={1}}^{\ell-s}\frac{1}{k+s+p}+\mathscr{C}_{\ell}\right)\frac{r_{0}^{\ell-p}}{r^{2\ell+3}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u,

which we do using (
B.1
) and (
B.3
) for the second and first term, respectively.
∎

We are left with having to integrate (
11.11
)
\ell+1
times from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
. For the Minkowskian part of the expression, we have

Proposition 11.3
.

Suppose that
\ell-p\geq 0
and that
p\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-s}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

(11.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathscr{B}_{\ell}\log r_{0}+\mathscr{C}_{\ell})r_{0}^{s-p}\sum_{n=0}^{\ell-s}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\cdot S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}(1+\mathcal{O}_{u}(Mr_{0}^{-1}))

\displaystyle+\mathscr{B}_{\ell}r_{0}^{s-p}\sum_{n=0}^{p-s-1}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}R_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}

\displaystyle+\mathscr{B}_{\ell}r_{0}^{s-p}\sum_{n=p-s}^{\ell-s}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-s-1-n}\frac{1}{\ell-p-k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-s}\frac{1}{k+s+p}\right),

where

R_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}:=(-1)^{\ell-p+1}\frac{(\ell-p)!(\ell-s)!(p+s)!}{(\ell+p)!(\ell+s)!}\frac{(p-n-s-1)!(\ell+n+s)!}{n!(\ell-n-s)!}.

(11.13)

Proof.

As in the proof of Prop.
10.8
, we show the result for
M=0
: Let
j\in\{0,\dots,\ell-s\}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}\left.{(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{\ell-s-j+i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}

(11.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathscr{B}\log r_{0}+\mathscr{C})r_{0}^{\ell-j-p}\sum_{n=0}^{j}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\cdot S_{\ell,p,j,n,s}

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\ell-s-j}r_{0}^{\ell-j-p}\frac{(s+p)!(\ell-s)!}{(\ell+s)!}

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{j}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\sum_{i=0}^{j}\binom{i}{n}\frac{(-1)^{i+n}}{i!}\frac{(2\ell-j+i)!}{(\ell-j+i+p)!}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-s-j+i}\frac{1}{k+p+s}\right).

For
j=\ell-s
, we now apply Proposition
14.2
to get a simpler expression for the last line: By comparing (
11.8
), with
N=\ell-s
, and (
14.14
), we can read off the constants
S_{\ell-s}
and
R_{\ell-s}
; in particular, we have
R_{\ell-s}/S_{\ell-s}=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-s}\frac{1}{k+s+p}
. The result then follows.
∎

Lastly, we prove the analogue of Proposition
10.7
:

Proposition 11.4
.

Let
\ell-p\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Then

\frac{r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\alpha}}_{\ell}}{\Omega^{2}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-s}\frac{1}{i!}\left(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r}\right)^{i}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{i}\alpha^{[s]}_{\ell}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}\\
-MC^{\nearrow}_{(\ell-s,p)}\frac{(\ell-p)(\ell+p+1)!}{(2\ell)!}\frac{(-1)^{p+1-s}}{{2}(p+1-s)!}r^{s-1-p}\\
\cdot\left(\mathscr{B}_{\ell}\log^{2}r+\log r\left(\mathscr{C}_{\ell}+\mathscr{B}_{\ell}\left(1-\sum_{k=1}^{p+1-s}\frac{1}{k}-\sum_{k=\ell-p+1}^{\ell+p+1}\frac{1}{k}+\sum_{k=1+s+p}^{\ell+p}\frac{1}{k}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}r_{0})\right).

(11.15)

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Proposition
10.7
, with the difference being that we now have to appeal to Lemma
B.8
as well (which is responsible for the
\mathscr{B}_{\ell}(\log^{2}r+\log r(1-\sum_{k=1}^{p+1-s}\frac{1}{k}))
-term).
∎

Superposing Theorem
10.1
with
p=0
and
\epsilon=1+\delta
with the combined results of Propositions
10.6
,
11.3
and
11.4
with
p=1
proves (
11.3
).
The other two equations (
11.4
) and (
11.5
) follow directly from Thm.
10.1
(as they are independent of
\mathscr{B}
).
∎

Asymptotics for

arising from the physical data of §

We now apply the results of §

to the solutions to the Regge–Wheeler equations
\Psi^{[\pm 2]}
arising from the physical data described in Def.

(cf. Prop.

). (Again, the case of Def.

is already contained in Cor.

.)
As we have seen in (
8.20
) and (
8.21
), respectively, both Regge–Wheeler quantities,
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}}=\Psi^{[2]}
and
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}=\Psi^{[-2]}
(and thus also
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\sigma}})=\frac{1}{4}(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\Psi}}-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\Psi}})
), will have the following expansion along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
:

{\bm{\Uppsi}}^{[s]}_{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}=\mathscr{P}^{1,[s]}+\mathscr{P}^{2,[s]}r_{0}^{-1}\log r_{0}+\mathscr{P}^{3,[s]}r_{0}^{-1}+\mathcal{O}_{1}(r_{0}^{-1-\delta}),

(12.1)

where the precise values of
\mathscr{P}^{3,[s]}
do not matter and where

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}^{1,[2]}=2\mathscr{A}=\overline{\mathscr{P}^{1,[-2]}},

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}^{2,[2]}=12\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}}=\overline{\mathscr{P}^{2,[2]}}.

(12.2)

Theorem 12.1
.

For
\ell\geq|s|
, the unique, smooth scattering solution
\Psi^{[s]}
restricting to (
12.1
) along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
and satisfying the no incoming radiation condition (
10.2
) at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
has the following asymptotic expansion for each angular mode
\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}
throughout
\mathcal{D}
:

\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}=\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{n}\left(\mathscr{P}^{1,[s]}_{\ell}S_{\ell,0,\ell,n,0}+\mathscr{P}^{2,[s]}_{\ell}S_{\ell,1,\ell,n,0}r_{0}^{-1}\log r_{0}+\mathcal{O}^{u}(r_{0}^{-1})\right)\\
+M(-1)^{\ell}\left(\ell(\ell+1)\mathscr{P}^{1,[s]}_{\ell}r^{-1}\log r/r_{0}-{\tfrac{1}{2}}(\ell-1)(\ell+2)\mathscr{P}^{2,[s]}_{\ell}r^{-2}\log^{2}r\right)+M\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}r_{0}\log r),

(12.3)

where the constants
S_{\ell,p,\ell-s,n,s}
are defined in (
10.64
).

Remark 12.1
.

Notice, in particular, that (since
S_{\ell,1,\ell,0,0}=0
)

\lim_{v\to\infty}\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}=\mathscr{P}^{1,[s]}_{\ell}S_{\ell,0,\ell,0,0}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-1})=(-1)^{\ell}\mathscr{P}^{1,[s]}_{\ell}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-1}),

(12.4)

and hence

\lim_{u\to-\infty}\,\lim_{v\to\infty}\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}=(-1)^{\ell}\lim_{v\to\infty}\,\lim_{u\to-\infty}\Psi^{[s]}_{\ell}.

(12.5)

This is, in essence, the antipodal matching condition at the level of linearised gravity. See also the discussion below Thm.
1.2
in the introduction.

Proof.

The statement follows by setting
s=0
in the proof of Theorem
11.1
, cf. the proof of Cor.
10.1
at the end of §
10
.
∎

13
Asymptotics for
\alpha^{[-2]}=r\Omega^{2}\underline{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scalebox{0.4}{(1)}}}}{{\alpha}}}
arising from the physical data of §
8

We now move to the asymptotic analysis of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
in the case of the physical data of Def.
8.2
of §
8
. (The cases of Definitions
8.3
and
8.4
, on the other hand, will only briefly be discussed at the end of the section.)
Recall from (
8.14
) that these satisfy the following expansion along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
:

r^{-s}\upalpha^{[s=-2]}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=r^{3}\Omega^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upalpha}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=-6\underline{\mathscr{B}}r^{-p}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-p-\delta})

(13.1)

with
p=1
.
We thus already see that, since the condition
p>-s-1
(cf. (
10.5
)) is violated (we have
p=1
and
s=-2
), Theorem
10.1
does not directly apply.

We recall that the condition of no incoming radiation (
10.2
) in the case
s<0
reads

\lim_{u\to-\infty}\not{\nabla}_{v}(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{|s|}\alpha^{[s]}\equiv 0.

(13.2)

It is thus clear that the specification of (
13.1
) and (
13.2
) does not suffice to determine a solution
\alpha^{[s]}
to the scattering problem if
s<0
; one needs to additionally specify all lower-order transversal derivatives
(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{i}\alpha^{[s]}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,|s|\}
as seed data at a sphere along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
, cf. Def.
10.1
.

In Theorem
10.1
, we simplified our lives by imposing
\not{\nabla}_{v}^{i}(r^{5}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}})|_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}=0
for
i=1,\dots s
, but this condition had no physical motivation.

In the present section, we instead discuss the physically motivated setup for the data of §
8
:
Since, for general
p
violating
p>-s-1
in (
13.1
), the integral along
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
of (
Teuk:s
) for
s=-2
diverges, the seed data for the transversal derivatives cannot be specified at
{\mathcal{S}_{\infty}}
. Instead, they have to be specified at the finite sphere
{\mathcal{S}_{1}}
(or at any other finite sphere).
In the context of our physical data, these data for transversal derivatives are encoded in (
8.21
) and (
8.22
) (recall the definitions (
3.37
)). We have:

\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v}\alpha^{[-2]}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=6(-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2})\underline{\mathscr{B}}\frac{\log r_{0}}{r_{0}^{2}}+\mathscr{C}_{8}r_{0}^{-2}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-2-\delta}),

(13.3)

(note that
(-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{B}})_{\ell}=\tilde{a}^{[-2]}_{0,0,\ell}\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\ell}=-(\ell-1)(\ell+2)\underline{\mathscr{B}}_{\ell}
) and

(\frac{r^{2}}{D}\not{\nabla}_{v})^{2}\alpha^{[-2]}|_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\bm{\Uppsi}}}_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}=2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}(2\underline{\mathscr{A}}+6\underline{\mathscr{B}}r_{0}^{-1}\log r_{0})+\mathscr{C}_{7}r_{0}^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-1-\delta}),

(13.4)

where
\underline{\mathscr{A}}
is such that
2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{A}}=\overline{\mathscr{A}}
.
Notice that

(2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\underline{\mathscr{A}})_{\ell}=\tilde{a}^{[-2]}_{0,0,\ell}\tilde{a}^{[-2]}_{1,0,\ell}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\ell}=\frac{(\ell+2)!}{(\ell-2)!}\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\ell}=\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{\ell}.

(13.5)

13.1
The main theorem (Thm.
13.1
)

With the preliminaries discussed in the preceding subsection, let us now state the main result of this section. We will write
6(-2\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2})(\underline{\mathscr{C}})=\mathscr{C}_{8}
(cf. (
13.3
)).

Theorem 13.1
.

Let
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
be the unique smooth scattering solution to (
Teuk
) arising from the physical data of Def.
8.2
, i.e. from (
13.2
) with
s=-2
and (
13.1
), (
13.3
)–(
13.4
). Then each angular mode
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}
of
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}
satisfies the following asymptotic expansion throughout
\mathcal{D}
:

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\alpha}}_{\ell}
