vanishes, we consider (
3.23
) together with (
17.6
); the result then follows from the vanishing of
\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}_{\ell}
.

Moving on, we note that (
3.22
), together with (
16.1
), (
17.3
) and (
17.9
) implies that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}_{\ell}\lesssim r^{-2}
.
Thus, integrating (
3.9a
) gives that
{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}_{\ell}\lesssim r^{-1}
; in particular,
\lim_{v\to\infty}{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}_{\ell}=0
.

We can similarly infer that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}_{\ell}\lesssim r^{-1}
by integrating (
16.16
).
Hence, by (
3.26
), the limit
\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}_{\ell}
also vanishes.

Lastly, (
17.18
) follows from integrating (
3.13
).

∎

17.4
Computing a few higher-order limits

For the sake of completeness, we here record the following higher-order limits:

Proposition 17.3
.

The Bondi normalised solution
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Bondi}}
satisfies for any
\ell\geq 2
:

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}:=\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\eta}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{v\to\infty}(r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell}-r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\Omega\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}_{\ell})),

(17.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\eta}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}},

(17.20)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\chi\right)}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=2(\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}^{\mathrm{E}}+\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}^{\mathrm{E}}),

(17.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\left(\Omega\mathrm{tr}\underline{\chi}\right)}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=-2\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\Omega\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}^{\mathrm{E}}_{\ell})-2\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}+2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\upeta}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}^{\mathrm{E}},

(17.22)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{3}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=(\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}})^{\mathrm{E}}+\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r\Omega\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\hat{\chi}}}^{\mathrm{E}}_{\ell}),

(17.23)

\displaystyle\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}:=\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=2\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\sum_{n=i+2}^{\ell+2}\underline{S}_{\ell,0,\ell+2,n,-2}\frac{i!}{i+1}+\mathcal{O}(\frac{\log r_{0}}{r_{0}}),

(17.24)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2)(r{\mathrm{tr}{\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\not{g}}}}_{\ell})

\displaystyle=2\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}-4\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{3}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{K}_{\ell},

(17.25)

\displaystyle\lim_{v\to\infty}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{b}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=-\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{\mathtt{g}}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}-2\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{{\hat{\upchi}}}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}.

(17.26)

All of these limits are to leading order constant in
u
, with the third one and the last two ones being exactly constant in
u
. (Cf. Conclusion 17.0.5 in
[
CK93
]
.)

Proof.

Equation (
17.19
) is as in (
17.9
); notice that the difference coming from the subtraction of
\mathfrak{S}_{\underline{f}^{\mathcal{I}^{+}}}
is subleading (it’s
\mathcal{O}(r_{0}^{-1}\log r_{0})
).

Equation (
17.20
) then follows from integrating (
3.19b
) from
\mathcal{I}^{+}
. Alternatively, it follows from (
3.12
) and the fact that
\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\omega}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-3}\log r)
towards
\mathcal{I}^{+}
(cf. Cor.
17.2
).

Equation (
17.21
) follows from (
3.22
); similarly, (
17.22
) follows from (
3.23
), and then also using (
17.6
) as well as
\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}(r^{2}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\underline{\beta}}_{\ell})^{\mathrm{E}}=-\mathring{\not{\nabla}}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\uprho}_{\ell,\mathcal{I}^{+}}
. One similarly deduces that the RHS of (
17.21
) is constant in
u
.

Equation (
17.23
) follows from (
3.25
) and the previous expressions.

Equation (
17.24
) follows from integrating (
16.16
) using (
B.3
) and taking the limit as
r\to\infty
. Equation (
17.25
) then follows from (
3.26
).

Equation (
17.26
) follows from multiplying (
3.10b
) with
r^{2}
and using that
\lim_{v\to\infty}r^{2}\not{\nabla}_{v}\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}_{\ell}=-\lim_{v\to\infty}r\accentset{\scalebox{.6}{\mbox{\tiny(1)}}}{\hat{\not{g}}}_{\ell}
.

The fact that the last two limits are conserved can either be seen directly, or by using (
3.9a
), (
3.13
), respectively.

∎

Appendices

Appendix A
Spin-weighted functions, the Christodoulou–Klainerman formalism and the Newman–Penrose formalism

In this section, we relate the formalism of the present paper to the Newman–Penrose formalism by defining spin-weighted quantities, the spin-weighted spherical harmonics and by explicitly writing down a dictionary between the notation used by Newman and Penrose and that of the present paper (which goes back to Christodoulou and Klainerman). We begin with a few calculations.

A.1
Miscellaneous calculations on
\mathbb{S}^{2}

We work with the metric
\mathring{\not{g}}=d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}d\varphi^{2}
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
We recall from (
2.32
) that any symmetric tracefree two-tensor
\alpha
can be written as
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)
for two smooth functions
f
and
g
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
that are uniquely specified up to
\ell\leq 1
-modes.
Let us spell out what this operator looks like in components: The only non-vanishing Christoffel symbols are

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi\varphi}^{\theta}=-\cos\theta\sin\theta,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi\theta}^{\varphi}=\cot\theta=\Gamma_{\theta\varphi}^{\varphi}.

Then, working in in the orthonormal frame
e_{1}=\partial_{\theta}
,
e_{2}=\tfrac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}
, the Christoffel symbols (
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{e_{i}}e_{j}=\Gamma_{ij}^{k}e_{k}
) become

\Gamma_{21}^{2}=\cot\theta,\quad\Gamma_{22}^{1}=-\cot\theta,\quad\Gamma_{12}^{2}=0=\text{all others}.

(A.1)

We can thus compute (using that
\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}f=\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}f
)

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)_{11}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}f-\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}f-\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}g=-\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)_{22},

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)_{12}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}f-\frac{1}{2}(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}g-\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}g),

and, using (
A.1
),

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}f

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}^{2}f,

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}f

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\left(\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\varphi}f-\cot\theta\partial_{\varphi}f\right)=\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}f,

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}f

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\left(\partial_{\varphi}^{2}f+\cos\theta\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}f\right).

In summary, we have

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)_{11}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}^{2}f-\frac{1}{2}\mathring{\not{\Delta}}f-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}(\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\varphi}g-\cot\theta\partial_{\varphi}g)=-(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g))_{22,}

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g)_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\varphi}f}{\sin\theta}-\frac{\cot\theta}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}f-\frac{1}{2\sin^{2}\theta}(\partial_{\varphi}^{2}g+\cos\theta\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}g)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}g.

We can similarly compute that the components of the Laplacian
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}
acting on symmetric traceless two-tensors are given by

\displaystyle(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\alpha)_{11}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}(\alpha_{11})-4\cot^{2}\theta\alpha_{11}-4\frac{\cot\theta}{\sin\theta}\alpha_{12},

(A.2)

\displaystyle(\mathring{\not{\Delta}}\alpha)_{12}

\displaystyle=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}(\alpha_{12})-4\cot^{2}\theta\alpha_{12}+4\frac{\cot\theta}{\sin\theta}\alpha_{11},

where we used

\displaystyle(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\alpha)_{11}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}^{2}\alpha_{11},

\displaystyle(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}\alpha)_{11}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}\alpha_{11}-4\frac{\cot\theta}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}\alpha_{11}+\cot\theta\partial_{\theta}\alpha_{11}-4\cot^{2}\theta\alpha_{11},

\displaystyle(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{1}\alpha)_{12}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}^{2}\alpha_{12},

\displaystyle(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{2}\alpha)_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}\alpha_{12}+4\frac{\cot\theta}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}\alpha_{11}+\cot\theta\partial_{\theta}\alpha_{12}-4\cot^{2}\theta\alpha_{12}.

A.2
Spin-weighted functions, the operators
\eth
,
\eth^{\prime}
, and the spin-weighted spherical harmonics
{}_{s}Y_{\ell,m}

Rather than working in the simple geometric framework of 1-forms and stf two-tensor fields, large parts of the literature work within the closely related framework of spin
s
-weighted functions.
We here define the spaces of spin
s
-weighted functions following
[
Sbi22
]
. Introduce the following complex frame vector fields on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
:

\displaystyle m=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(e_{1}+ie_{2}),

\displaystyle\overline{m}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(e_{1}-ie_{2}).

(A.3)

We again stress that the notation
\overline{m}
does not refer to magnetic conjugation (cf. §
2.4
),
\overline{m}
is simply the complex conjugate of
m
.

Definition A.1
.

We define

•

the space of smooth spin 2-weighted functions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
as the image of
\Gamma^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
under the map sending
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
to
\alpha(m,m)=\alpha_{11}+i\alpha_{12}
.

•

the space of spin (-2)-weighted functions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
as the image of
\Gamma^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
under the map sending
\alpha
to
\alpha(\overline{m},\overline{m})=\alpha_{11}-i\alpha_{12}
.

•

the space of smooth spin 1-weighted functions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
as the image of
\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})
under the map sending
\beta\in\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})
to
\sqrt{2}\beta(m)=\beta_{1}+i\beta_{2}
,

•

the space of spin (-1)-weighted functions as the image of
\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})
under the map sending
\beta
to
\sqrt{2}\beta(\overline{m})
.

•

the space of smooth spin 0-weighted functions on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
as the image of
\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}
under the map
\mathfrak{c}
sending
(f,g)\mapsto\mathfrak{c}(f,g):=(f+ig)
, or, equivalently, under the map
\overline{\mathfrak{c}}
sending
(f,g)\mapsto\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(f,g):=(f-ig)
.

For intrinsic definitions and properties of these spaces, see also the original work
[
NP66
]
, or
[
DHR19b
]
or
[
Sbi22
]
.
In large parts of the physics literature on spin
s
-weighted functions, readers will likely also encounter the eth operators (eth referring to the Old English letter
\eth
):

We here provide an extrinsic, geometric definition of these operators in terms of the formalism of the present paper:

Definition A.2
.

Let
f\in\Gamma^{\infty}{(\mathbb{S}^{2})}
, let
\beta\in\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{(0,1)}\mathbb{S}^{2})
, and let
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\infty}(T_{\mathrm{stf}}^{(0,2)}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
. We define the operators
\eth_{s\to s+1}
(sending spin
s
-weighted functions to spin
s+1
-weighted functions) and
\eth^{\prime}_{s\to s-1}
(sending spin
s
-weighted functions to spin
s-1
-weighted functions, respectively, via:

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,0))(m)=:\eth_{0\to 1}f,

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,0))(\overline{m})=:\eth^{\prime}_{0\to-1}f,

(A.4)

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}(\beta))(m,m)=:\eth_{1\to 2}(\beta(m)),

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}(\beta))(\overline{m},\overline{m})=:\eth^{\prime}_{-1\to-2}(\beta(\overline{m})),

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\alpha)(m)=:-\eth^{\prime}_{2\to 1}(\alpha(m,m)),

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\alpha)(\overline{m})=:-\eth_{-2\to-1}(\alpha(\overline{m},\overline{m})),

and, finally,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}\beta)=\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\beta+i\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\beta=:-\sqrt{2}\eth^{\prime}_{1\to 0}(\beta(m)),

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}\beta})=\mathring{\not{\mathrm{div}}}\,\beta-i\mathring{\not{\mathrm{curl}}}\,\beta:=-\sqrt{2}\eth_{-1\to 0}(\beta(\overline{m})).

(A.5)

Remark A.1
.

Using that
{}^{\ast}e_{1}=-e_{2}
,
{}^{\ast}e_{2}=-e_{1}
and thus
{}^{\ast}m=i\cdot m
,
{}^{\ast}\overline{m}=-i\overline{m}
, (
A.4
) implies that
(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,g))(m)=i\eth_{0\to-1}g
,
(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(0,g))(\overline{m})=-i\eth^{\prime}_{0\to 1}g
, so

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g))(m)=\eth_{0\to-1}(\mathfrak{c}(f,g)),

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}(f,g))(\bar{m})=\eth^{\prime}_{0\to 1}(\overline{\mathfrak{c}}({f,g})).

(A.6)

Remark A.2
.

It is easy to check that, in
(\theta,\varphi)
coordinates, Def.
A.2
implies

\displaystyle\eth_{s\to s+1}=-\partial_{\theta}-\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}+s\cot\theta,

\displaystyle\eth^{\prime}_{s\to s-1}=-\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}-s\cot\theta.

(A.7)

This is how most readers will have encountered the operator before (with the
s→s+1
subscript suppressed).
For instance, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\eth_{1\to 2}\beta(m)

\displaystyle:=(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\beta)(m,m)=(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\beta)_{11}+i(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\beta)_{12}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\theta}\beta_{1}-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}\beta_{2}\right)-\frac{i}{2}\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}\beta_{1}-\cot\theta\beta_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\theta}(\beta_{1}+i\beta_{2})+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}(\beta_{1}+i\beta_{2})-\cot\theta(\beta_{1}+i\beta_{2})\right).

Remark A.3
.

Recall the angular operator
\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}
appearing on the RHS of (
3.44
). We can rewrite this operator in terms of spin-weighted functions as follows:

\displaystyle 2(\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{2}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\alpha)(\overline{m},\overline{m})=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\eth^{\prime}_{-1\to-2}((\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}^{\ast}_{1}\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\alpha)(\overline{m}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eth^{\prime}_{-1\to-2}\eth^{\prime}_{0\to-1}(\overline{\mathfrak{c}}(\overline{\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\alpha))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eth^{\prime}_{-1\to-2}\eth^{\prime}_{0\to-1}({\mathfrak{c}}({\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{1}}\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\alpha))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2}\eth^{\prime}_{-1\to-2}\eth^{\prime}_{0\to-1}\eth^{\prime}_{1\to 0}((\mathring{\not{\mathcal{D}}}_{2}\alpha)(m))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eth^{\prime}_{-1\to-2}\eth^{\prime}_{0\to-1}\eth^{\prime}_{1\to 0}\eth^{\prime}_{2\to 1}(\alpha(m,m)).

We now use the
\eth
-operators to define the spin
s
-weighted spherical harmonics:

Definition A.3
.

Defining
18
18

18

In most parts of the literature, one typically starts with
\mathfrak{c}(Y_{\ell,m})=Y_{\ell,m}+iY_{\ell,m}
as
{}_{0}Y_{\ell,m}
.

{}_{0}Y_{\ell,m}=Y_{\ell,m}
, the spin
s
-weighted spherical harmonics
{}_{s}Y_{\ell,m}
are inductively defined for any
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\ell\geq|s|
via:

\displaystyle{}_{s+1}Y_{\ell,m}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\ell-s)(\ell+s+1)}}\eth_{s\to s+1}{}_{s}Y_{\ell,m},

(A.8)

\displaystyle{}_{s-1}Y_{\ell,m}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\ell+s)(\ell-s+1)}}\eth^{\prime}_{s\to s-1}{}_{s}Y_{\ell,m}.

To give an example, we have

{}_{\pm 2}Y_{\ell,m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\ell-1)\ell(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}}\left(\mp\partial_{\theta}-\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}\pm\cot\theta\right)\left(\mp\partial_{\theta}-\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}\right)Y_{\ell,m}={}\overline{{}_{\mp 2}Y_{\ell,m}}.

Remark A.4
.

With these definitions at hand, we can now relate the spin
s
-weighted spherical harmonics to the tensorial spherical harmonics of Def.
2.3
:

\displaystyle{}_{1}Y_{\ell,m}=\sqrt{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},1}(m)

\displaystyle=-i{\sqrt{2}}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},1}(m)=-\overline{{}_{-1}Y_{\ell,m}},

(A.9)

\displaystyle{}_{2}Y_{\ell,m}=\sqrt{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{E},2}(m,m)

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{2}Y_{\ell,m}^{\mathrm{H},2}(m,m)=\overline{{}_{-2}Y_{\ell,m}}.

(A.10)

The spin
s
-weighted spherical harmonics are eigenfunctions of the spin
s
-weighted spherical Laplacian:

Definition A.4
.

For
|s|=2
, the spin
s
-weighted Laplacian is defined via

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{[+2]}(\alpha(m,m)):=((\mathring{\not{\Delta}}+2)\alpha)(m,m),

\displaystyle\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{[-2]}(\alpha(\overline{m},\overline{m})):=((\mathring{\not{\Delta}}-2)\alpha)(\overline{m},\overline{m}),

(A.11)

and analogously for
|s|=1,0
. In particular,
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{[0]}
is simply the Laplacian acting on scalar functions.

The eigenvalues of
\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{[s]}
are
-\ell(\ell+1)+s(s+1)
.
In coordinates
(\theta,\varphi)
, the spin
s
-weighted Laplacian for
s=2
reads (cf. to (
A.2
))

\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{[s]}=\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{[0]}-\frac{2si\cot\theta}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}-4\cot^{2}\theta+s.

(A.12)

Finally, we remark that it can also be written as

\mathring{\not{\Delta}}^{[s]}=\eth^{\prime}_{s+1\to s}\eth_{s\to s+1}=\eth_{s-1\to s}\eth^{\prime}_{s\to s-1}+2s.

(A.13)

A.3
A dictionary between the Christodoulou–Klainerman formalism and the Newman–Penrose formalism

In this part of the appendix, we provide an explicit translation of the Christodoulou–Klainerman framework and notation, employed in the present paper, to the very closely related Newman–Penrose formalism.

Since many people are familiar with only one of them, even though both formalisms are essentially the same, we hope that this will make it easier for people to read papers written in the respective other formalism.

The C–K formalism

In the Christodoulou–Klainerman formalism
[
CK93
]
, given a spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g)
, a local orthonormal null frame
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
is picked, with
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
spacelike and
e_{3}
,
e_{4}
null.
That is to say, letting upper-case Latin letters denote indices
\in\{1,2\}
,
g(e_{A},e_{B})=\delta_{AB}
,
g(e_{A},e_{3})=0=g(e_{A},e_{4})
, and
g(e_{3},e_{4})=-2
. We further introduce the notation that
\not{g}
and
\not{\varepsilon}
denote the metric and volume form on the orthogonal complement
\langle e_{3},\mathrm{e}_{4}\rangle^{\perp}
induced by the spacetime metric and spacetime volume form, respectively.

Then, the Ricci/connection coefficients are decomposed as follows:

\displaystyle\chi_{AB}=g(\nabla_{A}e_{4},e_{B}),

\displaystyle\underline{\chi}_{AB}=g(\nabla_{A}e_{3},e_{B}),

(A.14)

\displaystyle\zeta_{A}=\frac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{A}e_{4},e_{3}),

\displaystyle\underline{\zeta}_{A}=\frac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{A}e_{3},e_{4})=-\zeta_{A},

\displaystyle\omega=\frac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{4}e_{3},e_{4}),

\displaystyle\underline{\omega}=\frac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{3}e_{4},e_{3}),

\displaystyle\eta=-\frac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{3}e_{A},e_{4}),

\displaystyle\underline{\eta}=-\frac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{4}e_{A},e_{3}),

\displaystyle\xi_{A}=\frac{1}{2}g(\mathring{\not{\nabla}}_{4}e_{4},e_{A}),

\displaystyle\underline{\xi}_{A}=\frac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{3}e_{3},e_{A}),

and, finally
19
19

19

We are not aware of a standard convention for the notation for the Christoffel symbols on the sphere within the C–K framework, so we here introduce the notation
\not{\Gamma}
.
,

\not{\Gamma}_{A}=g(\nabla_{A}e_{1},e_{2}),\quad\not{\Gamma}_{3}=g(\nabla_{3}e_{1},e_{2}),\quad\not{\Gamma}_{4}=g(\nabla_{4}e_{1},e_{2}).

(A.15)

All other connection coefficients either vanish or can be derived from the ones above by an application of the Leibniz rule, using the orthonormality of the frame.
Notice that, in general, neither
\chi
nor
\underline{\chi}
are symmetric.

Similarly, the Weyl curvature tensor
C
(which equals the Riemann curvature tensor if
(\mathcal{M},g)
solves the Einstein vacuum equations) is decomposed as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha_{AB}=C(e_{A},e_{4},e_{B},e_{4}),

\displaystyle\underline{\alpha}_{AB}=C(e_{A},e_{3},e_{B},e_{3})

(A.16)

\displaystyle\beta_{A}=C(e_{A},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),

\displaystyle\underline{\beta}_{A}=C(e_{A},e_{3},e_{4},e_{3})

\displaystyle-\rho\not{g}_{AB}+\sigma\not{\varepsilon}_{AB}=C(e_{A},e_{3},e_{B},e_{4}).

Notice that
\alpha
and
\underline{\alpha}
are symmetric, and also tracefree by definition of the Weyl tensor.

The N–P formalism

On the other hand, in the Newman–Penrose formalism
[
NP62
]
, one works with a null tetrad
(l,n,m,\overline{m})
which is related to a frame
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
as in the C–K formalism via

e_{4}=l
,
e_{3}=n
,
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(e_{1}+ie_{2})=m
,
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(e_{1}-ie_{2})=\overline{m}
.
In order to closely resemble the standard works employing this formalism, we will now use
;
subscripts to denote covariant differentiation.
Notice that the C–K and the N–P formalisms use an overlapping set of symbols for different quantities, it should however always be clear from context which ones we are talking about.
The Ricci coefficients are then decomposed into the following complex scalars:

\displaystyle\rho=l_{\mu;\nu}m^{\mu}\overline{m}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\sigma=l_{\mu;\nu}m^{\mu}m^{\nu},

(A.17)

\displaystyle\mu=-n_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}m^{\nu},

\displaystyle\lambda=-n_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}\overline{m}^{\nu},

\displaystyle\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}(l_{\mu;\nu}n^{\mu}\overline{m}^{\nu}-m_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}\overline{m}^{\nu}),

\displaystyle\beta=\tfrac{1}{2}(l_{\mu;\nu}n^{\mu}m^{\nu}-m_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}m^{\nu}),

\displaystyle\epsilon=\tfrac{1}{2}(l_{\mu;\nu}n^{\mu}l^{\nu}-m_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}l^{\nu}),

\displaystyle\gamma=\tfrac{1}{2}(l_{\mu;\nu}n^{\mu}n^{\nu}-m_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}n^{\nu}),

\displaystyle\tau=l_{\mu;\nu}m^{\mu}n^{\nu},

\displaystyle\pi=-n_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}l^{\nu},

\displaystyle\kappa=l_{\mu;\nu}m^{\mu}l^{\nu},

\displaystyle\nu=-n_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}n^{\nu}.

Finally, Newman and Penrose decompose the Weyl curvature coefficients according to

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}=C(l,m,l,m),

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}=C(\overline{m},n,\overline{m},n),

(A.18)

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}=C(m,l,n,l),

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}=C(\overline{m},n,l,n),

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}=-C(m,n,\overline{m},l).

The dictionary

We explicitly relate the definitions of Newman and Penrose to the C–K quantities as follows:

\displaystyle\rho=\chi(\overline{m},m)=\tfrac{1}{2}(\not{g}\cdot\chi+i\not{\varepsilon}\cdot\chi),

\displaystyle\sigma=\chi(m,m)=\hat{\chi}(m,m),

(A.19)

\displaystyle\mu=-\underline{\chi}(m,\overline{m})=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\not{g}\cdot\underline{\chi}-i\not{\epsilon}\cdot\underline{\chi}),

\displaystyle\lambda=-\underline{\chi}(\overline{m},\overline{m})=-\hat{\underline{\chi}}(\overline{m},\overline{m}),

\displaystyle\overline{\alpha}+\beta=2\zeta(m)=-2\underline{\zeta}(m),

\displaystyle-\overline{\alpha}+\beta=i\cdot\not{\Gamma}(m)=\not{\Gamma}({}^{\ast}m),

\displaystyle\epsilon+\overline{\epsilon}=-2\omega,

\displaystyle\gamma+\overline{\gamma}=2\underline{\omega},

\displaystyle\epsilon-\overline{\epsilon}=i\not{\Gamma}_{4},

\displaystyle\gamma-\overline{\gamma}=i\not{\Gamma}_{3},

\displaystyle\tau=2\eta(m),

\displaystyle\pi=-2\underline{\eta}(\overline{m}),

\displaystyle\kappa=2\xi(m),

\displaystyle\nu=-2\underline{\xi}(\overline{m}).

Let us give one detailed example for the required computations:

-\overline{\alpha}+\beta=\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{m}_{\mu;\nu}m^{\mu}m^{\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}m_{\mu;\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}m^{\nu}=\tfrac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{m}\overline{m},m)-\tfrac{1}{2}g(\nabla_{m}m,\overline{m})=-g(\nabla_{m}m,\overline{m})\\
=-\tfrac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}g(\nabla_{e_{1}+ie_{2}}e_{1}+ie_{2},e_{1}-ie_{2})=-\tfrac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}(-ig(\nabla_{1}e_{1},e_{2})+ig(\nabla_{1}e_{2},e_{1})+g(\nabla_{2}e_{1},e_{2})-g(\nabla_{2}e_{2},e_{1}))\\
=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(g(\nabla_{2}e_{1},e_{2})-ig(\nabla_{1}e_{1},e_{2}))=\tfrac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(g(\nabla_{1}e_{1},e_{2})+ig(\nabla_{2}e_{1},e_{2}))=i\not{\Gamma}_{A}m^{A}=\not{\Gamma}({}^{\ast}m)

Finally, we evidently have

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}=\alpha(m,m),

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}=\underline{\alpha}(\overline{m},\overline{m}),

(A.20)

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}=\beta(m),

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}=\beta(\overline{m}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}=\rho+i\sigma.

Appendix B
Useful integral identities

Recall the notation
r_{0}(u)=r(u,v=v_{1})
. Note that
r_{0}/r\leq 1
. Recall further the notation
D=1-2M/r=\partial_{v}r=-\partial_{u}r
, and
D_{0}=1-2M/r_{0}
.
Finally, we write, for any
q\in\mathbb{R}
such that
-q\notin\mathbb{N}_{>0}
,
q!
to mean
q!:=\Gamma(q+1)
.

B.1
Integrals in the
u
-direction

The following lemmata are used for integrating the approximate conservation law (
9.15
) or similar equations from
\mathcal{I}^{-}
:

Lemma B.1
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{R}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, with
-1<q<N-1
. Then

\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r(u^{\prime},1)^{q}}{r(u^{\prime},v)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=\frac{q!(N-q-2)!}{(N-1)!}\frac{1}{r(u,v)^{N-q-1}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r^{N-q}}+\frac{r_{0}^{q+1}}{r^{N}}+\frac{M}{r^{N-q}}\right).

(B.1)

In the special case where
q\in\mathbb{N}
, we have more precisely that

\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{r^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=\frac{q!(N-q-2)!}{(N-1)!}\frac{1}{r^{N-q-1}}\cdot\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{q}\frac{r_{0}^{i}}{r^{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{i}(N-q-2+j)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{M}{r^{N-q}}\right).

(B.2)

Finally, we have

\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r_{0}^{q}\log r_{0}}{r^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=\frac{q!(N-q-2)!}{(N-1)!}\frac{\log r}{r^{N-q-1}}+\frac{d_{N,q}}{r^{N-q-1}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r_{0}\log r_{0}}{r^{N-q}}+\frac{r_{0}^{q+1}\log r_{0}}{r^{N}}+\frac{M\log r}{r^{N-q}}\right),

(B.3)

where the constant
d_{N,q}
is given by
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{q}\log x}{(1+x)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\frac{q!(N-q-2)!}{(N-1)!}(H_{q}-H_{N-q-2})
.

Proof.

The second statement, (
B.2
), follows from

\displaystyle\int\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{r^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u}\left(\frac{1}{N-1}\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{r^{N-1}}\right)+\frac{qD_{0}}{N-1}\frac{r_{0}^{q-1}}{r^{N-1}}-\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{r^{N}}(1-D)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N-1}\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{r^{N-1}}+\frac{q}{N-1}\int\frac{r_{0}^{q-1}}{r^{N-1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u+M\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\int\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{r^{N+1}}+\frac{r_{0}^{q-2}}{r^{N-1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\right)

and complete induction.

Moving to the first statement, (
B.1
), we first prove it in the case
M=0
where
r-r_{0}
is independent of
u
:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{r^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{((r-r_{0})+r_{0})^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=\frac{1}{(r-r_{0})^{N-q-1}}\int_{r_{0}/(r-r_{0})}^{\infty}\frac{x^{q}}{(1+x)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(r-r_{0})^{N-q}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{q}}{(1+x)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\frac{1}{(r-r_{0})^{N-q-1}}\int_{0}^{r_{0}/(r-r_{0})}\frac{x^{q}}{(1+x)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

The first integral on the RHS can be looked up or be computed by
mathematica
and evaluates to
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{q}}{(1+x)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=q!(N-q-2)!/(N-1)!
. On the other hand, the second integral can be bounded as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r_{0}/(r-r_{0})}\frac{x^{q}}{(1+x)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\frac{(r_{0}/(r-r_{0}))^{q+1}}{q+1}+\mathcal{O}\left((r_{0}/(r-r_{0}))^{q+2}\right).

This proves the claim if
M=0
.

In order to prove the claim for
M>0
, we copy the above proof and use that
r-r_{0}=v+M\mathcal{O}(\log r)
. This immediately gives the claim, but with a logarithmic error bound of order
M\mathcal{O}(r^{q-N}\log r)
.
We remove this logarithmic error term by slightly deforming the path of integration: Instead of integrating along constant
v
, we integrate along constant
r-r_{0}
:
We work in coordinates
z=r-r_{0}
,
y=r_{0}
. Then
\partial_{u}=-D_{0}\partial_{y}+(D_{0}-D)\partial_{z}
,
\partial_{v}=D\partial_{z}
. Clearly, the integral
\int_{-\infty}^{u}r_{0}^{p}/r^{N}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}
then satisfies

\displaystyle-D_{0}\partial_{y}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{u}r_{0}^{q}/r^{N}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right)=r_{0}^{q}/r^{N}+(D-D_{0})\partial_{z}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{u}r_{0}^{q}/r^{N}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}\right).

We can now divide by
D_{0}
and integrate in
y
(along constant
z
). By using the estimate with the logarithmic error term for the second term on the RHS, we can show that that term is subleading:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r_{0}^{q}}{r^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=\int_{r_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{y^{q}}{(z+y)^{N}}+M\cdot\mathcal{O}\left((z+y)^{-N}y^{q-1}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

The first integral on the RHS is now the same one we had for
M=0
:

\displaystyle\int_{r_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{y^{q}}{(z+y)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\frac{q!(N-q-2)!}{(N-1)!(r-r_{0})^{N-q-1}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{r_{0}^{q+1}}{(r-r_{0})^{N}}\right).

The proof concludes by expanding
1/(r-r_{0})^{N-q-1}=1/(r^{N-q-1}\cdot(1-r_{0}/r)^{N-q-1})
.

Finally, we prove (
B.3
). We only consider the case
M=0
:
Then

\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r_{0}^{q}\log r_{0}}{r^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=\int_{r_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{y^{q}\log y}{(z+y)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\\
=\frac{1}{(r-r_{0})^{N-q-1}}\int_{r_{0}/(r-r_{0})}^{\infty}\frac{x^{q}}{(1+x)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\frac{\log(r-r_{0})}{(r-r_{0})^{N-q-1}}\int_{r_{0}/(r-r_{0})}^{\infty}\frac{x^{q}}{(1+x)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

The proof concludes by again writing both integrals as
\int_{r_{0}/(r-r_{0})}^{\infty}=\int_{0}^{\infty}-\int_{0}^{r_{0}/(r-r_{0})}
, and by using that
\log(r-r_{0})=\log r+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}/r)
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
. Then

\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{r_{0}^{-1}}{r^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u=r^{-N}\log(r/r_{0})+\mathcal{O}(r^{-N}).

(B.4)

Proof.

We only give the proof for
M=0
. The extension to
M>0
is immediate using
r-r_{0}=v+\mathcal{O}(\log r).

The integral is computed using partial fractions. Denote again
r_{0}=y
,
r-r_{0}=z
. Then

\frac{1}{r_{0}r^{N}}=\frac{1}{y(z+y)^{N}}=\frac{1}{z^{N}}\left(\frac{1}{y}+\frac{1}{y+z}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{z^{N+1-i}}\frac{1}{(z+y)^{i+1}}.

We now compute

\displaystyle z^{-N}\int_{-\infty}^{u}\left(\frac{1}{y}+\frac{1}{y+z}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=z^{-N}\int_{r_{0}}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{y}+\frac{1}{y+z}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{(r-r_{0})^{N}}\log r/r_{0}.

The statement then follows from
(r-r_{0})^{-N}\log(r/r_{0})=r^{-N}\log(r/r_{0})(1+r_{0}/r)^{N}
and the fact that
-x\log x
is bounded by
\mathrm{e}^{-1}
on
x\in[0,1]
.
∎

The previous lemmata can be extended to the entire range of
q\in\mathbb{R}
using integration by parts:

Lemma B.3
.

Suppose that
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\in\mathbb{R}
, with
-q<-1
. Then

\int\frac{x^{-q}}{(x+y)^{N}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=\frac{1}{(-q+1)}\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil q-2\rceil}\frac{x^{-q+1+i}}{(x+y)^{N+i}}\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{(N+j)}{(-q+j+2)}\\
+\prod_{j=0}^{\lceil q-2\rceil}\frac{(N+j)}{(-q+j+1)}\int\frac{x^{-q+\lceil q-1\rceil}}{(x+y)^{N+\lceil q-1\rceil}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

(B.5)

Proof.

Induction.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

We have

\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{\log r-\log r_{0}}{r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=1+\mathcal{O}(r_{0}/r).

(B.6)

Proof.

We again first prove this for
M=0
. Using the dilogarithmic identity
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{k}}{k!}=-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\log(1-x^{\prime})}{x^{\prime}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}(=\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x))
for
|x|\leq 1
(page 1004 in
[
AS74
]
), we have that

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{u}\frac{\log(v-u^{\prime})-\log|u^{\prime}|}{(v-u^{\prime})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}=-\int_{0}^{v/(v-u)}\frac{\log(1-x^{\prime})}{x^{\prime}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}=\frac{v}{v-u}+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}(v/(v-u))^{k-1}/k^{2},

so the result follows. In order to elevate the result to
M\neq 0
, we follow the same procedure as in the proof of Lemma
B.1
.
∎

B.2
Integrals in the
v
-direction

The next lemmata are relevant for integrating in
v
from
{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Lemma B.5
.

Define
x=1/r
and
x_{0}=1/r_{0}
.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
q\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\underbrace{\int_{v_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}^{v}\frac{D}{r^{2}}(u,v_{(1)})\cdots\int_{v_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}^{v_{(n-1)}}\frac{D}{r^{2}}(u,v_{(n)})}_{\text{$N$ integrals}}\int_{v_{{\underline{\mathcal{C}}}}}^{v_{(n)}}\frac{D}{r^{2}}r^{q}(u,v_{(n+1)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v_{(n+1)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v_{(n)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v_{(1)}\\
=(-1)^{N}\int_{x_{0}(u)}^{x}\cdots\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n-1)}}\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n)}}x_{(n+1)}^{-q}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(n+1)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(1)}.

(B.7)

Proof.

Direct substitution of the variable
x=1/r
.
∎

Lemma B.6
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

(-1)^{N}\underbrace{\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\cdots\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n-1)}}}_{\text{$N$ times}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(n)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(1)}=\frac{(x_{0}-x)^{N}}{N!}.

(B.8)

Proof.

Induction.
∎

Lemma B.7
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
q\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
N\leq q-1
, or if
q\notin\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, then

(-1)^{N}\underbrace{\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\cdots\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n-1)}}}_{\text{$N$ times}}x_{(n)}^{-q}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(n)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(1)}\\
=\frac{(q-N-1)!}{(q-1)!}x^{N-q}-x_{0}^{N-q}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\frac{(q+i-N-1)!}{(q-1)!i!}\left(1-\frac{x}{x_{0}}\right)^{i}.

(B.9)

Otherwise, if
N>q-1
and
q\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, then

(-1)^{N}\underbrace{\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\cdots\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n-1)}}}_{\text{$N$ times}}x_{(n)}^{-q}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(n)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(1)}\\
=\frac{(-1)^{N+1-q}}{(q-1)!(N-q)!}x^{N-q}\log(x/x_{0})-x_{0}^{N-q}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}d_{N,q,i}\left(1-\frac{x}{x_{0}}\right)^{i},

(B.10)

with some coefficients
d_{N,q,i}\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

The first statement (
B.9
) is easy to prove (via induction).

In order to show (
B.10
), one first shows that

\underbrace{\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\cdots\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n-1)}}}_{\text{$N$ times}}x_{(n)}^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(n)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(1)}=\frac{x^{N-1}\log(x/x_{0})}{(N-1)!}-x_{0}^{N-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\frac{(\frac{x}{x_{0}}-1)^{i}}{i!(N-1-i)!}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{1}{(N-1-j)}.

Combining this with the first statement of the lemma, and appropriately splitting up the integrals, we then obtain

\displaystyle(-1)^{N}\underbrace{\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\cdots\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n-1)}}}_{\text{$N$ times}}x_{(n)}^{-q}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(n)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(1)}=\frac{(-1)^{N+1-q}}{(q-1)!(N-q)!}x^{N-q}\log(x/x_{0})

\displaystyle-\frac{(-1)^{N+1-q}}{(q-1)!(N-q)!}x_{0}^{N-q}\sum_{i=1}^{N-q}\binom{N-q}{i}\left(\tfrac{x}{x_{0}}-1\right)^{i}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{1}{N-q-j}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(q-1)!}\sum_{i=0}^{q-2}\frac{i!}{(N+1-q+i)!}x_{0}^{-1-i}(x_{0}-x)^{N+1-q+i}.

We note that it’s much easier to prove only the schematic form of (
B.10
), without keeping track of the precise expressions of the coefficients.
∎

We finally record a version of the above Lemma when logarithms are present:

Lemma B.8
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
q\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
N\leq q-1
, or if
q\notin\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, then

(-1)^{N}\underbrace{\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\cdots\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n-1)}}}_{\text{$N$ times}}x_{(n)}^{-q}\log(x_{(n)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(n)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(1)}\\
=\frac{(q-N-1)!}{(q-1)!}x^{N-q}\log x-x_{0}^{N-q}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\frac{(q+i-N-1)!}{(q-1)!i!}\left(1-\frac{x}{x_{0}}\right)^{i}\log x_{0}+d^{\prime}_{N,q,i}\left(\frac{x}{x_{0}}\right)^{i}.

(B.11)

Otherwise, if
N>q-1
and
q\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, then

(-1)^{N}\underbrace{\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\cdots\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{(n-1)}}}_{\text{$N$ times}}x_{(n)}^{-q}\log x_{(n)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(n)}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{(1)}\\
=\frac{(-1)^{N+1-q}}{{2}(q-1)!(N-q)!}x^{N-q}\left(\log^{2}x+\log x\left(1-\sum_{i=1}^{N-q}\frac{1}{i}\right)\right)\\
-x_{0}^{N-q}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}(d^{\prime\prime}_{N,q,i}+d^{\prime\prime\prime}_{N,q,i}\log x_{0}+d_{N,q,i}\log^{2}x_{0})\left(1-\frac{x}{x_{0}}\right)^{i},

(B.12)

with some coefficients
d_{N,q,i}\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
B.7
, using also that
\int x^{-1}\log x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x={\frac{1}{2}}\log^{2}x
and

\int x^{n}\log^{2}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}\left(\log^{2}x-\frac{2}{n+1}\log x+\frac{2}{(n+1)^{2}}\right),\qquad\forall n\neq-1.

∎
